How to read this deck

Every card on this page answers something the midterm study guide actually asks. Nothing else was kept.

One card per study guide line

Why it mattersThe RCP 203 study guide is a numbered list of things to explain. Each card here answers one of those lines. Material the guide does not ask for — however useful — was removed rather than shortened.

The panels are colour-coded

MechanismWhy the fact behaves as it does.
Exam anchorThe pearl, the trap, the number to memorise.
GapHonestly absent from the decks and the book — go look it up.
CautionA number that conflicts between sources, or a claim that is easy to misread.

Sources are unchanged

Why it mattersThe grey line at the foot of each card still names the chapter, or the deck and slide, the answer came from. No wording was rewritten in the trim — cards were kept whole or dropped whole.

01

Infection Control, Sterilization, VAP & Tuberculosis

5 items · Transmission-based precautions · cleaning non-disposable equipment · sterilization · VAP and how to reduce it · tuberculosis

1Transmission-Based Precautions2Cleaning Non-Disposable Equipment and the Spaulding Classification3Sterilization and Disinfection Methods4Ventilator-Associated Pneumonia (VAP)5Tuberculosis

1Transmission-Based Precautions

Standard Precautions are the floor: you assume every patient might be infectious. Heuer's CDC table covers hand hygiene (after touching blood/body fluids/contaminated items, immediately after removing gloves, between patient contacts), gloves for mucous membranes and nonintact skin, gown when clothing may contact body fluids, mask + eye protection for anything likely to splash or spray — especially suctioning and intubation — no recapping of needles, sharps into a puncture-resistant container, a mouthpiece or bag-valve device for resuscitation, and respiratory hygiene/cough etiquette (cover cough, tissues into a no-touch receptacle, hand hygiene, and in waiting areas either a surgical mask or spatial separation of more than 3 feet).

Heuer's headline: because most hospital-acquired infections come from direct contact between patients and healthcare workers, good hand hygiene is the single best way to prevent transmission.

Transmission-Based Precautions are always layered on top of Standard Precautions, never instead of them. Heuer defines the three routes by droplet size: droplet transmission = large droplets larger than 5 μm, which fall out of suspension fast and therefore travel only short distances — generally 3 feet or less, which is the distance rule to memorize. Airborne transmission = small droplet nuclei 5 μm or smaller, which are stable and can travel long distances, even between rooms. Contact transmission is direct or indirect contact with the patient or the patient's environment, including contaminated equipment.

PrecautionPPE requiredRoom requirementsExample diseases
Standard (all patients, always)Hand hygiene; gloves for blood/body fluids, mucous membranes, nonintact skin; gown if soiling anticipated; mask + goggles/face shield for splash-generating care (suctioning, intubation)Routine room; single-patient room if the patient is high-risk, likely to contaminate the environment, or can't maintain hygieneApplied to everyone — the decks add that pneumonia alone gets standard precautions "unless the cause indicates" a higher level
ContactClean gloves and gown on entering the room; change gloves after contact with infectious material; remove gloves and gown before leaving the room, then wash hands (or alcohol rub if hands are not visibly soiled)Private room; separate clean and dirty supply areasGI infections (diarrhea of unknown origin, C. difficile, norovirus), wound and skin infections, multidrug-resistant organisms (MRSA), SARS. Deck adds RSV = contact + droplet together
DropletSurgical mask when within 3 feet of the patient; eye/face protection for aerosol-generating procedures or contact with secretions; patient wears a mask for transportPrivate room, but no special air handling or ventilation needed — the door may stay open; separate clean and dirty supply areasBacterial meningitis, pertussis, influenza, mumps, rubella, diphtheria, group A strep, SARS, epiglottitis (H. influenzae)
AirborneFit-tested NIOSH-certified N95 respirator on entering the room (plus a user-seal check every single time); eye/face protection for aerosol-generating procedures; for the highest level of protection or a poor N95 fit, a PAPR; the patient wears a surgical mask if transportedPrivate negative-pressure Airborne Infection Isolation Room (AIIR) with the door closed — air flows into the room so contaminated air can't escape; separate clean/dirty supply areas; if an AIIR is unavailable, private room + closed door + mask the patientTuberculosis, measles (rubeola), chickenpox (varicella-zoster), Aspergillus, smallpox, SARS

Air changes per hour

Heuer specifies the negative-pressure AIIR and the closed door but gives no air-changes-per-hour number anywhere in the book. The only ACH figure available comes from a deck: aerosolized pentamidine must be given in a negative-pressure room with six (6) air changes per hour, or in an isolation booth/hood with an exhaust fan directing air through a high-efficiency filter, with gloves, mask, and eyewear as barrier protection. Use the deck's 6 ACH if asked; know that it is the pentamidine-specific figure, not a Heuer number.

PPE order (Heuer Table 7-3) — a likely recall question. Applying: 1. hair and foot coverings → 2. gown → 3. mask → 4. goggles/face shield → 5. gloves. Removing: 1. hair and foot coverings → 2. goggles/face shield → 3. mask → 4. gloves (peel down from the wrist, turning inside out) → 5. gown (inside out).

Atypical viral infections (SARS, avian influenza, novel viruses) get a combined standard + contact + airborne protocol in an AIIR with the door closed, continued for 14 days after symptom onset or until testing clears the patient. For aerosol-generating procedures (aerosol drug delivery, sputum induction, bronchoscopy, suctioning, intubation, NIV, HFOV): use PPE covering torso/arms/hands plus eyes/nose/mouth, consider a surgical hood with an N95 or a PAPR, keep doors closed and minimize room entry/exit, consider sedation for intubation and bronchoscopy to minimize coughing, and put HEPA filtration on the expiratory limb of the ventilator.

SourceHeuer Ch 7 — Standard Precautions & Transmission-Based Precautions, Tables 7-2/7-3/7-4 and Protocol for Atypical Viral Infections (PDF p379-388, T4 tips p395-396) · Heuer Ch 15 — outbreak management (PDF p708-709) · Infectious Pulmonary Disease deck, slides 55, 65-66, 72 · Aerosolized Anti-infective Agents (Chptr 13) deck, slides 15 and 19 · Intro to Resp Care Pharm (Chptr 1) deck, slide 5

2Cleaning Non-Disposable Equipment and the Spaulding Classification

Yes — Heuer covers the Spaulding classification, but never uses the name "Spaulding." Table 7-5 is that exact three-tier scheme: critical / semicritical / noncritical, sorted by what the device touches.

  • Critical items — introduced into the bloodstream or other normally sterile parts of the body (surgical instruments, intravascular catheters, implants, heart-lung bypass and dialysis components, bronchoscope forceps and brushes). Processing: sterilization. Heat-tolerant → steam under pressure (autoclave). Heat-sensitive → gas or ionized vapor (ethylene oxide, hydrogen peroxide) or immersion in a liquid chemical sterilant (glutaraldehyde, ortho-phthalaldehyde).
  • Semicritical items — contact intact mucous membranes: endoscopes/bronchoscopes, oral/nasal/tracheal airways, ventilator circuits and humidifiers, PFT mouthpieces and tubing, nondisposable nebulizers, laryngoscope blades, nondisposable resuscitation bags, pressure/gas/temperature probes. Processing: sterilization or high-level disinfection, by liquid immersion (glutaraldehyde, OPA) or pasteurization. Heuer's key line: most respiratory care equipment is semicritical, so high-level disinfection is the RT's everyday standard.
  • Noncritical items — touch only intact skin or don't touch the patient at all: stethoscopes, the outside of face masks, BP cuffs, the ventilator itself. Processing: detergent washing or low- to intermediate-level disinfection. Stethoscopes between patients: wipe the diaphragm with 70% alcohol prep pads, ideally kept visibly wet for at least 10 seconds.
Why cleaning must come firstHeuer defines cleaning as "the physical removal of foreign material (e.g., dirt or organic material), usually with water, detergents, and mechanical action (washing)" — it removes rather than kills microorganisms. Disinfection is the separate step that inactivates them. Blood, mucus and organic debris shield organisms from the chemical or heat step, so a disinfectant applied over soil never reaches what's underneath: an item that has not been cleaned cannot be reliably disinfected or sterilized.

The processing sequence — Heuer's bronchoscope protocol (Box 6-1) is the model.

Cleaning: (1) at the bedside, flush water/saline/approved solution through the working channel for 20 seconds; (2) transport immediately in a sealed contaminated-equipment bag so organic material doesn't dry on; (3) mechanically clean (e.g., ultrasonic) all reusable accessories and send them for autoclaving; (4) remove disposables, cap and seal light/electrical connectors, place the scope in a cleaning basin; (5) perform the manufacturer's leak test; (6) add enzymatic cleaner and soak for 5 minutes; (7) wipe external surfaces with wet gauze and flush the suction channel; (8) brush all ports until no visible debris comes out; (9) flush again; (10) drain the basin; (11) rinse all internal and external surfaces.

High-level disinfection: place in an automatic endoscope reprocessor or manual basin, use only FDA-approved compatible disinfectant, confirm the disinfectant concentration each time, and fully immerse all surfaces for the proper time — 20 minutes or longer for glutaraldehyde — filling the working channel by syringe if disinfecting manually.

Post-processing: rinse the scope and working channel with sterile or filtered tap water; dry the working channel with 70% alcohol purged with compressed air; remove watertight caps and hang the scope vertically in a storage cabinet with no valve attached; document patient ID, date, bronchoscopist, model, serial number, and reprocessing date.

Rinsing rule (high-yield)after immersion in a liquid disinfectant, rinse semicritical equipment with sterile water. If that isn't feasible, rinse with filtered or tap water, then rinse with isopropyl alcohol and dry with forced air or in a drying cabinet.

Specific equipment rules from Heuer Ch 7: don't routinely sterilize or disinfect the internal machinery of ventilators; don't routinely change in-use circuits, humidifiers, HMEs or closed-suction systems (change only when visibly soiled or malfunctioning); fill humidifiers with sterile water only; place inspiratory bacterial filters proximal to the humidifier; high-level disinfect nondisposable heating probes. Large-volume nebulizers are a major infection source — use prefilled sterile disposables when possible, and replace nondisposable units with a sterile or high-level-disinfected unit every 24 hours.

Small-volume nebulizers between treatments on the same patient: clean, rinse with sterile water, air dry. Open suctioning: a sterile, single-use catheter every time. PFT equipment: change the mouthpiece and HEPA filter between patients rather than disinfecting the internal workings.

The decks agree in principle — "all equipment and supplies must be cleaned, disinfected, or sterilized" before use on another patient, bronchoscopes get high-level disinfection with scheduled maintenance, and TB/hepatitis B exposure escalates a scope to sterilization because M. tuberculosis has a waxy capsule resistant to many disinfectants — but the decks give no processing steps, no agents, and no times. All of that is Heuer.

SourceHeuer Ch 7 — Adhering to Disinfection Policies, Table 7-5 Infection Risk Categories (PDF p388-391, T4 tips p395) · Heuer Ch 6 — Box 6-1 Processing of Bronchoscopes After Use (PDF p366-367) · RCP 105 Wk 5 (Chang Chp 14-15) deck, slides 13 and 15 · Infectious Pulmonary Disease deck, slide 48

3Sterilization and Disinfection Methods

Heuer's definitions, in order of strength: sterilization destroys all forms of microbial life — bacteria, viruses, spores, fungi. High-level disinfection destroys vegetative bacteria, mycobacteria, fungi and viruses but not necessarily bacterial spores (some high-level agents can sterilize given enough contact time). Intermediate-level kills vegetative bacteria, mycobacteria, and most viruses and fungi, but not resistant spores. Low-level kills most vegetative bacteria, some fungi, and some viruses (hepatitis B and C, HIV) but not mycobacteria and not spores — it's for environmental surfaces.

An antiseptic kills organisms on living skin or mucous membranes; a germicide is any chemical that kills microorganisms; bacteriostatic agents only inhibit growth.

MethodHow it worksTemperature / time / concentrationUsed for
Steam autoclaveSaturated steam under pressure — moist heat sterilizationHeuer gives no temperature, pressure, or cycle timeCritical, heat-tolerant items — surgical instruments, reusable bronchoscope forceps and brushes
Ethylene oxide (EtO)Low-temperature gas sterilizationHeuer gives no concentration, cycle time, or aeration timeCritical, heat-sensitive items that can't survive an autoclave
Hydrogen peroxide — ionized vaporLow-temperature vapor/gas sterilizationNo numbers given for the gas processCritical, heat-sensitive items (listed alongside EtO)
Hydrogen peroxide — liquidChemical oxidizer, cold soak3% for 30 minutesHome/CF disinfection of cleaned semicritical items and nebulizer parts
Glutaraldehyde (Cidex)Liquid chemical — high-level disinfectant or sterilant depending on contact timeHigh-level disinfection: 20 minutes at room temperature. True sterilization: a full 10 hours. Bronchoscope immersion: 20 minutes or longerHeat-sensitive critical and semicritical items — endoscopes/bronchoscopes, airways, resuscitation bags. Ortho-phthalaldehyde (OPA) is used the same way
PasteurizationImmersion in hot water — moist heat, high-level disinfection (not sterilization)Immersion at > 158°F (70°C) for 30 minutesSemicritical, heat-sensitive respiratory equipment — ventilator circuits, humidifiers, nondisposable nebulizers
Isopropyl alcoholAntiseptic / intermediate-level disinfectant; also a drying agent after rinsing70% for 5 minutes (cleaned semicritical items at home). Stethoscopes: 70% wipes kept visibly wet ≥ 10 seconds. Bronchoscope channel dried with 70% alcohol. Line insertion prep: chlorhexidine 2% in 70% isopropyl alcoholSkin and surface antisepsis, stethoscope/headset wipe-down, post-disinfection rinse and channel drying, home equipment disinfection
ChlorhexidineAntiseptic for skin and mucous membranes — not a sterilant2% (in 70% isopropyl alcohol), scrubbed into the insertion site for 30 secondsCDC central line bundle skin prep; swabbing access ports; daily oral care to prevent VAP
Sodium hypochlorite (bleach)Low-/intermediate-level surface germicideRoutine surfaces during a C. difficile outbreak: dilute 5–6% solution (1:10 dilution of household bleach). Blood/body-fluid spills: 1:100 for small spills (< 10 mL), 1:10 for large spills. Home semicritical items: 1:50 for 5 minutesEnvironmental surface disinfection and spill decontamination. Note: not to be used on CF nebulizers
Detergent and waterPhysical removal, no killing claim—Noncritical items and surfaces in non-patient-care areas

Heat methods for home equipment (once cleaned): boil for 5 minutes, microwave submerged in water for 5 minutes, or a dishwasher at ≥ 70°C (158°F) for 30 minutes. Household products other than bleach — ammonia, vinegar, Borax, liquid detergents — should not be used, because they are ineffective against Staphylococcus aureus.

Verifying that sterilization worked (Heuer Table 7-6, used together): mechanical monitoring checks cycle time, temperature and pressure — correct readings don't guarantee sterility, but incorrect readings flag a problem; chemical indicators change color when the correct temperature or gas concentration is reached, giving an immediate warning (if the indicator on a package hasn't changed color, get a replacement item); biological indicators test whether the cycle killed bacterial spores on impregnated paper strips and are the best method for verifying sterilization — include them in every cycle containing critical items, and otherwise at least once a week per sterilizer. If mechanical and chemical monitoring look correct but the culture grows, recall critical items only.

Where the decks fall short here: the decks name steam autoclave and ethylene oxide as the two sterilization methods and use chlorhexidine (arterial-line site scrub, oral VAP care) and isopropyl alcohol (skin prep before transcutaneous monitoring) only as antiseptics — and give no temperature, time, or concentration for anything. Every number in the table above comes from Heuer.

SourceHeuer Ch 7 — Key Terms Table 7-1, High-Level Disinfection (Equipment Processing), Table 7-5, Surface Disinfection, Table 7-6 Methods to Monitor Sterilization (PDF p379-380, p388-393, T4 tips p395) · Heuer Ch 6 — Box 6-1 (PDF p367) · Heuer Ch 17 — home-care equipment disinfection and CF nebulizer cleaning Box 20-1 (PDF p775, tips p809) · RCP 105 Wk 5 (Chang Chp 14-15) deck, slide 15 · RCP 203 Lssn 2b (Chp 3) deck, slides 12 and 24

4Ventilator-Associated Pneumonia (VAP)

Definition — note the source disagreement

Heuer defines VAP as pneumonia occurring in patients ventilated for more than 48 hours (Ch 9) / developing after more than 48 hours of mechanical ventilation (Ch 20), and adds a distinction worth knowing: a patient with severe nosocomial pneumonia who needs ventilation after the infection starts does not meet the VAP definition. The Infectious Pulmonary Disease deck instead defines VAP as pneumonia developing more than 48 to 72 hours after intubation.

The RCP 105 Wk 5 deck slide text says "within 48 hours," but its own notes correct this to "48 hours or more after endotracheal intubation." Safest answer on an exam: 48 hours or more after intubation, in a patient who was not admitted with pneumonia. The concept behind all three: the ETT is the risk factor, not the ventilator — it bypasses the filtering defenses of the nose and mouth, and secretions pooling above the cuff leak around it into the lungs (microaspiration, the primary mechanism). The single greatest risk factor is prolonged mechanical ventilation.

Epidemiology (decks; Heuer gives no percentages)

VAP affects 10 to 25% of ventilated patients and accounts for about 25% of all ICU infections, with mortality 33 to 50%. Incidence is 3% over the first 5 days, then 2% per day for the next 5 days, then 1% per day thereafter. Trauma, neurosurgical and burn patients have higher rates than respiratory/medical ICU patients.

How it's diagnosed

No single finding makes the diagnosis. Suspect VAP when a ventilated patient develops new or progressive infiltrates on chest radiograph or CT plus clinical signs of infection: fever, leukocytosis or leukopenia, and purulent tracheobronchial secretions. Heuer adds the bedside picture: dyspnea and distress, tachypnea, hemoptysis, rhonchi and crackles, reduced breath sounds, bronchospasm, worsening hypoxemia on ABG, plus mechanical clues — falling tidal volume, rising inspiratory and plateau pressures, decreasing compliance.

The deck gives the test statistics: infiltrates plus two of the three other criteria carry a sensitivity of 69% and a specificity of 75%, and a modified Clinical Pulmonary Infection Score (CPIS) greater than 6 raises suspicion but does not confirm it. Confirmation requires identifying a pathogen from a lower respiratory tract sample, obtained before starting or changing antibiotics: bronchoscopic BAL or protected specimen brush (PSB), non-bronchoscopic mini-BAL, or — if invasive sampling isn't available — an endotracheal aspirate drawn into a sterile specimen trap.

Organisms shift with time on the vent: first 48 hours = upper-airway flora (H. influenzae, S. pneumoniae); 3–7 days = gram-negative bacilli; more than 7 days = resistant organisms including MRSA. Start empiric broad-spectrum antibiotics, then de-escalate once cultures return.

The VAP prevention bundle (Heuer / CDC):

  • Elevate the head of the bed 30–45 degrees, unless contraindicated — this is the number to memorize. It prevents gastric reflux aspiration and also improves distribution of ventilation and diaphragm efficiency. (Deck adds the contraindications: after neurosurgery, or with severe hypotension.)
  • Daily "sedation vacations" plus daily spontaneous breathing trials (SBTs) — watch for self-extubation when sedation is stopped. (Deck specifies a sedative vacation of 6 to 8 hours daily and pairs the SBT with a daily spontaneous awakening trial.)
  • Peptic ulcer disease prophylaxis — using sucralfate rather than H2 antagonists. (Heuer only; the decks omit this.)
  • DVT prophylaxis. (Heuer Ch 20 only.)
  • Daily oral care with chlorhexidine, plus assessing the oral cavity, rotating the oral ETT position at least every 24 hours, brushing the teeth, and applying mouth moisturizer/lip balm.
  • Subglottic ETTs (Hi-Lo tubes) with a separate dorsal lumen that continuously removes secretions pooled just above the cuff.
  • In-line / closed-suction catheter system so the circuit is never broken; the AARC endorses this. Use a T-adapter with a one-way valve for in-line nebulized medications for the same reason.

Airway and equipment measures: avoid intubation entirely where possible (use NIV); if intubating, use the oral route (nasal intubation causes sinusitis and more VAP); maintain cuff pressure 20–30 cm H₂O and avoid the minimal leak technique; flush suction catheters with sterile water or saline only if needed. Circuit changes — a real conflict. The RCP 105 Wk 5 deck's bundle slide lists "change ventilator circuits every 7 days" and "change inline suction devices and HMEs every 24 hours," but the same deck's speaker notes and Heuer/CDC/AARC both say the opposite: do NOT change circuits on a routine schedule — change only when visibly soiled or malfunctioning, because unnecessary changes increase contamination.

Same for HMEs: place them vertically above the tracheal tube and change only when visibly soiled. VAP incidence does not differ between heated humidification and HMEs; if using a heated humidifier, drain condensate away from the patient regularly without breaking the circuit.

If you remember only four things: cuff pressure 20–30 cm H₂O, head of bed 30–45°, wash your hands, and extubate as early as it is safe — the deck's clinical pearl is that early liberation from mechanical ventilation is the single best way to prevent VAP. One hospital bundling these steps cut VAP incidence by 95% in three years.

SourceHeuer Ch 9 — Initiate Protocols to Prevent Ventilator-Associated Infections (PDF p471-473, T4 tips p479) · Heuer Ch 20 — Ventilator-Associated Pneumonia, CSE Case Management Pearls (book p461-462) · RCP 105 Wk 5 (Chang Chp 14-15) deck, slides 43-47 · Infectious Pulmonary Disease deck, slide 18 · RCP 105 Wk 1 Lctr 2 (Airway Mgmt, Hess Chp 17) deck, slides 15-16

5Tuberculosis

Organism

Mycobacterium tuberculosis — an aerobic, rod-shaped bacillus with a waxy capsule that makes it resistant to many disinfectants (which is why a bronchoscope used on a TB patient gets sterilized, not just disinfected) and requires acid-fast staining to be seen. Because it is an obligate aerobe it thrives where oxygen tension is highest — the lung apices, which is why TB is classically upper-lobe disease. TB is a chronic, contagious bacterial infection that primarily attacks the lungs but can spread to almost any organ.

Transmission

Almost exclusively by aerosol droplets from coughing, sneezing, or laughing. The droplet nuclei are 0.5–1.0 μm and can remain airborne for hours — well under Heuer's 5 μm airborne cutoff — which makes TB highly contagious in enclosed spaces. Hence airborne precautions (Item 1): standard precautions plus a fit-tested N95 (PAPR for high-risk situations or poor fit) in a negative-pressure AIIR with the door closed.

Remember the PPE asymmetry: the patient wears a surgical mask; the provider wears an N95 or higher. Limit transport. Heuer specifically flags PFT testing as requiring airborne precautions when TB exposure is possible, and the decks list the aerosol-generating, high-risk procedures: intubation, sputum induction, bronchoscopy, chest physiotherapy, and aerosolized medication delivery.

Clinical forms

Primary TB — initial infection; inhaled bacilli implant in alveoli and multiply over 3 to 4 weeks, then get walled off in a tubercle/granuloma with a caseous necrotic core. Latent TB — bacilli sealed inside tubercles, dormant for months, years, or life, usually asymptomatic and not infectious. Reactivation (active/postprimary) TB — recurrence when immunity falls (malnutrition, crowded or institutional living, HIV/AIDS, steroids, alcohol abuse), presenting with low-grade fever, extreme fatigue, night sweats, violent cough, greenish or bloody sputum, chest pain, anorexia and weight loss; the patient is highly infectious at this stage.

Disseminated (miliary/extrapulmonary) TB — bacilli spread via blood or lymph, seeding 1–5 mm tubercles in lymph nodes, kidneys, long bones, genital tract, brain and meninges.

Which test for which question (Heuer's framing)

Recommend the tuberculin skin test to diagnose a latent TB infection. Recommend serial sputum collection for AFB stain to detect an active infection. Chest x-ray and IGRA are added concurrently to confirm active TB or prior exposure. Heuer's high-risk list for skin testing: TB symptoms (fever, night sweats, cough, weight loss), known exposure, origin in a high-prevalence country, living or working in shelters/prisons/some nursing homes, HIV or other immunosuppression, and illegal drug use.

TestDetail
Mantoux tuberculin skin test (PPD)Intradermal PPD injection; read the induration at 48–72 hours. Deck thresholds: < 5 mm = negative; 5–9 mm = suspicious; ≥ 10 mm = positive — positive means exposure or latent TB, not necessarily active disease. Heuer describes the Mantoux test but gives no induration cut-offs and no read time at all, so the millimeter numbers here are the deck's alone; be aware the CDC's published scheme is risk-stratified (5 / 10 / 15 mm) rather than the single 10 mm line the deck teaches — answer with the deck's numbers for this course
Sputum AFB smear & cultureDefinitive diagnosis requires isolating bacilli from sputum, BAL, pleural fluid, or tissue biopsy. Collect 3 sputum samples over 8–24 hours, each ≥ 5 mL of deep lung secretions, including an early-morning sample — saliva and nasopharyngeal secretions are not acceptable. Stains: Ziehl-Neelsen = red bacilli on a blue background; fluorescent stain = yellow-green bacilli, easier to read
Sputum induction (the RT's job)Hypertonic saline (3%, 5%, 7%, or 10%) via ultrasonic nebulizer, or a jet nebulizer that can run continuously for the 15- to 20-minute session. Must be done in a negative-pressure room or booth with the RT in an N95, using a valved breathing circuit with an expiratory HEPA filter. Pretreat reactive-airway patients with a fast-acting beta agonist and get a baseline FEV₁ 10–15 minutes after; stop if FEV₁ falls ≥ 20%. An adequate sample is 2 to 5 mL; 4–5 mL is needed for reliable AFB analysis. Space repeat attempts at least 24 hours apart
Interferon-gamma release assay (IGRA) / QuantiFERON-TB GoldBlood test measuring IFN-γ release after exposure to TB antigens. Heuer: as sensitive as the Mantoux test for latent TB and more sensitive for active TB
Xpert MTB/RIF & UltraRapid molecular test, results in < 2 hours; detects TB and rifampin resistance. Ultra is more sensitive for smear-negative, pediatric, extrapulmonary, and HIV cases

Classic chest x-ray

Upper-lobe / apical disease. Early primary TB shows a Ghon nodule (a small area of consolidation), becoming a Ghon complex when the draining lymph nodes are involved. Advanced disease shows cavitary lesions, with or without an air-fluid level. Chronic or healed disease shows calcification, fibrosis, pleural effusion, and retraction of lung segments. Miliary TB = numerous tiny nodules scattered throughout both lungs.

Right ventricular enlargement signals cor pulmonale. The deck's case study is the template to recognize: bilateral apical calcification, a cavity in the right upper lobe, and consolidation in the right middle lobe, with dull percussion over the apices and coarse crackles. Physiologically, extensive TB produces a restrictive pattern: ↓ FVC, ↓ VC / IC / TLC, with a normal or slightly increased FEV₁/FVC ratio; PFTs are usually normal unless disease is extensive.

Drug treatment

Heuer gives no TB drug regimen anywhere in the book — every number below is from the Infectious Pulmonary Disease deck. Standard therapy is 2–4 drugs for 6–9 months. The six-month protocol: an induction phase (first 2 months) of INH + RIF + PZA + either EMB or SM, then a continuation phase (next 4 months) of INH + RIF daily or twice weekly.

The nine-month protocol: INH + RIF daily for 1–2 months, then twice weekly out to 9 months. Isoniazid (INH) is the most effective first-line agent; rifampin (RIF) is bactericidal and paired with INH. Prophylaxis: INH daily for 1 year for exposed individuals or a positive PPD. Patients usually become non-contagious after a few weeks, but completing the full course is essential. Noncompliance with these long regimens drives MDR-TB, countered by Directly Observed Therapy (DOT) — a healthcare worker watches each dose — and, once resistance is documented, by adding ≥ 3 additional antibiotics and extending the duration.

The deck teaches the induction drugs as INH + RIF + PZA + EMB/SM and never uses the acronym "RIPE", though those are the same four drugs.

SourceHeuer Ch 5 — Testing for Tuberculosis, Tuberculin Skin Test (Mantoux) and IGRA (PDF p259, T4 tips p280) · Heuer Ch 7 — Airborne Precautions Table 7-4 and PFT airborne precautions (PDF p383-385, p391) · Heuer Ch 3 — Sputum Induction (PDF p177-179, tips p195) · Infectious Pulmonary Disease deck, slides 48, 55-57, 59-64 and 65-67 · RCP 203 Lssn 2b (Chp 3) deck, slide 63 · RCP 103 Clinical Data deck, slides 57 and 59
02

Medical Gas Delivery, Cylinders & Flow Devices

10 items · Cylinders, LOX and gaseous systems · cylinder factors and duration · concentrators · home delivery devices · flow and delivered O2 · condensate · air-entrainment · NRB · Briggs adaptor · Bourdon vs Thorpe

1Oxygen Cylinders — Use, Storage, Maintenance, Safety Systems, Change-Out2Cylinder Factors and Duration-of-Flow Calculations3Liquid Oxygen (LOX) and Gaseous Systems — Stationary and Portable4Oxygen Concentrators — How O2 Is Produced, Purity, Maintenance5Home Oxygen Delivery Devices — Concentrator vs LOX vs Cylinders vs Conserving Devices6How Changes in Flow Affect Delivered O2 Concentration; Condensate / Rainout7Air-Entrainment Devices and Inspiratory Flow Demand8Non-Rebreather (NRB) Mask9Briggs Adaptor (T-Piece)10Bourdon Gauge Regulator vs Thorpe Tube Flowmeter

1Oxygen Cylinders — Use, Storage, Maintenance, Safety Systems, Change-Out

In the hospital, O2 and air reach the bedside as piped wall gas at a standard working pressure of 50 psi, which is what ventilators, blenders and flowmeters are built to run on. High-pressure cylinders are used only where there is no piped gas, during ambulation/transport, or as a home backup. Specialty gases (He, NO, CO, CO2) are available only in cylinders.

Sizes

If portability is the priority, pick a small cylinder — A through E, or M. If portability doesn't matter or the run will be long, pick a large cylinder — G, H, or K. In home care, ambulatory patients typically use small aluminum cylinders sized M-6/B, M-9/C, D, or E, and because home flows usually run 0.25–4.0 L/min, a low-flow metering device is used.

An H cylinder in the home is a backup for concentrator failure or power outage, or for infants on very low flow — impractical otherwise because H tanks must be tied together and swapped often.

Full pressure

Heuer's duration table "assumes a full cylinder is at 2200 psi," with the explicit warning that filling pressures vary — always check the gauge before computing.

Safety connection systems. Heuer names two by function:

  • DISS (Diameter Index Safety System) — the threaded connection used to take 50 psi directly off a piped source for ventilators/blenders. Also the connector for wall vacuum/suction.
  • PISS (Pin Index Safety System) — the yoke used on small cylinders. Heuer's troubleshooting line is the testable one: a hissing tank means a loose connection or a missing/damaged washer on a PISS regulator. In home care the same clue reads "hissing from the regulator → regulator not seated properly, tighten it, or the washer is missing/needs replacing."
  • ASSS (American Standard Safety System, the threaded outlet on large cylinders): Not covered in Heuer or the available decks. Likewise the 10-year hydrostatic test interval is Not covered in Heuer or the available decks.
Heuer's 7 safe-use steps (memorize the order)(1) check the label to confirm the correct gas; (2) confirm you have enough gas for the planned duration; (3) transport large cylinders chained to a wheeled cart and put small cylinders in the gurney/wheelchair holder; (4) investigate any hissing (loose connection, missing/damaged washer); (5) open the cylinder valve slowly all the way, then turn it back half a turn to keep the valve from "freezing" fully open; (6) record the pressure and compute duration of flow; (7) before disconnecting anything, close the cylinder valve and bleed the pressure out of attached devices.

Storage

Secure the cylinders, store in a well-ventilated area, and keep full cylinders separated from empty ones. In the home, teach no smoking / no open flame (fireplace, stove), grounded plugs, a no-smoking sign on the door, and tank storage attention to location, secureness, and temperature.

MRI

Use iron-free equipment only — aluminum cylinders and carts, brass and aluminum regulators. The deck says the same thing more bluntly: the magnet can turn a steel cylinder into a projectile, so only aluminum oxygen cylinders enter the suite.

Change-out rules

Heuer's stated practice: change out a cylinder at least 15–30 minutes before the contents are fully exhausted, or when pressure drops below 500 psi. The transport deck adds its own rule: an "E" cylinder with a wrench must be full before transport, and for a possible delay carry at least twice the volume needed for a one-way trip.

SourceHeuer Ch 6 — Gas Delivery, Metering, and Clinical Analyzing Devices / Gaseous Oxygen Cylinders (PDF p298-301) · Heuer Ch 17 — Home O2 Systems / Compressed (Gaseous) O2 Cylinders, Table 17-3 (PDF p775-776, p779-780) · Heuer App B — Equipment Calculations, Table B-6 (PDF p1048) · RCP 105 Wk 5 deck (Procedures Related to Mech Vent), slides 16, 20, 23-24

2Cylinder Factors and Duration-of-Flow Calculations

This is the guaranteed calculation question. The formula:

Duration of flow (min) = (gauge pressure in psig × cylinder factor) ÷ flow (L/min)

Cylinder factors — Heuer Table 6-10 (Ch 6, PDF p301):

CylinderM-6 / BM-9 / CDEGH / K
Cylinder factor (L/psig)0.070.110.160.282.143.14
Hours of flow at 1 L/min (full @ 2200 psi)2.64.05.910.378.5115.1

Cylinder factors — Heuer Appendix B Table B-6 (PDF p1048), which adds a heliox row:

GasDEGH / K
O2, air0.160.282.413.14
He/O20.140.231.932.50
Gap⚠ Heuer contradicts itself on the G cylinder. Table 6-10 (Ch 6, p301) gives G = 2.14; Appendix B Table B-6 (p1048) gives G = 2.41. Table 6-10's own duration column is internally consistent with 2.14 (2.14 × 2200 = 4,708 min = 78.5 h, which is the printed value; 2.41 would give 88.4 h). The widely published textbook value is 2.41. Know both numbers and pick whichever matches the answer choices. Every other factor agrees between the two tables.
Ballpark checks (Heuer App B, p1048)a full E cylinder at 10 L/min lasts about 1 hour; a full H cylinder at 10 L/min lasts about 10 times longer — 10 hours.
Worked example 1 — Heuer Problem B.26 (p1048)How long will an E cylinder with a gauge pressure of 800 psi set to deliver 5 L/min take to become empty?
Duration (min) = (psig × factor) ÷ flow Duration (min) = (800 × 0.28) ÷ 5 Duration (min) = 224 ÷ 5 Duration = 44.8 ≈ 45 minutes
Worked example 2 — the change-out version (built on Heuer's own 500-psi rule)A full E cylinder reads 2200 psi and the patient is on 3 L/min. How long until you must swap it, using the "change out at 500 psi" rule?
Usable pressure = 2200 − 500 = 1700 psig Duration (min) = (1700 × 0.28) ÷ 3 Duration (min) = 476 ÷ 3 Duration = 158.7 min ≈ 2 hours 39 minutes Sanity check against the ballpark rule: a full E at 10 L/min ≈ 60 min, so at 3 L/min ≈ 200 min to bone-dry — 158 min to the 500-psi swap point is the right order of magnitude. ✔

Worked example 3 — a large cylinder. An H cylinder reads 1800 psi and the patient needs 6 L/min. How long will it run to empty?

Duration (min) = (1800 × 3.14) ÷ 6 Duration (min) = 5652 ÷ 6 Duration = 942 minutes = 15.7 hours Ballpark check: a full H (2200 psi) at 10 L/min ≈ 10 h. This tank is at ~82% of full and running at ~60% of that flow, so ~10 h × 0.82 ÷ 0.6 ≈ 14 h. ✔
SourceHeuer Ch 6 — Gaseous Oxygen Cylinders, Table 6-10 (PDF p300-301) · Heuer App B — Equipment Calculations, Table B-6, Problem B.26 (PDF p1048) · RCP 203 Lssn 2b deck, slide 31 (points you to Appendix B, p521-534, as the complete list of NBRC calculations) · RCP 105 Wk 5 deck, slide 20 (the "twice the one-way volume" transport rule)

3Liquid Oxygen (LOX) and Gaseous Systems — Stationary and Portable

How LOX worksOxygen is stored as a cryogenic liquid in an insulated reservoir and vaporizes into a gas before it reaches the patient. The reason to bother: 1 L of liquid O2 vaporizes into 860 L of gaseous O2 — an enormously efficient way to store a large supply in a small footprint. Heuer notes that with portable concentrators now available, LOX is used less often than in the past.

Stationary ("base") unit. Depending on the model, the home base reservoir holds 45 to 100 pounds of LOX.

Portable unit

Filled from the base unit as needed. A full typical portable holds about 1 L of LOX, weighs less than 6 pounds, and — when paired with a demand-flow/conserving device — provides 8 hours or more of O2.

The conversion factor and the duration formula. At normal operating pressures, 1 pound of LOX ≈ 344 L of gaseous O2. Convert weight → gas volume, then divide by flow:

Gaseous O2 available (L) = LOX weight (lb) × 344 Duration (min) = total gaseous O2 (L) ÷ flow (L/min)
Worked example 1 — Heuer's own stationary-unit example (Ch 6 T4 tips, p371)A LOX home storage unit filled to 75 lb, running continuously at 2 L/min:
Gas available = 75 lb × 344 L/lb = 25,800 L (Heuer rounds this to "about 26,000 L") Duration = 25,800 ÷ 2 = 12,900 minutes = 12,900 ÷ 60 = 215 hours = 215 ÷ 24 ≈ 9 days Heuer's phrasing: "enough to last more than a week at a constant flow of 2 L/min (more if using a pulse-dose system)." ✔
Worked example 2 — the portable unit, cross-checked two waysA full portable holds ~1 L of LOX. How long at 2 L/min continuous?
By the liquid-to-gas factor: 1 L liquid × 860 L gas/L liquid = 860 L gas 860 ÷ 2 = 430 min = 7.2 hours By weight (1 L LOX ≈ 2.5 lb): 2.5 lb × 344 = 860 L gas — same answer ✔ Heuer says a portable "can provide 8 hours or more" WITH a demand-flow device — consistent, because conserving devices cut O2 use by 50–75% (see item 5).
Evaporation and venting — the exam questionLOX is stored below its boiling point, so it warms and boils off continuously and the reservoir must vent. Heuer gives the clinical decision rule:
  • Intermittent hissing from a stationary LOX tank is NORMAL. No action needed except to keep the tank upright.
  • Loud, constant hissing — especially with a steady stream of "mist" coming off the tank — is a PROBLEM. Put the patient on backup O2 and call the supplier.
  • If the LOX reservoir is empty, no O2 is delivered → refill the reservoir (same troubleshooting branch as "concentrator is off" or "gas cylinder is empty").

Gaseous side, stationary vs portable

Stationary = piped wall gas at 50 psi in hospital, or an H/K cylinder with a preset 50-psi reducing valve where piped gas is unavailable. Portable = small cylinder (M-6/B, M-9/C, D, E) with a reducing valve and flowmeter, sized by portability need and planned duration. Gaseous tank troubleshooting: regulator on but no O2 flowing → the tank was never turned on, or it is empty; if flow still fails with both tank and regulator open, replace the tank.

SourceHeuer Ch 6 — LTOT / Liquid Oxygen (LOX) Systems, Table 6-12 (PDF p302-303, p305), T4 Test-Taking Tips (PDF p371) · Heuer Ch 17 — Liquid O2 (LOX) Systems, Table 17-3 (PDF p779-780) · Not covered in the available decks — no deck in the 62-file corpus mentions liquid oxygen, LOX, or the 860/344 conversion factors; the decks' only stationary-vs-portable gas content is the ventilator loss-of-50-psi-source alarm (RCP 105 Wk 1 Lctr 3 deck, slide 93) and transport cylinders (RCP 105 Wk 5 deck, slide 20)

4Oxygen Concentrators — How O2 Is Produced, Purity, Maintenance

How it worksA concentrator is an electrically powered device that physically separates the O2 in room air from nitrogen. Most use sodium-aluminum silicate pellets (a molecular sieve) that adsorb nitrogen, CO2, and water vapor, letting oxygen pass through.

Purity and the flow relationship

Heuer's number: concentrators produce about 90–95% O2 at flows up to 10 L/min. The clinically enforced floor is the maintenance threshold below: an analyzed output less than 85–90% of the manufacturer's spec means the sieve is failing. Heuer does not print a purity-vs-flow decay curve — the mechanism (higher flow = less contact time with the sieve = more nitrogen slips through = falling FiO2 as you approach and exceed the rated maximum) is the reason for the "up to 10 L/min" ceiling but is not stated numerically in Heuer or the available decks.

Concentrators are the most cost-efficient supply method for patients in alternative settings needing continuous low-flow O2.

Portable concentrators (POCs)

Smaller units powered by household AC, 12-volt DC (cars, RVs), or batteries. Typical battery life 1–4 hours, with optional packs extending to more than 6 hours. Most POCs deliver pulse-dose only, which is enough for patients needing 30% O2 or less; only continuous-flow units can provide more than 30% O2, which is what's needed for adequate FiO2 at altitude (airliner cabins) — and continuous-flow POCs are bigger and heavier, so less portable.

POC start-up sequence (Heuer, p302)

(1) check the air intake filter is clean and correctly positioned; (2) place the unit in a well-ventilated area with inlets/outlets unobstructed (keep a window open in a small room or car); (3) connect to the best power source — AC first, auto-DC next, battery last; (4) attach a pulse-dose nasal cannula; (5) power on, confirm self-test, set the prescribed flow; (6) confirm the unit is sensing inhalation (flashing indicator + pulse sound); (7) expect maximum O2 output in approximately 1–2 minutes after start-up.

Maintenance and troubleshooting (this is what gets tested):

ClueCauseAction
Unit will not turn onPower source / plug / circuit breakerCheck power; if power is fine, put patient on the gaseous backup and replace the concentrator
Analyzed FiO2 < 85–90% of manufacturer's specSodium-aluminum pellets are exhaustedSwitch to gaseous backup; replace the concentrator or send for service
Concentrator alarmIntake filters blocked or dirtyClean the intake filters; keep them a proper distance from walls and curtains
Low O2 output alarmFlowmeter turned off, or unit needs serviceTurn flow on / call provider
Patient on cannula at ≥ 4 L/min complains of nasal drynessDry gasAdd a bubble humidifier
No O2 deliveredKinked/obstructed tubing, or source offCheck the whole tubing run; turn the unit on
General malfunction—Switch to the backup tank, contact the service provider

POC warning indicators (Ch 17 Table 17-2)

Low battery → switch to AC/DC and charge; if no power available, go to backup O2. Cannula disconnect → check connections, breathe through your nose; if it persists, backup O2 + call provider. Capacity exceeded → reduce activity, switch to backup. General malfunction / service needed / low O2 concentration → backup O2 and call the provider.

Placement teaching: there is a limit to how much O2 tubing can be added, so the stationary unit must sit as central as possible in the patient's range of activity; account for the heat it generates (a closet or small room may not work); don't put intake filters against a wall or curtain; warn that O2 tubing is a trip hazard.

Deck contribution

The decks give only the travel angle: unlike oxygen cylinders, a POC continuously extracts oxygen from the surrounding air, which is why it works for travel rather than being a finite supply. A POC reduces high-altitude hypoxia in patients not requiring mechanical ventilation — relevant because cabins are pressurized to 8,000 ft (PB 564 mm Hg, PaO2 ≈ 59 mm Hg). Battery life is the biggest single consideration because airlines are not required to supply electricity or oxygen for non-emergency use; carry battery for at least 150% of one-way travel time.

FAA approved several POCs in 2005; examples under 10 lbs with max battery duration: AirSep FreeStyle (4.4 lbs, 10 h), Delphi Central Air (9.8 lbs, 3 h 25 min), Inogen One G2 (7 lbs, 8 h), Invacare XPO2 (6 lbs, 7 h), Inova Labs LifeChoice (4.9 lbs, 5 h), Phillips Respironics EverGo (8.5 lbs, 12 h).

SourceHeuer Ch 6 — LTOT / Oxygen Concentrators, Table 6-12 (PDF p301-302, p305) · Heuer Ch 17 — Oxygen Concentrators, Tables 17-2 and 17-3 (PDF p776, p778-780) · Heuer Ch 6 T4 Test-Taking Tips (PDF p371) · RCP 105 Wk 7 deck (Mech Vent Nontraditional Settings), slide 38

5Home Oxygen Delivery Devices — Concentrator vs LOX vs Cylinders vs Conserving Devices

Home O2 systems are concentrators, LOX systems, and high-pressure gaseous cylinders — often in combination. Bulk systems with wall outlets are not an option at home, so the system must be safe and compatible in the home, and portability matters because these patients are often active.

O2 Concentrator (stationary / POC)Liquid O2 (LOX)Gaseous CylindersO2-Conserving / Demand-Flow Device
How O2 is suppliedElectrically separates O2 from N2 in room air with sodium-aluminum silicate pellets — never runs out while poweredCryogenic liquid in an insulated reservoir, vaporized to gas before deliveryCompressed gas at up to 2200 psi, finite volumeNot a source — an accessory that fires O2 only on inspiration
Purity / output90–95% O2, flows up to 10 L/minEssentially 100%Essentially 100%Same as its source
CapacityUnlimited while poweredBase unit 45–100 lb (1 lb ≈ 344 L gas; 1 L liquid = 860 L gas)Sized M-6/B, M-9/C, D, E for ambulation; H for backupReduces O2 use by 50–75%, doubling or tripling duration of flow
Portable versionPOC — AC / 12-V DC / battery; battery 1–4 h, packs > 6 h; most are pulse-dose only (≤ 30% O2); only continuous-flow units exceed 30%Portable strap-on holds ~1 L LOX, weighs < 6 lb, 8 h or more with a demand deviceSmall aluminum cylinders; home flows usually 0.25–4.0 L/min → use a low-flow metering deviceTwo types: simple reservoir cannulas and pulse-dose/demand-flow systems
Main advantageMost cost-efficient for continuous low-flow; no deliveriesEfficient storage of a very large supply in a small packageSimple, no power required — the backup for power failureExtends every other system's duration
Main drawbackNeeds electricity; generates heat; sieve pellets wear outContinuous evaporative loss/venting; needs supplier refills; increasingly uncommonRuns out; H tanks must be tied together and replaced frequentlyReservoir cannulas are bulky and poorly tolerated; demand systems must be individually titrated to SpO2
Failure mode → actionSieve exhausted (FiO2 < 85–90% of spec) or power loss → gaseous backup, replace unitLoud constant hissing + mist → backup O2, call supplier (intermittent hissing is normal)Empty / not turned on → replace the tankIf the device fails, switch to a continuous O2 source at 2–3× the conserving-device flow

O2-conserving devices in detail

All of them minimize the O2 wasted during exhalation with a standard nasal cannula. They cut O2 usage by 50–75%, which doubles or triples duration of flow from a bulk source. Simple reservoir cannulas store O2 in a small reservoir during exhalation and release it on inhalation — effective but bulky and not well tolerated. Pulse-dose/demand-flow systems use a sensor to trigger a valve that delivers O2 only during inspiration; systems differ, so flow must be individually adjusted to reach the desired SpO2.

Demand-flow sensor alarm troubleshooting: turn the unit off and back on to reset; check that cannula/tubing connections are tight and not kinked; adjust the cannula for a comfortable fit, then initiate an inspiration.

Heuer's prescribing rules (these are the NBRC scenarios):

  • Patient with restricted activity → concentrator plus a gaseous cylinder for backup.
  • Ambulatory patient → always recommend a portable source (portable concentrator, LOX unit, or small cylinder).
  • Especially active/mobile, or needing extended duration of flow → make sure the system incorporates an O2-conserving device.
  • Sleep-only user → a stationary system without portables will suffice.
  • Appliance of choice at home = a simple low-flow device (usually nasal cannula) or an O2-conserving system. Home cannula flows are generally ≤ 4 L/min for adults, ≤ 2 L/min for infants; at those flows supplemental humidification is generally not needed, but add it for anyone complaining of nasal dryness.

Medicare/insurance qualifying criteria (Heuer Ch 17, p777): for hypoxemia at rest (covers stationary and portable), COPD or other chronic pulmonary disease with SpO2 ≤ 88% or PaO2 ≤ 55 torr, at rest, on room air — and for a hospitalized patient the value must be obtained no sooner than 48 hours before discharge. For sleep-only coverage (stationary, not portable): PaO2 ≤ 55 mm Hg or SaO2 ≤ 88% during sleep in a patient who is PaO2 ≥ 56 mm Hg / SaO2 ≥ 89% awake, or a greater-than-normal nocturnal fall (PaO2 drop > 10 mm Hg).

Deck contributionThe RCP 203 deck gives the same qualifying thresholds from the nocturnal side: nocturnal oximetry showing a > 5% drop in SpO2, or an SpO2 less than 88% — "basically the same as for continuous LTOT in COPD (SaO2 ≤ 88% or PaO2 ≤ 55 torr on room air) — QUALIFY FOR HOME O2!!" The TDP deck names a dedicated Oxygen Delivery Device Protocol in the AARC protocol family and carries the O2 protocol through to post-discharge considerations such as pulmonary rehab or home oxygen therapy. The RCP 105 Wk 7 deck adds the POC travel detail (see item 4).
SourceHeuer Ch 6 — LTOT / Oxygen-Conserving or Demand-Flow Devices, Tables 6-12 and 6-13 (PDF p301-307) · Heuer Ch 17 — Home O2 Systems, Oxygen Appliances, Recommending/Troubleshooting Home O2 Systems, Tables 17-2/17-3 (PDF p777-781) · RCP 203 Lssn 3a deck, slide 66 · TDP/Communication deck, slides 19-20 · RCP 105 Wk 7 deck, slide 38

6How Changes in Flow Affect Delivered O2 Concentration; Condensate / Rainout

The low-flow rule

A low-flow device delivers gas at a flow less than the patient's inspiratory flow, so the delivered O2 is always diluted with entrained room air and FiO2 is variable. That's why low-flow devices are ordered in L/min, not FiO2 — the FiO2 ranges printed on any chart are estimates only. The adult rule of thumb: each 1 L/min of nasal cannula flow raises FiO2 by roughly 4% above room air (so 1 L/min ≈ 24%, 2 ≈ 28%, up to 6 L/min ≈ 44–45%; Heuer's stated adult cannula range is 1–6 L/min, 22–45%).

Heuer Table 6-2 — what pushes FiO2 up vs down (memorize this two-column table):

Less air dilution → HIGHER FiO2More air dilution → LOWER FiO2
Higher O2 flow inputLower O2 flow input
Reservoir present (masks)Reservoir absent (cannula)
Leak absent (masks)Leak present (masks)
Valves present (masks)Valves absent (masks)
Lower patient inspiratory flowHigher patient inspiratory flow
Lower tidal volumeHigher tidal volume
Slower respiratory rateFaster respiratory rate
Lower minute ventilationHigher minute ventilation

Note the four device factors are things you control and the four patient factors are things you can only observe — which is why with a low-flow system you always evaluate the patient's response to set the flow.

The high-flow rule — the opposite, and the one students get wrong. On a high-flow device you titrate by changing FiO2, NOT by changing flow. If you turn the flow down on an air-entrainment device, the FiO2 does not change — but the reduced total flow may no longer meet the patient's inspiratory demand, at which point the patient starts entraining room air and the delivered O2 becomes variable again. On a reservoir system, cutting flow can additionally cause CO2 rebreathing and suffocation.

Obstructing an entrainment port (bedding, clothing, a hand) decreases air entrainment → higher delivered O2 concentration but lower total flow, with an unpredictable net effect on the FiO2 the patient actually inspires. Remove the obstruction and monitor.

On blenders, FiO2 varies with the setting and, to a small degree, with flow: higher flows minimize or eliminate air dilution, so set the desired O2% on the blender and run the high end of the patient's flow range to guarantee it.

Condensate / rainout — the three exam presentations:

  1. Aerosol tubing entirely blocked by condensate — the tell is tubing filled with water and NO gurgling sound. → Drain the tubing condensate and add a water trap.
  2. Delivered O2% is HIGHER than set or expected — condensate creates back pressure in the circuit, which acts like a partially obstructed entrainment port. → Drain the condensate, add a water trap, check/correct kinking or other outlet obstructions. (Ch 13 states the same thing as "obstruction caused by condensate rainout in the tubing → recommend a water trap.")
  3. Intermittent flow or "bubbling" in a heated-humidifier tubing circuit = partial obstruction with condensate.
Why it rains outgas leaves a heated humidifier saturated and cools as it travels down the tubing, so water vapor condenses and pools. Fixes: heated-wire circuit, inline water trap, or an HME for temporary use. An HFNC avoids the problem by design — it includes a heated delivery system to prevent tubing condensation and gas cooling, and delivers gas at BTPS without condensation. Water traps go at the low points of a circuit.
Deck contribution

The decks teach the flow→oxygenation link with the rule of thumb that PaO2 should be at least 5 times the O2% — so 50% should give a PaO2 of at least 250 torr, 21% gives 80–100 torr, 100% about 500 torr. Decision rule from the TDP algorithm: if the patient needs an FiO2 greater than 0.40, select an appropriate high-flow system; otherwise start continuous low-flow O2 and adjust FiO2 per guidelines, titrating on SpO2 and work of breathing, and use pulse oximetry whenever FiO2 ≥ 0.44.

Standard nasal cannula is titrated in 1–2 L/min increments; high-flow systems can reach FiO2 ≥ 0.90; discharge goal SpO2 ≥ 91% or baseline, watching for oxygen toxicity. In COPD the target is SpO2 88–92% because excessive FiO2 can worsen hypercapnia, and Venturi masks and HFNC are the named devices for precise FiO2 control. On condensate the decks teach the ventilator-circuit version: condensation is the most common threat to circuit patency — gas cools traveling from the heated humidifier, water collects, and this leads to significant airflow obstruction; fixed with a heated-wire circuit, an inline water trap, or an HME (efficiency 70–90% RH at 30–31 °C).

A weaning checkpoint asks literally, "Is there water (condensation) in the ventilator tubing?" — a patient failing against a circuit full of condensate is failing on added resistance, not weak lungs. In sidestream capnography, secretions and water get drawn into the sampling tube and occlude it, whereas mainstream sampling is unaffected by water condensation.

SourceHeuer Ch 6 — Low-Flow Devices/Systems, Table 6-1, Table 6-2, Air-Entrainment Systems, Blending Systems, Table 6-6 (PDF p287-296), T4 Test-Taking Tips (PDF p368) · Heuer Ch 13 — Recommending Changes in Oxygen Therapy, Table 13-8 · RCP 203 Lssn 3a deck, slides 32-33 · TDP/Communication deck, slides 19-20 · COPD deck, slide 28 · RCP 105 Mgmt of Mech Vent deck, slides 37-38 · RCP 105 Wk 3 deck, slide 28 · RCP 105 Monitoring in Mech Vent deck, slide 44

7Air-Entrainment Devices and Inspiratory Flow Demand

How they workAir-entrainment systems mix air and O2 at specific ratios set by the jet and port size. Bigger ports and smaller jets → more air dilution → lower FiO2 and HIGHER total flow. Smaller ports and bigger jets → less dilution → higher FiO2 but LOWER total flow. That inverse relationship is the whole topic.

Heuer Table 6-5 — air-to-O2 ratios and total output (Ch 6, PDF p294):

Delivered O2 %Air : O2 ratioTotal ratio parts (air + O2)Total flow at 10 L/min O2 input
80%0.3 : 11.313 L/min
70%0.6 : 11.616 L/min
60%1 : 1220 L/min
50%1.7 : 12.727 L/min
40%3 : 1440 L/min
35%5 : 1660 L/min
31%7 : 1880 L/min
28%10 : 111110 L/min
24%25 : 126260 L/min
Anchor values to memorize60% = 1:1 (the pivot). Anything below 60% has a ratio greater than 1:1 (e.g. 1.7:1 at 50%); anything above 60% has a ratio less than 1:1 (e.g. 0.6:1 at 70%). Heuer states the two endpoints in prose too: at FiO2 0.80, every 1 L/min of flowmeter setting yields 1.3 L/min of output; at FiO2 0.24, every 1 L/min yields 26 L/min.

The ratio formula ("magic box"):

Air : O2 ratio = (100 − desired O2%) ÷ (desired O2% − 21)

Total flow formula (Heuer App B, p1049):

Total flow = input (O2) flow × total ratio parts = input flow × (air parts + O2 parts)

Meeting inspiratory demand — Heuer's benchmark

"In general, the best way to ensure a stable FIO2 is to provide a total flow of at least 40 L/min." At an input flow of 10 L/min, Table 6-5 shows that only settings at or below 40% O2 clear that bar. You can raise total flow somewhat by raising the O2 input, but input is limited by the back-pressure created at the device jet, typically to 12–15 L/min.

Beyond that, run two air-entrainment nebulizers in parallel to double the flow. Device input ranges: air-entrainment (Venturi) mask 4–12 L/min input, FiO2 24–50%; air-entrainment nebulizer 10–15 L/min input, 28–100%, with output that should exceed 60 L/min to ensure a stable FiO2. Both become less precise at FiO2 ≥ 0.35 precisely because total flow falls as FiO2 rises.

Worked example 1 — Heuer Problems B.27 and B.28 chained (p1048-1049)What is the air-to-O2 ratio for a 35% air-entrainment mask, and with an input O2 flow of 8 L/min, what total flow reaches the patient?
Air:O2 = (100 − 35) ÷ (35 − 21) Air:O2 = 65 ÷ 14 Air:O2 = 4.6 : 1 (Heuer rounds to 5:1) Total flow = input flow × (air parts + O2 parts) Total flow = 8 × (5 + 1) Total flow = 8 × 6 Total flow = 48 L/min
Worked example 2 — does the device meet the patient's demand? (the decision question)An adult is set up on a 40% air-entrainment mask with the O2 flowmeter at 8 L/min. Total flow must be at least 40 L/min to guarantee a fixed FiO2. Does this setup meet demand?
Step 1 — ratio at 40%: Air:O2 = (100 − 40) ÷ (40 − 21) = 60 ÷ 19 = 3.16 ≈ 3:1 (Table 6-5 confirms 3:1) Step 2 — total ratio parts: 3 (air) + 1 (O2) = 4 parts Step 3 — total flow: Total flow = 8 L/min × 4 = 32 L/min Step 4 — decide: 32 L/min < 40 L/min → DOES NOT meet inspiratory demand. The patient will entrain extra room air and the actual FiO2 will drop below 0.40 and become variable. Step 5 — fix it. Raise the O2 input to 12 L/min (within the jet's 12–15 L/min back-pressure limit): Total flow = 12 × 4 = 48 L/min ≥ 40 L/min → MEETS demand. ✔ Note the FiO2 stays 0.40 — raising input flow changed total flow, not FiO2.
Worked example 3 — why high FiO2 entrainment devices failSame patient now needs 50%. O2 input maxed at 15 L/min. Does it work?
Ratio at 50% = 1.7:1 → total parts = 2.7 Total flow = 15 × 2.7 = 40.5 L/min → barely meets the 40 L/min bar At 60%: parts = 2 → 15 × 2 = 30 L/min → FAILS At 70%: parts = 1.6 → 15 × 1.6 = 24 L/min → FAILS badly Conclusion (Heuer, p291): above FiO2 0.60 at flows > 40 L/min, abandon entrainment and use a BLENDER-based system — a mechanical/manual blender (requires 50-psi air AND O2) or a heated high-flow nasal cannula. Alternative: run two air-entrainment nebulizers in parallel to double flow.

Troubleshooting an entrainment device whose SaO2 is lower than expected: inadequate total flow → increase input flow, correct obstructions/leaks; inadequate O2 concentration → increase FiO2 or switch to a higher-capability device; device displaced → reposition. For a nebulizer at high O2% settings (> 35–40%): maximize input flow, add an open reservoir to the expiratory side of the T-tube, connect multiple nebulizers in parallel, provide an inspiratory reservoir with a one-way expiratory valve, or set the nebulizer to a low O2% and bleed in extra O2.

Deck contributionNo deck in the 62-file corpus prints an entrainment ratio table — searches for "entrainment," "entrain," and "air:oxygen" return zero hits, so the table above is Heuer-only. The decks give the decision rule and the escalation: FiO2 > 0.40 → select an appropriate high-flow system; Venturi mask and HFNC are the named precise-FiO2 devices; a sample bronchiectasis plan orders O2 via nasal cannula at 2 L/min or venturi 28%; and the clinical escalation ladder is 2 L nasal cannula → Venturi mask → HFNC or BiPAP → intubation.
SourceHeuer Ch 6 — High-Flow Devices/Systems, Air-Entrainment Systems, Table 6-4, Table 6-5, Table 6-6, Blending Systems (PDF p289-296) · Heuer App B — Equipment Calculations, Table B-6, Problems B.27 and B.28 (PDF p1048-1049) · Heuer Ch 6 T4 Test-Taking Tips (PDF p368) · TDP/Communication deck, slides 19-20 · COPD deck, slide 28 · Bronchiectasis deck, slide 21 · RCP 105 Wk 5 deck, slide 31

8Non-Rebreather (NRB) Mask

Required flow

Minimum 10 L/min — and the operative definition of "enough" is whatever flow keeps the reservoir bag from collapsing. Heuer's ED scenario prescribes "nonrebreathing mask, > 10 L/min" for suspected MI, with the alternative of an HFNC at ≥ 20 L/min @ 100%.

FiO2 delivered

Heuer Table 6-1 gives 60–80%. It is classified as a low-flow/variable-performance reservoir device, not a true high-flow system — which is exactly why the FiO2 is a range and not a number.

Valve function

The NRB is the "valves present" case in Table 6-2 — valves present → less air dilution → higher FiO2, and valves absent → more dilution → lower FiO2. Heuer names the failure directly: an inspiratory valve that is jammed or reversed makes the bag stay fully inflated during inhalation and must be repaired or replaced. Functionally: a one-way valve between bag and mask lets reservoir gas in on inspiration but stops exhaled gas from entering the bag, and one-way flaps on the mask ports let exhaled gas out but block room air in.

Hess fills this (Hess, MacIntyre & Galvin, Respiratory Care: Principles and Practice, 4th ed. — not your 203 textbook, so treat it as supporting reference rather than the number your instructor grades from). Hess Ch 14 gives the valve anatomy: the NRB uses the same basic system as the partial rebreathing mask but incorporates valves BOTH between the bag and the mask AND on at least one of the exhalation side ports — and the diagnostic consequence, "Removing the valves converts the nonrebreathing mask to a partial rebreathing mask." Mechanically: a one-way valve prevents exhaled gas from entering the reservoir, so the entire exhaled volume exits through the mask ports and between face and mask; during exhalation the bag refills with O₂; and at the start of inspiration the mask exhalation-port valves close, minimising entrained room air, while the reservoir valve opens, letting the patient draw 300 to 500 mL of O₂ from the reservoir on top of the incoming flow.

Hess adds the honest caveat: "If the system were perfect, delivery of 100% O₂ would be possible" — but these inexpensive disposable masks cannot provide an airtight fit, and their valves are simple rubber or vinyl. ⚠ Original note: Heuer does not itemize the individual valves by name — that level of anatomical detail is not covered in Heuer or the available decks — but every troubleshooting consequence is.

Reading the bag — the whole exam question:

Bag behaviorCauseAction
Bag collapses when the patient inhalesInadequate flow, or increased minute ventilationIncrease the flow
Bag fails to remain inflatedInsufficient input flow; O2 tubing disconnect or obstructionIncrease flowmeter setting; check tubing is connected and unkinked
Bag remains FULLY inflated during inhalationToo much flowDecrease flow until the bag deflates slightly during inspiration
Same, but flow is rightLarge mask leakCorrect the leak
Same, but flow and seal are rightInspiratory valve jammed / reversedRepair or replace the mask

So: a collapsing bag means flow is too low — turn it up. A bag that never moves means flow is too high, the mask leaks, or a valve has failed.

Hazards and limits

Heuer lists potential suffocation hazard for both the partial rebreather and the non-rebreather, plus the usual mask problems (claustrophobia, must come off to eat or take oral meds). Two decision rules:

  • An NRB does not provide ventilation. If a physician orders an NRB for a "code blue" patient in respiratory arrest, immediately recommend a bag-valve-mask that can give 100% O2 and actually ventilate.
  • If switching from a simple mask to an NRB only moves SpO2 from 83% to 87%, the cause is inability to meet high inspiratory flow demand at high FiO2 or alveolar shunting — recommend an HFNC at 20–40 L/min titrated to SpO2, or consider mask CPAP with high FiO2.

Other NRB uses in Heuer: for spontaneously breathing patients, heliox is delivered via a tight-fitting nonrebreathing mask or a high-flow cannula; and a house-fire victim with an SpO2 of 98% on a cannula gets the highest possible FiO2 (usually an NRB) plus CO-oximetry, because SpO2 cannot detect carboxyhemoglobin. For comparison, the partial rebreathing mask runs at 6–10 L/min and delivers 40–70%.

Deck contributionThe airway deck uses the NRB for preoxygenation before intubation: 100% FiO2 via a tight-fitting non-rebreather mask (or BVM with PEEP) for at least 3–5 minutes, or until end-tidal O2 (ETO2) > 85–90% if monitored — the tight-fitting emphasis is the deck's one statement on application technique. The ABG deck shows the NRB as the escalation step in acute severe hypoxemia: a severe asthma exacerbation with pH 7.52, PaCO2 27 mm Hg, HCO3 22 mEq/L, PaO2 51 mm Hg was switched to a non-rebreather mask with aerosolized bronchodilator, after which PaO2 improved and the respiratory rate normalized.
SourceHeuer Ch 6 — Low-Flow Devices/Systems, Table 6-1, Table 6-2, Table 6-3, Table 6-7, Table 6-8 (PDF p287-291, p297-298), T4 Test-Taking Tips (PDF p372) · Heuer Ch 13 — Recommending Changes in Oxygen Therapy, Table 13-8 · RCP 105 Wk 1 Lctr 2 deck (Airway Management), slide 55 · ABG Assessments deck (RCP 103), slide 14

9Briggs Adaptor (T-Piece)

What it is

The T-piece, also called the Briggs adapter — a T-shaped connector that serves as one of the patient interfaces for a large-volume air-entrainment nebulizer (LVN). Heuer lists the LVN interfaces together: t-piece (Briggs adapter), trach mask, aerosol mask, or face tent.

What it connects to

Two of its three ports are fixed: one arm takes the inspiratory limb from the large-volume nebulizer / aerosol tubing, and the stem connects directly to the patient's artificial airway (ETT or tracheostomy tube). The third arm is the expiratory limb, and that is the one that matters on the exam.

Why reservoir tubing on the expiratory limb mattersBecause the LVN's total output is limited (see item 7), a patient with a high inspiratory demand can out-breathe the device. When that happens the patient draws room air backward through the open expiratory arm, diluting the delivered FiO2 and making it unpredictable. A length of corrugated reservoir tubing on the expiratory side acts as a reserve of source gas for the patient to pull from instead of room air. Heuer prescribes it explicitly:
  • The tell: "Aerosol mist disappears during inspiration (T-tube or mask)" → the causes are inadequate flow and increased patient inspiratory flow demands. Heuer's T4 tip states it as a rule: "If the aerosol mist disappears during inspiration in a patient receiving O2 via a large-volume nebulizer and T-piece or mask, the flow is inadequate."
  • The fixes, in Heuer's order: maximize input flow; add an open reservoir to the expiratory side of the T-tube; connect multiple nebulizers in parallel; provide an inspiratory reservoir with a one-way expiratory valve; set the nebulizer to a low O2% and bleed in extra O2.
  • The prescribing scenario: "A postoperative patient with an ETT requiring a moderate FIO2" → air-entrainment nebulizer at 40–50% O2 and a T-tube with an open reservoir. The reservoir is written into the order.

Other T-piece problems to know: inadequate mist output from an LVN → inadequate input flow (increase it), corrugated tube obstruction (drain condensate), jet orifice misalignment (repair/replace), or low water level (refill).

Deck contribution

The decks define the Briggs adaptor from the weaning side: a spontaneous breathing trial (SBT) can be run with a T-piece (Briggs adapter), with low-level pressure support (5–8 cm H2O to offset the ETT), with CPAP (~5 cm H2O), or with automatic tube compensation (ATC) — and all of these methods produce similar weaning outcomes. The T-piece SBT is the purest test because it gives no ventilator assistance at all: the patient performs most or all of the work of breathing, typically for 30 minutes, while being watched for fatigue or distress.

Heuer Ch 11 agrees, listing straight T-tube breathing first among SBT methods and specifying T-piece, trach collar, or CPAP ≤ 5 cm H2O with PS < 5 cm H2O.

SourceHeuer Ch 6 — Table 6-6 (PDF p296), Table 6-8 (PDF p298), Table 6-16 Nebulizer Types (PDF p314), Table 6-17 Troubleshooting Aerosol Delivery Systems (PDF p315), T4 Test-Taking Tips (PDF p371) · Heuer Ch 11 — Weaning / Spontaneous Breathing Trials · RCP 105 Wk 3 deck (Weaning/Extubation), slides 19-20

10Bourdon Gauge Regulator vs Thorpe Tube Flowmeter

Thorpe tube flowmeter

Heuer's use case: "To deliver a controlled flow of gas to a patient or equipment" with a piped source available → connect a calibrated Thorpe tube flowmeter to the piped gas source at 50 psi. It is the standard bedside metering device wherever there is wall gas. For pediatrics, the specific order is a nasal cannula at ¼–2 L/min with a calibrated low-flow flowmeter; in home care, because flows run 0.25–4.0 L/min, a low-flow metering device is likewise required.

Bourdon gauge regulator

Heuer's use case is defined by one property: when using cylinders for transport, use a flowmeter that is unaffected by gravity — either a Bourdon gauge or an integrated regulator/variable-orifice flow controller (e.g. Praxair Grab 'n Go, Western Medica Oxytote). This is the distinction being tested. A Thorpe tube is gravity-dependent — it works by floating a ball or bobbin in a vertical tapered tube, so it must be kept upright and will misread if tipped on a gurney.

A Bourdon gauge is not gravity-dependent, so it reads the same at any angle — which is why it goes on the transport tank.

How each works, and the back-pressure distinction — read this carefully

A Thorpe tube actually measures flow (a float rising in a tapered tube against a variable orifice). A Bourdon gauge is a pressure gauge with a scale printed in L/min: it measures the pressure upstream of a fixed orifice and infers flow from it. The consequence — that a Bourdon gauge over-reads whenever there is a downstream obstruction (kinked tubing, occluded cannula), because back pressure raises the measured pressure while actual flow falls, whereas a back-pressure-compensated Thorpe tube stays accurate downstream of a restriction — is the classic exam point, and it is Not covered in Heuer or the available decks. Heuer states only which device to use where and why (gravity), never the error behavior.

Hess fills this (Hess, MacIntyre & Galvin, Respiratory Care: Principles and Practice, 4th ed. — not your 203 textbook, so treat it as supporting reference rather than the number your instructor grades from). On the Bourdon gauge, Hess states the consequence outright: "With Bourdon gauge flow meters, the flow meter is accurate if the outlet flow is unrestricted. However, when resistance is added downstream of the fixed outlet orifice — such as with the addition of a long length of tubing and respiratory equipment — the outlet pressure (P2) rises and the actual outlet flow decreases. In fact, if the Bourdon gauge outlet became completely obstructed, the gauge continues to show flow even though none is present." That last clause is the examinable one: a fully obstructed Bourdon gauge still reads a flow. Hess draws the practical rule from it — pneumatic nebulisers present a large downstream resistance and should not be used with a Bourdon gauge.

On the Thorpe tube, Hess gives the contrast cleanly: "Unlike flow resistors and Bourdon gauges, a pressure-compensated Thorpe tube flow meter displays the actual outlet flow regardless of downstream resistance" — provided the inlet pressure remains constant. That is why these are the most prevalent dosage-regulation device in hospitals for piped outlets. Construction: a clear tapered glass tube, wider at the top than the bottom, with graduated markings and a float, plus a needle valve.

("Rotameter" implies a rotating bobbin rather than a spherical float, and is used where anaesthetic gases need greater accuracy.) Hess's note on the Bourdon's one advantage stands too: it can be read correctly without being held vertical, which is why it travels.

Gap⚠ Original note: the bench distinction between a back-pressure-compensated and an uncompensated Thorpe tube (in a compensated tube the float jumps and returns to zero when the flowmeter is pressurized before the valve is opened) is Not covered in Heuer or the available decks. Look these up in Egan's Fundamentals of Respiratory Care, "Storage and Delivery of Medical Gases."

Where each sits in the gas train (Heuer Table 6-9):

PurposeSettingEquipment
50-psi unrestricted flow to ventilators or blendersPiped source availableNone — connect directly to the piped source at 50 psi using a DISS connection
SameNo piped sourceLarge cylinder (H or K) with a preset (50-psi) pressure-reducing valve; for air, a piston air compressor with reservoir
Controlled flow to a patient or equipmentPiped source availableCalibrated Thorpe tube flowmeter on the piped source at 50 psi
SameNo piped sourceGas cylinder with a reducing valve and flowmeter, cylinder size chosen by portability need and planned duration; for air, a portable diaphragm compressor (limited flow/pressure)

Checking output accuracy — what Heuer does give you:

  • Verify with a second instrument, not the dial. For blenders: "Verify the prescribed O2% using an O2 analyzer." If the blender setting and the measured O2% disagree substantially, check both source pressures (usually ≥ 35 psig), check for leaks between source and blender, and if both check out, replace the blender.
  • Gold standard for flow calibration (Heuer Ch 8): a computer-controlled air pump generating standard expiratory waveforms, with a recommended accuracy standard of ± 5% of the reading or ± 0.2 L/sec, whichever is greater. The general QC principle Heuer applies to every measuring device is two-point calibration before each use.
  • Gas-specific correction — an accuracy trap you must know. A flowmeter is calibrated for one gas. If heliox is delivered through an O2 flowmeter, apply a correction factor: 1.8 for 80/20 He/O2, 1.6 for 70/30, 1.4 for 60/40. Worked: for every 10 L/min indicated on an O2 flowmeter delivering 80% He / 20% O2, the actual flow is 10 × 1.8 = 18 L/min. The alternative is to use a device calibrated for that He%.
  • Air compressor output inadequate? Check the inlet filter for obstruction, then the tubing and connected equipment for obstruction, then tubing and connections for leaks. If a portable compressor won't start, check the outlet for power and the fuse or circuit breaker.
Deck contributionNeither "Bourdon" nor "Thorpe" appears anywhere in the 62 decks. The decks' only related content is the ventilator pneumatic source pressure alarm — loss of the air or O2 source below 50 psig triggers electronic or pneumatic reed alarms (Heuer Ch 11 Table 11-9 lists the same causes: gas lines not connected to the high-pressure 50-psi outlets, or gas lines connected to the low-pressure outlet, e.g. flowmeters) — plus the general habit the question is testing: calibrate the device per the manufacturer's instructions before use, and confirm the analyzer was properly calibrated prior to running the sample.
SourceHeuer Ch 6 — Gas Delivery, Metering, and Clinical Analyzing Devices, Table 6-9, Blending Systems, Air Compressors, Table 6-8 (PDF p292, p298-301), Heliox delivery (PDF p345), T4 Test-Taking Tips (PDF p372) · Heuer Ch 8 — Flow Calibration (PDF p415) · Heuer Ch 11 — Table 11-9 Clinical Alarms · RCP 105 Wk 1 Lctr 3 deck (Principles of Mech Vent), slide 93 · RCP 203 Lssn 2b deck, slides 10, 12, 29
03

Aerosol Drug Delivery, Lung Expansion & Airway Clearance

5 items · MDI education, spacers and valved holding chambers · aerosol delivery modalities · incentive spirometry · PEP · cromolyn sodium

1Educating a Patient to Use an MDI; Spacers and Valved Holding Chambers2Aerosol Delivery Modalities Compared3Incentive Spirometry: Indication, Mechanism, Coaching, Frequency, Goal, Troubleshooting4PEP Therapy (Positive Expiratory Pressure) — Including Vibratory PEP5Cromolyn Sodium

1Educating a Patient to Use an MDI; Spacers and Valved Holding Chambers

The teaching sequence (Heuer)

(1) Shake the canister, and prime it — waste a dose into the air — if it is new or has not been used for a few days; warm a cold canister in the hands or it will not function properly. (2) Uncap and hold upright. Position it either open-mouth — MDI held 2 inches (4 cm, about 2 finger widths) from the open mouth with the tongue down, which enhances lung deposition — or closed-mouth, lips sealed on the mouthpiece with the tongue down.

Use closed-mouth only if (a) the patient cannot do open-mouth, (b) no spacer is available, and (c) the drug is not a steroid. (3) Exhale normally. (4) Actuate at the start of a slow, deep inhalation lasting 3 to 5 seconds — not before it, not after it. (5) Hold the breath for 10 seconds to allow maximum drug deposition. (6) Exhale slowly.

(7) Wait about 1 minute between puffs. (8) Rinse the mouth with water and spit — essential with steroids to prevent pharyngitis and oral candidiasis and to reduce the drug swallowed and systemically absorbed. (9) Confirm learning with a return demonstration / teach-back ("Show me how you're going to use your inhaler at home").

Why a spacer or VHC gets added
