The classic pMDI failure is timing: actuating too late after inspiration has begun means the drug is not inhaled deeply; actuating too soon, before inspiration, dumps it in the mouth. A spacer or valved holding chamber creates space between the MDI and the mouth so the propellant can dry and the larger particles fall out of the stream — less pharyngeal deposition, less coordination required, and therefore less thrush with steroids.

Heuer's rule: any adult who is not acutely ill and cannot coordinate actuation with breathing should get a holding chamber (a breath-actuated MDI is the alternative), most infants and small children should get an MDI plus holding chamber and mask, and anyone prescribed steroids by MDI should use a spacer or holding chamber. Cost is the trade-off: more expensive, bulkier, and more complex for some patients than a bare MDI.

Technique with the chamber attached

Slow deep inhalation (about 30 L/min, or 3 to 5 seconds), followed immediately by a 10-second breath-hold. Actuate only ONCE into the chamber per inhalation — never stack puffs into the chamber. If a face mask is used it must fit snugly and the patient takes 3 to 5 breaths per actuation; note the mask cuts lung deposition by 50%, so wean to a mouthpiece as soon as the child can use one.

Rinse plastic VHCs once a month in water with dilute dishwashing detergent and let them air-dry — hand-drying builds an electrostatic charge that pulls the drug onto the chamber walls; antistatic VHCs avoid the problem.

Plain spacer vs valved holding chamber — the exam distinction

A plain spacer is just an open tube or chamber added between the canister and the mouth: it removes big particles and dries the propellant, but it has no valve, so it still requires coordinated actuation and the patient can blow the dose back out. A valved holding chamber adds a one-way inspiratory valve that holds the aerosol cloud in suspension until the patient inhales and stops the patient from exhaling back into the chamber, so it gives a bit more deposition than a spacer.

Heuer's bottom line: VHCs are preferred. Two device cautions: breath-actuated MDIs and the Respimat slow-mist inhaler CANNOT be used with a spacer or VHC, and DPIs must never be used with one either.

Deck numbers to pair with this: a CFC MDI alone deposits 8.8% of the dose in the lung versus 14.8% with a spacer, and HFA reaches 53%; the pMDI is the method of choice for infants and children under 5 years old ONLY when used with a reservoir device (spacer or VHC); and the four ways to raise the lung-to-total-body (L/T) ratio are an efficient device, rinse-and-spit, a reservoir device, and proper instruction.

SourceHeuer Ch 6 — Metered-Dose Inhalers, Spacers, Valved Holding Chambers, and DPIs / Table 6-19 (PDF p317-318) and Heuer Ch 12 — Table 12-3, Optimal Technique (PDF p596-597) · Chptr 3 deck (Admn of Aerosolized Agents), slides 20, 34, 36, 38-39, 42, 51; Chptr 11 deck (Corticosteroids), slide 39 ("rinse mouth after use")

Particle size and where it lands

An aerosol is a suspension of liquid or solid particles from 0.001 to 100 μm in a carrier gas. The single number that matters is the MMAD (mass median aerodynamic diameter) — the particle diameter above and below which 50% of the aerosol mass lies — reported with a GSD (geometric standard deviation), the ratio of the size below which 84% of particles fall to the size below which 50% fall, which tells you how tight or scattered the distribution is.

Real aerosols are heterodisperse. The fine particle fraction (FPF) — the mass with a high probability of reaching the lower airway — is defined as under 5 to 6 μm. Heuer states the respirable range plainly: an SVN delivers 1 to 5 micron aerosol, and input flow of 6 to 8 L/min is what produces it.

Deposition ranges to memorize (decks; Heuer gives only the 1-5 μm figure)

the range of clinical interest is 1 to 10 μm. Over 10 μm — deposits in the upper airway, nose and mouth. 5 to 10 μm — upper airway and roughly the first six airway generations. 2 to 5 μm — the bronchoactive range, the target for bronchodilators, depositing through the lower respiratory tract (a typical SVN runs about 2.4 μm). 0.8 to 3 μm — lung parenchyma, terminal airways and alveolar region.

1 to 2 μm — the MMAD recommended for pentamidine, to maximize alveolar deposition and minimize large-airway irritation. Under 1 μm particles are so small they are largely exhaled again.

Three deposition mechanisms

Inertial impaction — a function of particle mass and velocity; happens where flow is fastest and most turbulent and cross-sectional area is smallest, i.e., the first 10 airway generations and the oropharynx. This is exactly what a spacer, a slow inhalation, and holding the MDI 2 inches from the mouth are designed to defeat. Gravitational settling (sedimentation) — a function of particle size and time, with settling rate proportional to the square of particle size; this is why the 10-second breath-hold works.

Diffusion (Brownian motion) — dominates for particles under 1 μm, and usually takes longer than an inspiration lasts, so those particles are breathed back out. Aerosols are also hygroscopic: going from dry ambient air to BTPS at 37 °C, cromolyn's MMAD grows from 2.31 to 3.02 μm. Traditional devices put only about 10% to 15% of the total dose in the airway; newer devices reach 30% to 50%. Size is measured by cascade impaction.

SourceHeuer Ch 12 — Aerosol Drug Delivery Systems, Tables 12-2 and 12-3 (PDF p592-599) and Heuer Ch 6 — Nebulizers, Table 6-16, and Tables 6-17/6-18/6-19 (PDF p313-318) · Chptr 3 deck (Admn of Aerosolized Agents), slides 4-11, 15, 17-26, 30-36, 38-43, 45-47, 51; Chptr 13 deck (Aerosolized Anti-Infective Agents), slides 29-30, 32 (SPAG/ribavirin)

3Incentive Spirometry: Indication, Mechanism, Coaching, Frequency, Goal, Troubleshooting

What it is

IS uses a simple disposable indicator device to coach the patient into slow, deep breaths with a breath-hold — a sustained maximal inspiration (SMI). The SMI is the whole mechanism: a slow inspiration to near total lung capacity held long enough to raise transpulmonary pressure, which re-expands collapsed alveoli, recruits low-compliance lung units, and restores FRC.

Indication

Prevention and treatment of atelectasis in a patient who is awake, cooperative, and able to breathe deeply — classically after upper abdominal or thoracic surgery, and in prolonged bed rest, restrictive disease and neuromuscular conditions. Note Heuer's current-evidence caveat: IS provides little to no benefit after thoracic or upper abdominal surgery and is NO LONGER recommended for routine prophylactic use in postoperative patients — early mobilization and ambulation are now the primary means of preventing postoperative pulmonary complications. IS remains appropriate for treating documented or suspected atelectasis in a cooperative patient.

Patient selection

The patient must be able to cooperate and ideally generate an inspiratory capacity at least one-third (33%) of predicted. If the patient cannot cooperate, or cannot generate an IC greater than 33% of predicted, recommend IPPB or intermittent CPAP instead.

Coaching and technique

Teach it preoperatively whenever the therapy is planned around surgery — the pre-op volume also sets the post-op target. Seat the patient upright, splint the incision, and coordinate the session with pain medication, since a patient who hurts will not inspire deeply. Instruct: seal the lips on the mouthpiece, inhale slowly and as fully as possible to full inspiratory capacity, then hold for 5 to 10 seconds, exhale, and allow adequate recovery time between breaths to avoid hyperventilation and dizziness.

Frequency: 6 to 10 sustained maximal inspirations per hour while awake (the Atelectasis deck teaches approximately 10 breaths per session, hourly while awake). Always finish with a return demonstration to confirm understanding, then assist with coughing and observe for sputum. Document the volume achieved, plus date and time.

Goal volume and endpoint

Coach the patient to keep increasing the volume goal each session. The deck's practical target is at least one-half (½) of the patient's pre-op inspiratory capacity. Heuer's discontinuation criterion is volumes greater than 60% to 70% of the predicted IC together with clinical resolution of atelectasis — improved breath sounds, improved oxygenation, normalized respiratory rate. Assess breath sounds, vital signs and SpO2 before and after therapy.

Troubleshooting

Pain is the primary hazard and the most common reason for failure — medicate and splint. Make sure the patient INHALES through the device, not exhales into it (blowing into the indicator is the classic error). If the volume target is not met, check all connections and the lip seal for leaks. If the readout is unreliable or you need a true number, attach a one-way breathing valve and a respirometer to the IS device to measure the volume accurately.

Dizziness or tingling means the breaths are too frequent — lengthen the recovery pause. Retained secretions alongside atelectasis means IS alone is not enough — add airway clearance therapy. If the patient simply cannot perform the maneuver, escalate per the Lung Expansion Therapy Protocol to IPPB or CPAP (the decks note IPPB has been removed from NBRC exams, with IPV and PEP/CPAP as the modern alternatives).

SourceHeuer Ch 10 — Hyperinflation Therapy: Incentive Spirometry and Box 10-5 (PDF p509-510); Heuer Ch 6 — Hyperinflation Devices (PDF p345) · Atelectasis deck (Respiratory Care Treatment Protocols), slides 16-17, 19, 22-23; The Therapist-Driven Protocol Program and Communication deck, slide 22

4PEP Therapy (Positive Expiratory Pressure) — Including Vibratory PEP

How it works

The patient inhales freely (inspiration is unrestricted) and then exhales actively — but not forcibly — against a fixed or adjustable resistance at the mouth. That back-pressure does two things: it splints the unstable airways open during exhalation, preventing the dynamic compression and premature airway closure that traps gas behind mucus plugs, and it lets gas move through collateral channels (pores of Kohn, canals of Lambert) to get behind retained secretions and push them proximally toward the large airways where a cough or huff can clear them.

The deck's image is the right one: "PEP devices create pressure behind the mucus, helping push it upward so the patient can cough it out."

The pressure range — the number they will ask. PEP devices typically generate 6 to 25 cm H2O during exhalation (Heuer's device-class range). In practice you START at the LOWEST setting — the largest orifice, the lowest spring tension or flow — and then slowly increase the pressure to a therapeutic 10 to 20 cm H2O per the manufacturer's recommendation. Memorize 10 to 20 cm H2O as the working range and 6 to 25 cm H2O as the device capability. For the Flutter valve specifically, the weight of the steel ball creates expiratory pressures of 10 to 25 cm H2O.

Procedure

Sit upright or semi-Fowler's with the abdomen unrestricted. Set the lowest PEP, then titrate up into 10 to 20 cm H2O. Have the patient perform sets of 10 to 20 slow, moderately deep inspirations with a short breath-hold followed by active — not forced — exhalation, at an I:E ratio of 1:3 to 1:4. After each cycle of 10 to 20 breaths, have the patient cough or do the forced expiratory technique (huff cough) — the FET being two or three huffs with the glottis open followed by rest. Repeat the whole cycle 4 to 8 times, not exceeding a 20-minute session.

Indications

Secretion retention and mucus plugging: cystic fibrosis, bronchiectasis, COPD exacerbation, asthma with mucus plugging. PEP is also used to prevent or treat atelectasis and to reduce air trapping in asthma and COPD, and, with a nebulizer attached, several PEP devices can deliver a bronchodilator at the same time (the exceptions are the Flutter valve and the Threshold PEP device, which have no nebulizer adaptor).

Heuer's honest framing: PEP is no more effective than other airway clearance methods, so pick it on patient need and preference — but if the goal is mobilizing retained secretions, a vibratory PEP device is probably the best choice.

Contraindications

Do not use PEP in a patient who cannot tolerate any increased work of breathing, or who is hemodynamically unstable, or who has bullous emphysema, elevated ICP, untreated pneumothorax, sinusitis, epistaxis, or middle-ear problems.

PEP is NOT PEEP

PEP is patient-generated expiratory resistance applied at the mouth, during exhalation only, in a spontaneously breathing patient, delivered by a small handheld device, and it returns to zero at end-expiration — its purpose is airway clearance and lung expansion. PEEP is machine-generated baseline airway pressure held by a ventilator at END-EXPIRATION in a mechanically ventilated patient, it never returns to zero, and its purpose is alveolar recruitment and improved oxygenation (raising FRC and reducing shunt). Different pressure source, different phase, different patient, different goal. (Also distinct is CPAP, continuous positive pressure on both inspiration and expiration.)

Vibratory / oscillatory PEP

These add airflow oscillation on top of the back-pressure, which shears mucus loose from the airway wall and can shift its rheology. Heuer's device table:

  • Flow resistor — the patient exhales against a fixed orifice sized to age and expiratory flow (e.g., TheraPEP).
  • Threshold resistor — the patient exhales against an adjustable counterweight, spring-loaded valve, or reverse Venturi (e.g., Threshold PEP, EZPap, which also gives positive pressure on inspiration).
  • Vibratory PEP — the patient exhales against a threshold resistor with an expiratory valve oscillating at 10 to 30 Hz (e.g., Flutter valve, Acapella, Aerobika, Quake).

The Flutter is the pipe-shaped device containing a steel ball resting on a cone-shaped orifice under a perforated cap; exhaled gas lifts the ball, pressure drops, the ball falls, and the cycle repeats — generating 10 to 25 cm H2O with oscillation. Because it is gravity-dependent, Flutter PEP rises when the device is angled above horizontal and with higher expiratory flow — so the patient must hold it correctly. The Acapella uses a counterweighted lever and magnet; a plug on the lever intermittently blocks flow to create the oscillations, and PEP is adjusted by turning the knob at the distal end — clockwise to increase.

The Acapella is NOT gravity-dependent, so it works in any position (and unlike the Flutter it accepts a nebulizer adaptor) — that positional independence is the exam's favorite Flutter-vs-Acapella discriminator.

Coaching and troubleshooting

With vibratory PEP, you should feel the vibrations over the central airways during exhalation — if you cannot, the settings are wrong. If a PEP device generates no pressure, the cause is almost always a LEAK — tighten every connection (and check the mouth seal). Unexpectedly HIGH pressure means the outlet port is obstructed — typically by the patient's hand or the bedding; keep the port clear. Home patients clean the device in warm soapy water, rinse, and air-dry completely.

SourceHeuer Ch 10 — Vibratory Positive Expiratory Pressure (PEP) Devices, Table 10-4 and Figure 10-7 (PDF p500-503); Heuer Ch 10 — Forced Expiratory Technique (PDF p508-509) · Chptr 9 deck (Mucus Controlling Agents), slides 55, 59, 62-64; The Therapist-Driven Protocol Program and Communication deck, slides 21-22; Atelectasis deck, slide 17; Bronchiectasis deck, slide 20

5Cromolyn Sodium

Class

Mast cell stabilizer — a nonsteroidal anti-asthma (anti-inflammatory) agent, brand name Intal. In the deck's three-part taxonomy of nonsteroidal anti-asthma drugs — (1) mast cell stabilizers, (2) antileukotrienes, (3) monoclonal antibodies — cromolyn is the sole member of group 1. Its sibling is nedocromil (Tilade).

MechanismIt stabilizes the mast cell membrane and prevents degranulation, blocking release of the chemical mediators of inflammation (histamine, leukotrienes and the rest). In plain terms: it stops the mast cell from dumping its chemicals, interrupting the allergen → IgE → mast cell → mediator release → bronchoconstriction, mucosal edema, mucus cascade before it starts. It has no bronchodilator activity whatsoever — it does not relax airway smooth muscle.

Use

Maintenance and control of asthma — a controller, listed by Heuer alongside inhaled corticosteroids, LABA+ICS combinations and leukotriene inhibitors as maintenance medication for asthma. Its niche is prophylaxis in very young children: Heuer's clinical-scenario table pairs "prophylactic asthma management in patients age 2 years or younger" with montelukast (Singulair) or cromolyn sodium (Intal). The decks add that it is used as an alternative to inhaled corticosteroids at step 2 care and chosen for its safety profile — less potent than a steroid, but safer in a small child. It is also used before a known trigger such as exercise or allergen exposure.

Dose (Heuer, Table 12-1)

MDI: 1 mg per puff, 2 to 4 puffs 4 times daily. SVN: 20 mg per 2 mL ampule (1% solution), 1 ampule 4 times daily. For comparison, nedocromil (Tilade): MDI 1.75 mg per puff, 2 to 4 puffs 2 times daily.

PROPHYLACTIC ONLY — NOT A RESCUE DRUG

This is the single most testable fact about cromolyn. It is a controller/preventive agent taken on a fixed schedule; it must NOT be used to treat acute bronchospasm or an asthma attack. It cannot reverse bronchoconstriction that has already happened, and giving it during an exacerbation wastes time the patient does not have. In an acute attack the answer is a SABA (albuterol), never cromolyn.

Onset

It is not immediate. Expect 2 to 4 weeks of regular dosing before symptom improvement — so the patient must be told to keep taking it even though nothing seems to be happening, and not to abandon it when it fails to relieve an attack.

Side effects

Mild, local, and annoying rather than dangerous — this is the whole reason it is used in toddlers. Cough, bronchospasm/wheezing on inhalation, throat irritation and unpleasant taste, nasal congestion, sneezing, nasal itching, nose burning, and epistaxis. Systemic toxicity is minimal because it is poorly absorbed.

Caveats the decks stress

Several studies found it not effective in the treatment of asthma, and the Global Initiative for Asthma (GINA) does NOT include cromolyn among its recommended asthma agents — so in current practice it is a weak, preventive-only, largely superseded drug. Two curiosities worth knowing: it can suppress the cough associated with ACE inhibitors, and it has anti-sickling effects (hypothesized to act via blockade of calcium-activated potassium channels). Finally, cromolyn is the decks' textbook example of a hygroscopic aerosol: its MMAD grows from 2.31 to 3.02 μm going from dry ambient air to BTPS.

SourceHeuer Ch 12 — Table 12-1, Mast Cell Stabilizers (PDF p587-590) and Table 12-3, MDI/SVN technique for cromolyn (PDF p596-599); Heuer — asthma maintenance/controller table and the "prophylactic asthma management in patients age 2 years or younger" clinical-scenario row (recommend-therapy chapter; page marker not present in the extracted text) · Chptr 12 deck (NonSteroidal AntiAsthma Agents), slides 3-4, 6-8, 21-22; Chptr 3 deck, slide 22 (hygroscopic growth)
04

Quality Control, Calibration & Blood Gas Sampling

6 items · Levy-Jennings · point-of-care analyzers · QC on pulse oximeters and O2 analyzers · ATS spirometry calibration and the 3-L syringe · ABG sample preparation · bench hemoximeter

1Levy-Jennings Chart — Mean, ±2 SD, and Trend vs. Shift2Point-of-Care (POC) Analyzers3QC on a Pulse Oximeter and on an Oxygen Analyzer4Spirometry Calibration to ATS Standards5Preparing an ABG Sample for Analysis6Bench Hemoximeter / CO-Oximeter

1Levy-Jennings Chart — Mean, ±2 SD, and Trend vs. Shift

A Levy-Jennings chart (Heuer's spelling; the RCP 103 deck spells it "Levey-Jennings" — same chart) is the standard plotting format for statistical QC. Heuer's Table 8-1 defines it under control charts: "a record of the measurements of standard samples used for statistical evaluation of analyzer performance."

What it plots

The individual measured values of a control medium over successive runs/days (x-axis = run number or date), compared against the mean of those measurements and the standard deviation (SD) of the data. Build a separate chart for each level of control AND for each analyte you report — so a blood gas lab keeps separate charts for pH, PCO2, and PO2 at each control level.

The control limits

Bounding limits are typically set at ±2 SD around the mean. Control values that consistently fall inside ±2 SD mean the analyzer is "in control" and ready to run patient samples. A control value outside ±2 SD indicates an analytical error. Heuer's Figure 8-2 example is a PCO2 control standardized to 40 torr with all points inside ±2 SD.

How QC is actually run

Blood gas analyzers get frequent two-point calibration (usually automated) of pH, PCO2, and PO2 using precision gases and buffer solutions. On top of that they need calibration verification with at least 3 different levels of control media spanning the reportable range, at least daily, plus after any maintenance and whenever performance is questioned. The gold standard for verifying PO2 and PCO2 is tonometry — equilibrating a liquid medium to a reference gas of known partial pressures.

The RCP 103 deck describes the same thing: control solutions at three levels — acidotic, alkalotic, normal, supplied in ampules of about 2–3 mL, judged against ± 2 SD.

Random vs. Systematic (Bias) Error

Random errorSystematic error (bias)
Chart appearanceOne isolated point jumps outside the limits (Heuer's Fig. 8-3: day 16, almost 3 SD from the mean), then the next points come back inValues move as a group away from the mean and stay there — Heuer's Fig. 8-4 shows an upward trend starting at observation 20 that pulls the mean above the 40-torr control value
What it meansNormal statistical scatter / a one-off contamination or handling problem. If subsequent measures are in control, no remedial action is neededBias — more serious than random error. Means incorrect procedure or instrument component failure
When it becomes fatalFrequent random points scattered above and below ±2 SD = poor precision/repeatability → the instrument is "out of control"Always. No patient samples may be analyzed until the problem is corrected
Fix (Heuer Table 8-3)Reanalyze the control after a two-point calibration; if the repeat QC is in control, stop there; if still out, follow manufacturer's corrective actionsTroubleshoot; repair or replace faulty components (contaminated buffers, wrong gas concentrations, degraded components). Continue until you get an acceptable two-point calibration and control values within ±2 SD of the mean

Trend vs. Shift

TrendShift
Pattern on the chartControl values drift progressively in one direction (up or down) over successive runs — a sloping lineControl values jump abruptly to a new level and then run flat at that new level
What it implies about the analyzerGradual deterioration — an aging/failing electrode, a slowly degrading component, reagent or buffer deteriorating over time. Heuer: an upward or downward trend outside the ±2 SD limits is a bias errorAn abrupt change — new reagent/buffer lot, a recalibration, a replaced electrode or membrane, a new gas cylinder. Also a bias error, just with a sudden onset
ActionBoth are bias/systematic errors: stop patient testing, troubleshoot, repair or replace the faulty component, recalibrate, and confirm controls are back within ±2 SD before resuming

Honest caveat on wording: Heuer describes and illustrates the trend explicitly ("an upward trend in the reported values … over time, this trend shifts the mean above the control value, causing a positive measurement bias") but never uses "shift" as a named QC pattern, and neither do the decks. The shift row above is the standard companion concept — know it, but expect Heuer-based test items to call both of them bias errors.

Westgard rules: the word Westgard does not appear anywhere in Heuer (all chapters checked). It comes only from the lecture: the RCP 103 deck says QC errors are evaluated "using Westgard Rules" to tell a single random error from a systematic problem needing intervention, and gives the practical version — one control outside the SD = a warning; two or more consecutive controls outside the acceptable range = an "out-of-control" situation, which requires the electrode to be recalibrated or replaced. Heuer's equivalent framing is the random-vs-bias table above. If a question quotes "Westgard," it is coming from the deck.

Other QC layers both sources name: proficiency testing — an outside agency sends "blind"/unknown samples on a schedule, the lab reports results back, and the agency compares them to its own and to peer labs using similar instruments. Discrepancies must be resolved for the lab to keep its certification, which is a prerequisite for Medicare/Medicaid reimbursement. The deck adds multiple-machine comparison and gas exchange validation (tonometry).

SourceHeuer Ch 8 — Key Terms/Table 8-1, Laboratory Blood Gas and Hemoximetry Analyzers, Review of QC and Proficiency Testing Results, Table 8-3, T4 Tips (PDF p401-410, 423-424) · ABG Assessments PP (RCP 103), slides 65-68

2Point-of-Care (POC) Analyzers

Definition

Point-of-care testing (POCT) is "the collection, measurement, and reporting of selected lab tests at or near the site of patient care" — i.e., at the bedside instead of sending the specimen to the central lab. Most POCT instruments use disposable cartridges designed for each test panel, and calibration occurs with each cartridge test, so minimal user intervention is required. The most common RT-performed POCT is ABGs, alone or as a combined panel with electrolytes, hemoglobin, glucose, lactate, and BUN.

ExampleHeuer's named device is the Abbott Laboratories I-STAT. Its procedure: confirm the analyzer's power-on-self-test (POST), select the cartridge/panel, scan operator and patient IDs, avoid touching the cartridge contact pads, obtain the sample and analyze it within 3 minutes — do NOT place it on ice, mix thoroughly, fill the cartridge well to the fill mark, insert, and enter sample type, FiO2, and patient temperature. The RCP 103 deck names the same device and states I-Stat returns ABG results within about 90 seconds and can also run other lab tests.

Advantages.

  • Speed — results in ~90 seconds (deck) versus transport-plus-run time to the central lab.
  • Bedside availability for patients in urgent need of assessment; valuable in critical care and emergency settings where rapid decision-making is essential and during interfacility transport (deck).
  • Self-calibrating cartridges — calibration happens automatically with every test, so there is little routine calibration burden.
  • Heuer calls POCT analyzers "highly reliable devices."

Disadvantages / limitations (the QC burden Heuer attaches to them).

  • Operator competency must be formally documented — personnel performing POCT must have documented competency with the procedure and the equipment.
  • Every result must be auditable/traceable to the patient, the instrument, the operator, the date and time, and the process used, and complete instrument maintenance records must be kept.
  • Failed calibration wastes a cartridge and can cost you the analyzer: if a POCT device fails to calibrate, rerun with a new cartridge; if a second attempt fails, remove the device from service and either get a replacement analyzer or send the sample to the central lab.
  • It cannot stand alone. Most facilities require POCT results to be regularly compared against a calibrated bench-top analyzer by running the same sample on both instruments simultaneously — "inter-instrumental comparison." Heuer's T4 tip: this is the best way to validate POCT instrument measurements. Most facilities also enroll POCT in proficiency testing.
  • Critical-range results need repeating. You may get a POCT value outside the device's critical range (e.g., pH < 7.20 or > 7.60). That is not the same as a flagged or out-of-reportable-range result — treat it as potentially valid data and repeat with a fresh sample and a new cartridge; some protocols require the repeat be done in the central lab. But never wait for a repeat if the findings are life-threatening or match a deteriorating patient — e.g., a POCT PO2 < 40 torr in a patient with signs of severe hypoxemia means raise the FiO2 now and confirm later.
  • Very short analysis window — the I-STAT sample must be run within 3 minutes and must not be iced, so there is no storage cushion.
SourceHeuer Ch 8 — Point-of-Care Analyzers, T4 Tips (PDF p410-411, 424) · Heuer Ch 6 — Blood Analyzers/POCT Analyzers (PDF p335-336) · Heuer Ch 3 — Blood Gas Analysis/Hemoximetry, Box 3-3 (PDF p151-152) · ABG Assessments PP (RCP 103), slide 68 · RCP 203 Lssn 2b (Chp 3), slide 29

3QC on a Pulse Oximeter and on an Oxygen Analyzer

Pulse Oximeter

Why there is no "calibration."Pulse oximeters measure relative light intensities, not absolute values, so they do not require "true value" calibration the way a blood gas analyzer does. Instead you perform an operational verification using a healthy person as a "biological control" — Heuer's four steps:
  1. Connect the device to a healthy person.
  2. Compare the displayed pulse rate with the pulse measured manually.
  3. Confirm an SpO2 reading of 97–100%.
  4. Confirm loss-of-signal detection by removing the device.

Failure criteria — take the oximeter out of service if you get an incorrect pulse rate, an SpO2 below 97% on a healthy subject, or failure to detect loss of signal.

Stated accuracy tolerance

Pulse oximeters give relatively accurate (±2–4%) estimates of oxyhemoglobin saturation when perfusion is good. (RCP 203 Lssn 3a quotes the identical ± 2–4% figure.) Note also that oximeters give few useful data once PaO2 rises above 100 torr (hyperoxia), and that SpO2 is normally > 93–95% on room air.

What makes readings unreliable (and which direction the error goes).

FactorEffect on SpO2
HbCO (smoke inhalation / CO poisoning)Falsely HIGH %HbO2 — standard 2-wavelength oximeters read CO-saturated Hb as oxygenated. Do not use standard pulse oximetry; use a CO-oximeter
High metHbFalsely LOW %HbO2 if SaO2 > 85%; falsely HIGH %HbO2 if SaO2 < 85%
Vascular dyes (e.g., methylene blue)Falsely LOW %HbO2 — correlate with an ABG SaO2 before trusting the value
Dark skin pigmentation and nail polishFalsely HIGH %HbO2 (by 3–5%) — remove polish/artificial nails/jewelry, or rotate the probe 90 degrees in an emergency
Ambient lightVaries; falsely high %HbO2 in sunlight, and may also give a falsely high pulse reading — shield the probe
Poor perfusion / vasoconstrictionInadequate signal, unpredictable results — move to a less peripheral site (ear, nose, forehead) or use an ABG
Motion artifactThe most common source of errors and false alarms. Relocate the sensor; Masimo SET technology helps
Probe fitted too tightlyVenous pooling/pulsation → falsely LOW SpO2

Practical QC at the bedside (deck): verify a good pulse signal/waveform and compare the displayed pulse rate with the patient's actual pulse or ECG rate — "before you believe the SpO2, make sure the pulse oximeter is actually seeing the patient's pulse." If the SpO2 looks wrong: (1) check the pulse rate against the cardiac monitor, (2) confirm the sensor is shielded from bright light, (3) confirm the sensor is not fitted too tightly.

And when the SpO2 disagrees with a blood gas report, remember the ABG SaO2 is usually a computed, not measured, value — compare the SpO2 to a hemoximeter's SaO2 instead, and use the SaO2 − SpO2 difference to "calibrate" the oximeter.

Oxygen Analyzer

Technology

Most O2 analyzers use either a Clark electrode (polarographic) or a galvanic fuel cell to measure PO2. Polarographic analyzers require external power to maintain the electrode reaction; galvanic cells do not — but both need external power for alarm functions.

Stated accuracy. Both types are generally accurate to within ±2% of the actual concentration.

Two-point calibration — the calibration points are 100% O2 and 21% room air. Heuer's four steps:

  1. Expose the sensor to a source of 100% O2.
  2. After the reading stabilizes, adjust the analyzer to 100%.
  3. Remove the sensor from the 100% O2 source.
  4. After restabilization, expose the analyzer to room air and confirm a reading of 21% (±2%).

Failure

A failure to reach either 100% or 21% may indicate a damaged probe or a defective analyzer. Heuer's T4 tip is blunt: if an O2 analyzer fails a two-point calibration, replace the probe. Common specific causes (Table 8-4): probe not exposed to room air / to 100% O2, bad probe (electrode or cell), water condensation on the probe membrane (a named T4 tip — condensate causes inaccurate measurements; dry the membrane), probe not connected, and for 100% failure specifically a low battery in a polarographic unit. If the analyzer reads 0%, suspect a dead battery, a disconnected probe, or a dead probe.

No deck covers O2 analyzer calibration

Searched all 62 decks for "oxygen analyzer," "O2 analyzer," "galvanic," and "polarographic" — the only hit is the RCP 103 ABG deck's mention of the Clark electrode inside a blood gas analyzer. The 21% / 100% calibration points are Heuer-only. The decks' analogous content is blood gas analyzer calibration: solutions or gases of known value, one high and one low, for pH, PO2, and PCO2, with low calibration gas or insufficient buffer solution causing auto-calibration to fail.

SourceHeuer Ch 8 — Noninvasive Monitors/Pulse Oximeters, Table 8-8, Gas Analyzers/Oxygen Analyzers, Table 8-4, T4 Tips (PDF p410-412, 418-419, 421, 423-424) · Heuer Ch 4 — Interpreting SpO2, Table 4-3 (PDF p204-206) · Heuer Ch 6 — Pulse Oximeters, Table 6-37 (PDF p359-361) · RCP 203 Lssn 3a (Chp 4), slides 14-15, 17 · RCP 203 Lssn 2b (Chp 3), slide 9 · RCP 203 Lssn 3b (Chp 5), slide 20 · ABG Assessments PP (RCP 103), slide 65

4Spirometry Calibration to ATS Standards

The syringe. Volume calibration requires a large-volume 3.0-L calibration syringe.

The tolerance — Heuer gives both a percentage and an absolute volume. The ATS volume accuracy standard for diagnostic spirometers is ±3% OR ±50 mL, whichever is larger. Applied to the 3.0-L syringe, that means an acceptable measured volume falls between 2.91 and 3.09 L. (3% of 3.0 L is 90 mL, which is larger than 50 mL, so the percentage governs at this volume; the ±50 mL floor is what protects you at small volumes.)

How oftenVolume calibration is done DAILY with the 3-L syringe. The full ATS/ERS QC schedule (Heuer Table 8-5):
TestMinimum intervalAction / standard
LeaksDaily3 cm H2O constant pressure for 1 minute; leak must be < 10 mL
VolumeDailyCalibration check with a 3-L syringe — ±3% or ±50 mL
Volume linearityQuarterly1-L increments with a calibrating syringe over the entire volume range
Flow linearityWeeklyTest at at least three different flow ranges
TimeQuarterlyMechanical recorder checked against a stopwatch
SoftwareNew versionsLog the install date and test a known subject ("biological control")

Leak check detail (volumetric bell/bellows spirometers), done daily BEFORE the volume check and after any cleaning/reassembly: inject ~3 L of room air, occlude the circuit at the patient interface, pressurize to 3 cm H2O, and watch for volume change over 1 minute. The system should lose no more than 10 mL/min. Bigger losses mean a leak — usually loose connections, cracked tubing, or missing/damaged seals — and must be fixed before testing patients.

BTPS correction

Before calibrating, you may need to enter ambient temperature and altitude or barometric pressure, and you must make sure BTPS correction is DEACTIVATED (OFF) — otherwise the calibrating volume will read approximately 10% higher than actual. (BTPS = body temperature, pressure, saturated; it is applied to patient volumes, not to the syringe.) If the device uses an electronic flow transducer, zero it before volume or flow calibration.

Troubleshooting a failed volume calibration.

  • Low volume (< 2.91 L) → repeat the leak check first.
  • High volume (> 3.09 L) → recheck volume/flow zeroing, confirm BTPS correction is OFF, and confirm the syringe and spirometer are at the same temperature.
  • Do not test patients until you can both identify the cause and obtain an accurate calibration on repeat. If repeated checks are inaccurate with no identifiable mechanical cause, recalibrate the device. Log the leak test and volume/flow calibration results daily on a QC log.

Flow calibration

The gold standard is a computer-controlled air pump generating standard expiratory waveforms; with it the accuracy standard is ±5% of the reading or ±0.2 L/sec, whichever is greater. Acceptable alternative: inject the full 3.0-L syringe volume at three different time intervals — e.g., 0.5 second (≈6 L/sec), 6 seconds (≈0.5 L/sec), and one in between. Volume linearity is confirmed if the recorded volume deviates by no more than 100 mL from the 3.0-L target.

ATS acceptability criteria — get at least THREE error-free maneuvers. An FVC maneuver is acceptable if it is free of:

  • A slow or false start — back-extrapolated volume ≥ 5% of FVC or 150 mL (produces a characteristic S-shaped curve, low slope early then steepening)
  • Coughing during the maneuver
  • Breathing during the maneuver (an extra breath shows as a short plateau; measured volume may overestimate the true FVC)
  • Variable effort — prematurely ending exhalation (curve ends abruptly in a plateau)
  • Exhalation time less than 6 seconds

ATS repeatability/reproducibility criterion

After acceptability, confirm reproducibility: the two largest values for BOTH the FVC and the FEV1 must be within 0.150 L (150 mL) of each other. If either criterion fails, keep testing until both are met or the patient cannot continue.

Bedside NLHEP validity prompts for portable spirometers (Heuer Table 6-33): back-extrapolated volume > 150 mL → "Don't hesitate."; time to peak expiratory flow > 120 ms (>160 ms for school-age children/adolescents) → "Blast out faster."; forced expiratory time < 6.0 sec with end-of-test volume > 100 mL → "Blow out longer."; repeat FEV6 values not matching within 150 mL → "Take a deeper breath."

The decks do NOT cover any of the calibration numbers. Searched all 62 for "calibration syringe," "3-L syringe," "3.0-L," and ATS spirometry standards — nothing. Every number above is Heuer's. What the decks add is technique: turn the spirometer on with a new mouthpiece/sensor, remove gum/candy/dentures, keep the patient's position consistent and record it, use nose clips to prevent leaks, coach a full inhalation to TLC, then blast out as fast and as long as possible for at least 6 seconds (severe COPD patients may take up to 15 seconds), and repeat until you have three acceptable maneuvers.

Deck-listed technique errors match Heuer's: incomplete inhalation, inadequate expiratory force, breathing during the maneuver, leakage/poor lip seal or nose breathing, too slow a start, stopping early, and coughing.

SourceHeuer Ch 8 — Pulmonary Function Test Equipment / Accuracy of PFT Equipment, Table 8-5, Obtaining Valid Test Results, Table 8-6, T4 Tips (PDF p411-417, 424) · Heuer Ch 6 — Portable Electronic Spirometers, Table 6-33 (PDF p348-351) · RCP 203 Lssn 2b (Chp 3), slides 20, 73-75 · RCP 203 Lssn 3a (Chp 4), slides 70-75

5Preparing an ABG Sample for Analysis

Collection technique

Review the patient's PT, PTT, or INR first to anticipate longer bleeding time. Choose the nondominant wrist. The radial artery is preferred because it is superficial, not adjacent to any large vein, and has ulnar collateral circulation; alternatives (brachial, femoral, dorsalis pedis) carry greater risk. Perform a modified Allen's test: occlude radial and ulnar arteries with the hand clenched for about 5 seconds, have the patient open the hand (it should look blanched), release the ulnar artery — the palm should flush within 5–10 seconds.

A prolonged delay means inadequate ulnar flow: test the other wrist; if both fail, use the brachial artery. Assemble a heparinized syringe with the bevel up, plunger drawn back. After withdrawal, compress the site until bleeding stops (deck: at least 5 minutes; longer for patients on anticoagulants or with prolonged PT/PTT/INR), apply a sterile bandage, recheck the site after 20 minutes, and document. Then place a filter cap, expel all air, label with patient identifiers, O2 settings/FiO2 and time of draw, and mix thoroughly.

Heparin

Heuer's procedural language is simply a heparinized syringe and pre-heparinized capillary tubes — it does not specify a heparin volume. The RCP 103 deck supplies the error picture: too much heparin in the collection tube dilutes the sample → PaCO2 falls toward 0, PaO2 shifts toward room-air partial pressure (~150 torr), and because heparin is acidic, pH falls toward 7.0.

Documentation you must attach

To avoid interpretation errors you must always document the FiO2, the O2 delivery device, the mode of ventilation and current settings, and the results of any related assessments made at the time of sampling. When reporting, add a brief statement addressing test quality, including any problems with the specimen or its measurement.

Before you run it

Confirm the specimen was labeled correctly and stored properly; assess for obvious preanalytical errors such as air bubbles or clots; confirm the analyzer was properly calibrated; mix the sample thoroughly and discard a drop or two; and confirm the sample is properly aspirated/injected into the analyzer.

Timing

Analysis must occur within: < 30 minutes if at room temperature in a plastic syringe (3 minutes for capillary samples); < 5 minutes if the sample is for a shunt study; immediately if the patient has an elevated leukocyte or platelet count; and if kept > 30 minutes, the sample must be in a glass syringe chilled to 0–4°C.

Conflict to know: Heuer's number is < 30 minutes (plastic syringe, room temperature). The RCP 103 deck's slide text says "run sample within 10–30 minutes or place on ice," while its speaker notes on the same slide say "ABG samples should be analyzed within 15 minutes." Heuer = 30 min; deck slide = 10–30 min; deck notes = 15 min. Give 30 minutes on a Heuer/NBRC-style item.

The Three Analytical Stages

StageDefinition (Heuer Table 8-1)When it happensExample errors
Preanalytical"Result errors due to mistakes in the collection, handling, or storage of samples before analysis"Everything before the sample enters the analyzerHeuer's three most common: air contamination (bubbles), venous admixture, continued blood metabolism. Also clots, wrong/excess anticoagulant, failure to ice, mislabeling, not documenting FiO2/O2 device/vent settings
Analytical"Result errors due to mistakes made during measurement and analysis"While the analyzer runs the sampleUncalibrated or drifting analyzer, failed two-point calibration (low calibration gas, insufficient buffer solution), malfunctioning/degraded electrodes, contaminated buffer solutions, incorrect gas concentrations, poor or ignored QC, improper aspiration/injection of the sample
Postanalytical"Result errors due to mistakes in data handling, results reporting, or interpretation"After the numbers come outTranscription errors, wrong patient information, failure to record the FiO2, reporting without a test-quality statement, deck adds sampling less than 20 minutes after an FiO2 change

The RCP 103 deck names a fourth category: interpretation errors — incorrect classification of any ABG result (e.g., calling a compensated respiratory acidosis a metabolic acidosis).

Air Bubble in the Sample

Effect (Heuer Table 8-2): an air bubble decreases the PCO2, increases the pH, and has a variable effect on the PO2 and %HbO2. Mechanism: CO2 diffuses out of the blood into the bubble, so measured PaCO2 falls; because CO2 is lost, pH rises and the sample looks falsely alkalotic; and PaO2 is pulled toward the room-air partial pressure of about 150 torr — so it reads falsely HIGH if the true PaO2 was below ~150 torr and falsely LOW if the true PaO2 was above ~150 torr (which is why the direction is called "variable" and why it matters most in hyperoxic patients).

Recognitionvisible bubbles, and results not consistent with the patient's condition/settings. Avoidance: expel bubbles immediately, never mix a sample that still contains air bubbles, and cap syringes / seal capillary tubes quickly.

(For contrast — the other two preanalytical errors, same table: venous admixture → increases PCO2, decreases pH, decreases PO2 and %HbO2; recognized by no pulsations as the syringe fills, darker-looking blood, and results inconsistent with the patient. Avoid by not aspirating the sample, avoiding brachial/femoral sites, and using short-bevel needles.)

Delay Without Icing

Continued blood metabolism

Blood cells stay alive and metabolically active in the syringe. They keep producing CO2 → PaCO2 INCREASES; they keep consuming O2 → PaO2 DECREASES; and the accumulating CO2 plus metabolic byproducts lower the pH → falsely ACIDOTIC. That is exactly the opposite direction from an air bubble on PCO2 and pH, which is a favorite exam contrast.

Recognitiona lag time between collection and analysis, plus results that don't fit the patient. Avoidance: analyze within 30 minutes in a plastic syringe (3 minutes for capillary), immediately with a high leukocyte or platelet count (those cells consume O2 and generate CO2 fastest), and for storage > 30 minutes use a glass syringe chilled to 0–4°C — chilling slows cellular metabolism and preserves the values. Note the POCT exception: an I-STAT sample is run within 3 minutes and is NOT placed on ice.
SourceHeuer Ch 8 — Preanalytical Phase, Table 8-1, Table 8-2, T4 Tips (PDF p401-404, 423) · Heuer Ch 3 — Blood Gas Sample Collection, Figure 3-2, Blood Gas Analysis/Hemoximetry (PDF p144-146, 150-152) · ABG Assessments PP (RCP 103), slides 53-55, 71-74 · RCP 203 Lssn 2b (Chp 3), slide 29

6Bench Hemoximeter / CO-Oximeter

What it is

A hemoximeter (CO-oximeter) is a multi-wavelength spectrophotometer. Because each hemoglobin species absorbs light at a different set of wavelengths, the device can directly measure each species rather than infer it. (Deck vocabulary distinction: hemoximetry = the measurement; hemoximeter = the device.)

The hemoglobin species it directly measures — the four plus total:

  1. Oxyhemoglobin (HbO2) — a true, measured SaO2/%HbO2
  2. Deoxyhemoglobin (HHb) — reduced hemoglobin
  3. Carboxyhemoglobin (COHb / HbCO)
  4. Methemoglobin (MetHb)
  5. Total hemoglobin (tHb)

Heuer's Table 5-4 also lists sulfhemoglobin among the abnormal Hb levels hemoximetry measures. Hemoximeter calibration uses standard solutions with known values for total Hb, HbO2, COHb, and MetHb.

What a blood gas analyzer cannot do

An ABG analyzer directly measures only three things — pH, PCO2, and PO2 — using the Sanz (pH), Severinghaus (PCO2), and Clark (PO2) electrodes. Everything else on the report is CALCULATED: HCO3−, base excess, and SaO2. The SaO2 is derived from the PaO2 via the oxyhemoglobin dissociation curve, which assumes normal hemoglobin function — so a blood gas analyzer cannot distinguish normal hemoglobin from dysfunctional hemoglobins and does not measure actual Hb content, actual Hb saturation, HbCO, or metHb.

Heuer's oximeter troubleshooting table makes the practical point: an ABG SaO2 is usually a computed rather than a measured value, so compare an SpO2 to a hemoximeter's SaO2, not to the ABG's.

When a hemoximeter is required.

  • Whenever you need accurate measures of ANY hemoglobin parameter — actual %HbO2, total Hb, or any abnormal Hb.
  • Smoke inhalation / CO poisoning — Heuer's most common scenario. In CO poisoning, do not trust the SpO2 (a 2-wavelength oximeter reads falsely high) and the PaO2 can look normal because dissolved oxygen is unaffected. Use a CO-oximeter.
  • During inhaled nitric oxide (iNO) therapy — to monitor metHb concentrations.
  • When you need to "calibrate" a pulse oximetry reading (SpO2) against the actual arterial saturation — use the SaO2 − SpO2 difference.
  • Any time pulse oximetry would be substituted for a decision that demands accuracy — pulse oximetry should never replace ABG analysis or hemoximetry when the clinical situation demands accurate oxygenation assessment.

Reference COHb levels (deck): 1–3% = normal, 5–10% = smokers, 10–15% = heavy smokers, > 20% = CO poisoning. In practice, ABG and CO-oximetry are run together for the full oxygenation picture.

SourceHeuer Ch 8 — Analyzer Calibration and Calibration Verification (PDF p403) · Heuer Ch 5 — Hemoximetry (CO-oximetry), Table 5-4 (PDF p269-270) · Heuer Ch 6 — Table 6-37 (PDF p360-361) · Heuer Ch 8 — Table 8-8 (PDF p421) · RCP 203 Lssn 2b (Chp 3), slide 29 · RCP 203 Lssn 3b (Chp 5), slides 19-20 · ABG Assessments PP (RCP 103), slides 64, 70
05

Mechanical Ventilation, BiPAP, Weaning & Compliance

8 items · BiPAP · how VT is achieved in each mode · ventilator alarms · circuits · what hyperventilation causes · weaning parameters · vent changes from an ABG · static and dynamic compliance

1BiPAP — Indications, Contraindications, Hazards, Monitoring, Adjustments, Interfaces2How Tidal Volume Is Achieved in Each Mode — Control, Trigger, and Who Benefits3Ventilator Alarms — What Triggers Them4Ventilator Circuits — Hospital and Home Care5What Hyperventilation Causes6Weaning Parameters, SBT Protocol, and Extubation Criteria7Making Ventilator Changes from an ABG8Static and Dynamic Compliance — Formulas, Normal Ranges, and Reading Pplat

1BiPAP — Indications, Contraindications, Hazards, Monitoring, Adjustments, Interfaces

BiPAP is noninvasive ventilation delivered at two pressure levels, and the patient must be spontaneously breathing, alert, cooperative, able to protect the upper airway, and able to manage secretions before you use it instead of intubating. IPAP (Inspiratory Positive Airway Pressure) is applied only during inspiration — think ventilation / VT / CO2. EPAP (Expiratory Positive Airway Pressure) is applied continuously through inspiration and exhalation — it is functionally PEEP, so think oxygenation: it increases FRC, recruits alveoli, reduces shunt, and raises SpO2.

The difference (IPAP − EPAP) is the Pressure Support / driving pressure ΔP, which the decks say must never be less than 4 cmH2O. Heuer states the same relationship as an equation: ΔP = IPAP − EPAP during NPPV, and VT is a function of patient effort and ΔP.

The single most tested rule from the decks: you can always raise IPAP without raising EPAP, but you can NEVER raise EPAP without raising IPAP by the same amount (otherwise you eat into ΔP and lose ventilation). Heuer teaches the identical rule from the other direction — "to avoid changing the PaCO2, keep ΔP constant."

Initial settings — the three sources disagree, know all of them:

SettingRCP 105/202 Chang decksRCP 105 NIV 2023 deckHeuer
IPAP8 cmH2O8–12 cmH2O10–15 cmH2O (Ch 11, Table 11-7); 10 cmH2O in the COPD-exacerbation protocol (Ch 20)
EPAP4 cmH2O4–6 cmH2O5 cmH2O
PS / ΔPmust be ≥ 4 cmH2O; ex. 15/5 = PS 10samenot given as a minimum; instead target VT 6–8 mL/kg
Backup rate10–12, or 2–5 breaths below the patient's spontaneous RRsameat least 8/min (Ch 11); 10/min (Ch 20)
Target VT8–10 mL/kg IBW—6–8 mL/kg
Trigger/sensitivitynot specified—1–2 cmH2O below baseline pressure, or 1–3 L/min below baseline flow
%I-time (timed modes)I-time is set off the backup rate only—20–30%
FiO2titrate to SpO2—to keep PaO2 ≥ 65 torr or SaO2 ≥ 92%
IPAP ceilingnot givennot givenkeep IPAP < 20–25 cmH2O — that is the esophageal opening pressure; above it you insufflate the stomach
Rise / Ramp / LeakRise: lower number = faster. Ramp OFF or 5–45 min. Total Leak normal 7–25, never > 60, never "---"/"0"—set rise time to reach the pressure plateau without spiking (a left "dog-ear")

Heuer's three exam problems (memorize the pattern) — patient on IPAP/EPAP 10/5:

Problem 1 — improve OXYGENATION only Raise BOTH equally: 10/5 -> 12/7 (delta-P stays 5; baseline rises) Problem 2 — improve VENTILATION only Raise IPAP only: 10/5 -> 15/5 (delta-P 5 -> 10; baseline unchanged) Alternative if oxygenation is stable: LOWER EPAP 10/5 -> 10/3 (delta-P 5 -> 7) Problem 3 — improve BOTH Raise both, IPAP more: 10/5 -> 15/7 (delta-P 5 -> 8 AND baseline rises)

Heuer adds one more rule the decks lack: if air trapping / auto-PEEP develops on NPPV and the pH/PaCO2 are acceptable, raise EPAP and IPAP equally — that counteracts the inspiratory threshold load without changing ventilation.

Indications

Decks: prevent intubation in end-stage COPD, chronic ventilatory failure, restrictive chest wall disease, neuromuscular disease, nocturnal hypoventilation, plus acute respiratory failure, severe hypoxemia, severe asthma, COPD exacerbation, acute heart failure, post-extubation support, moderate acidemia, moderate hypercarbia, obesity hypoventilation, sleep apnea. Heuer's CPAP-specific indications: obstructive sleep apnea, acute cardiogenic pulmonary edema, postoperative atelectasis, and in neonates RDS and apnea of prematurity.

Acute pulmonary edema — a numeric conflict

Decks: first-line is CPAP 8–12 cmH2O with 100% oxygen. Heuer: start at 10 cmH2O and 100% O2, titrate to SpO2 > 90%, and avoid it if systolic BP < 90 mmHg, acute MI, Glasgow ≤ 8, or intubation is needed; switch to bilevel with a backup rate if hypopnea or hypercapnia appears. For OSA, Heuer sets CPAP by prescription or auto-CPAP to a maximum of 15 cmH2O, judging success by an AHI < 5/hour (normal 0–4/h, mild 5–14/h, moderate 15–30/h, severe > 30/h).

Contraindications

Decks: lack of cooperation, cardiac arrest, respiratory arrest, excessive secretions, inability to fit a mask, inability to protect the airway, severe facial burns or trauma. Heuer's Table 20-3 splits them — Respiratory: respiratory arrest, upper airway obstruction, unable to protect the airway, unable to clear secretions, high aspiration risk. Non-respiratory: cardiac arrest/hemodynamic instability, mental status changes (obtundation), active upper GI bleeding, facial surgery or trauma, facial deformity, non-fitting mask with significant leak, nausea or vomiting.

Hazards/complications

aerophagia and gastric insufflation, airway dryness, aspiration, claustrophobia, decreased cardiac output, dry mouth, secretion build-up in the mask, eye irritation from leak, pressure sores from the mask/tubing/straps.

Monitoring and when to give up — the sources conflict on timing. Decks: watch the ABG (success = PaO2 rising, PaCO2 falling); if the patient continues to deteriorate 30 minutes after starting NIV, intubate. Heuer is slower and more specific — intubate for NPPV failure defined as worsening ABGs in the first 1–2 hours or lack of improvement in ABGs after 4 hours, or for severe acidosis (pH < 7.25) with hypercapnia (PaCO2 > 60 torr), severe hypoxemia (P/F < 200), or severe tachypnea (> 35/min). Heuer also adds a target the decks lack: always aim for a normal pH, not necessarily a normal PCO2.

Alarms (V60, from the decks)

Lo Rate one higher than the set backup rate (BUR 12 → Lo Rate 13); HIP 5 to 10 ABOVE IPAP; LIP 2 to 3 BELOW IPAP; Hi/Lo VT and Hi RR set off actual measurements; Lo VE normally 4 or 5 L/min; NIV deck adds high/low pressure ±5 in S/T, Low VE start at 4 then 10–20% below baseline, VT alarms ±150 mL around the desired VT from IBW.

Monitor Patient Leak vs Total Leak: use Total Leak only when a Circuit/Leak Test has been completed with after-market equipment; Patient Leak if no circuit test was run. Heuer's minimum requirement for any acute-care NPPV device: a low-pressure/disconnect alarm and a power-failure alarm, usually plus apnea, and sometimes high/low volume and high pressure.

How to make changes

High CO2 → increase IPAP (increase ΔP); also raise the mandatory rate if the patient's own rate is low (Heuer). Low CO2 → decrease IPAP/ΔP. Low SpO2 → increase EPAP or FiO2 — Heuer applies the shunt rule: if PaO2 ≤ 50 torr on FiO2 ≥ 0.50, raise EPAP (keeping ΔP constant); if it is a simple V/Q problem on FiO2 < 0.50, just raise FiO2.

Weaning off: first drop FiO2 to ≤ 0.50, then lower EPAP. Deck worked examples: COPD, pH 7.28 / PaCO2 75 → ventilation problem → raise IPAP; CHF, SpO2 84% with normal PaCO2 → oxygenation problem → raise EPAP. Related modes: AVAPS auto-adjusts PS to a user-set target VT; C-Flex drops pressure at the start of exhalation for comfort.

Weaning off NPPV (Heuer only — the decks do not give thresholds). Readiness: underlying condition mostly resolved, RR < 25/min, HR < 110/min, IPAP < 15 cmH2O, EPAP < 10 cmH2O, compensated pH > 7.35, SpO2 > 90% on < 50% O2. Procedure: wean during daytime hours first, extend time off in increments, use the off periods for meals/fluids/airway clearance/skin care, then give at least one more night of support before stopping.

Interfaces

Heuer names three common NPPV interfaces — oronasal ("full") mask, nasal mask, nasal pillows — plus oral devices for chronic hypercapnic patients on intermittent support (e.g., progressive neuromuscular disease). His selection rule: use an oronasal/full face mask when initiating NPPV for acute respiratory failure (lower leakage), but avoid it if the patient is at high risk of vomiting or aspiration; for chronic hypercapnic respiratory failure, nasal appliances are preferred because they have less deadspace. Any full oronasal mask must have a safety inlet valve (a nonrebreathing/plateau exhalation valve fixes rebreathing).

The decks give the same four types with more complication detail — nasal mask (most common for CPAP/sleep apnea; less aspiration risk, better secretion clearance, less claustrophobia, easier speech, less deadspace; biggest problem is leak in mouth-breathers → chin strap; fit the smallest mask that does not obstruct the nostrils; treat congestion with humidification); oronasal (better seal, less leak, but aspiration, regurgitation, asphyxiation risk if power/gas fails); nasal pillows (smallest, most comfortable, cause nasal congestion, dry mouth, nosebleeds; used for CPAP but NOT recommended for BiPAP); full-face (largest, most uncomfortable, for significant mouth-breathing as in COPD exacerbation).

Heuer's Table 6-24 remedies: mouth leak → chin strap; nasal bridge redness → reduce strap tension, forehead spacer, artificial skin/nose pad; claustrophobia → clear mask with minimal bulk; impeded speech/eating → permit periodic removal. Vented masks require the vent for exhalation and use only one corrugated tube to the ventilator.

SourceHeuer Ch 11 — Initiating and Adjusting Mask/Nasal CPAP (PDF p527-529) and Initiating and Adjusting BiPAP, Table 11-7 (PDF p547-549) · Heuer Ch 6 — CPAP/NPPV Devices (PDF p306-309) and Table 6-24 NPPV Interface Problems (PDF p330-331) · Heuer Ch 20 — NPPV settings and Table 20-3 Contraindications (heuer_full.txt, unpaginated) · RCP 202 Lssn 1b (BiPAP Basics, Chang Ch 4), slides 1-4 · RCP 105 Wk 1 Lctr 1 (NIV 2023), slides 4-13 · RCP 105 Wk 1 Lctr 1 (NIV/NiPPV, Chang Ch 4 & 7), slides 3-6, 10-13 · RCP 105 Wk 1 Lctr 4 (Operating Modes, Chang Ch 4), slides 12-13

2How Tidal Volume Is Achieved in Each Mode — Control, Trigger, and Who Benefits

The whole chapter reduces to one Golden Rule: you can only control ONE of volume or pressure; the other becomes the variable. The deck's clinical example: in VC with VT set to 500 mL, a patient who develops ARDS still gets 500 mL but the pressure rises; in PC set to 20 cmH2O, that patient still gets 20 cmH2O but the VT falls.

Heuer classifies modes the same way the NBRC does — by control variable (volume vs pressure) and by breath sequence (A/C = machine breaths only; SIMV = machine breaths plus spontaneous breaths) — which yields the four combinations you will actually be tested on: VC A/C, VC SIMV, PC A/C, PC SIMV, with PS and PEEP as "add-ons" and PRVC and APRV as advanced/dual modes.

To describe any mode, ask: What is controlled? What triggers (starts) and cycles (ends) the breath? (Trigger = Start, Cycle = Stop) What is the breath sequence? A patient trigger shows on the pressure-time waveform as a negative deflection below baseline; a time trigger shows no deflection.

ModeHow VT is achievedControl variableTriggerWho benefits
VC (Volume Control) — deck's "1st choice"Set VT delivered at a set flow; VT guaranteed, pressure is whatever it takes. Heuer: maintains a constant VT despite changes in Crs and Raw, and changes in mechanics are easy to detect by watching PIPVolumeTime (set rate) or PatientAnyone needing a guaranteed minute ventilation; PaCO2 control is predictable. Downside (Heuer): pressures rise as compliance falls / resistance rises → barotrauma risk, and the fixed square flow pattern can cause asynchrony
PC (Pressure Control) — "2nd choice"Set inspiratory pressure is held; VT is whatever the lungs accept and falls if compliance drops or resistance risesPressureTime or PatientStiff lungs / high airway pressures. Heuer: variable decelerating flow aids synchrony and there is less barotrauma risk; downside is VT varies and changes in mechanics are hard to detect, so VT and VE must be watched closely. Switch VC → PC whenever Pplat exceeds 30 cmH2O, using a limit ≤ 30 cmH2O
CMV (pure Control)Preset VT every breath; ventilator does 100% of the workVolume (or pressure)Time only — no patient triggering allowedTetanus/seizures, complete rest, crushed chest, paradoxical chest wall movement; also (Heuer) inverse I:E ratio ventilation and permissive hypercapnia, when the rate and pattern must be controlled. Needs deep sedation or paralysis; poorly tolerated, patient efforts cause "fighting the ventilator" and ↑WOB; rapid disuse atrophy
A/C (Assist/Control)Every breath — assisted or controlled — gets the same full preset breath. Rate set at 12, patient triggers 24 → 24 full breathsVolume (VC-A/C) or Pressure (PC-A/C)Assist = patient-triggered (negative deflection); Control = time-triggeredFull ventilatory support — ARDS, pneumonia, sepsis, post-arrest, severe respiratory failure; minimizes atelectasis, reduces WOB; patient controls his own rate/PaCO2 above the set rate and may avoid sedation/paralysis. Hazards (Heuer): hyperventilation at high trigger rates from anxiety, fear, pain, brain injury, fever, or hypoxemia; trigger/flow/cycle asynchrony raising WOB and O2 consumption; may worsen auto-PEEP in COPD/asthma
SIMVMandatory breaths get the preset VT (ex. 450 mL); spontaneous breaths in between are whatever VT the patient wants (200, 350, 600 mL) and can be boosted with PSVolume or Pressure for the mandatory breathsMandatory breaths time-triggered (rate 10 → one every 60 ÷ 10 = 6 sec) or patient-triggered/synchronizedPartial support — maintains respiratory muscle strength, avoids atrophy, allows graded support, lowers sedation need, lower mean airway pressure than A/C. Heuer's specific indication: when full support is needed but the patient's rate causes hyperventilation on A/C. Hazards: hypoventilation at low rates, fatigue without PS. NOT recommended as a stand-alone weaning mode (Heuer: "being replaced by SBTs on T-tube, CPAP, or BiPAP")
PSV (Pressure Support)Patient starts the breath, ventilator adds a preset pressure plateau; VT is set by the PATIENT (effort + compliance + resistance). Same PS 10 gives 700 mL in good lungs, 300 mL in ARDSPressure (pressure-limited)Patient only — an apneic patient cannot be on pure PSVLow spontaneous VT, high spontaneous rate, increased WOB; overcoming the imposed WOB of the ET tube; boosting spontaneous VT during SIMV/CPAP; weaning and SBTs. Board phrase: "patient-triggered, pressure-limited, FLOW-cycled" — ends when flow falls to a preset % of peak flow (commonly ~25%). Heuer's cautions: hypoventilation without a backup rate, variable VT/VE, and in COPD, flow-cycling to end inspiration may be prolonged because of air trapping
PRVC (= Autoflow, VC+, Adaptive Pressure Control, Volume Guarantee)Closed loop: VT is guaranteed while the ventilator finds the lowest possible PIP, adjusting the pressure limit breath to breath according to measured mechanicsVolume-targeted, pressure-controlled ("dual")Time or PatientChanging compliance or resistance; patients needing the lowest possible pressure with a consistent VT and variable inspiratory flow. Advantages (Heuer): lower PIP than VC or PC A/C, less barotrauma, near-constant VT, decelerating flow, automatic weaning of support as the patient improves. Disadvantages: variable patient effort → variable VT and asynchrony; severe ↑Raw or ↓Crs can produce unsafe PIP or reduced VT; when patient demand rises the pressure level may DROP just when support is most needed, lowering Pmean and causing hypoxemia; may worsen auto-PEEP; the ventilator cannot tell a patient-caused rise in Raw from a machine-caused one (e.g., a wet filter)
CPAPNO mandatory breaths at all — every breath's VT is generated entirely by the patientPressure (one continuous baseline)Patient onlyOSA, cardiogenic pulmonary edema, postoperative atelectasis, post-extubation, neonatal RDS/apnea of prematurity; also facilitates weaning. Requires adequate drive, VT, and spontaneous PaCO2. CPAP improves oxygenation but does NOT ventilate — wrong choice for the apneic, oversedated, or severely fatigued patient
APRV (Heuer only)CPAP at Phigh with intermittent timed releases to Plow; VT comes from the release plus spontaneous effort. Technically time-triggered, pressure-limited, time-cycled with spontaneous breathing at both levelsPressureTime (release) + spontaneousAcute lung injury, especially when Pplat > 30 cmH2O; refractory hypoxemia from collapsed alveoli; massive atelectasis. Settings: Plow 0–8 cmH2O, Phigh set for an inflation volume of 4–8 mL/kg PBW but < 30–35 cmH2O, Thigh 3–5 sec, Tlow 0.2–0.8 sec, giving ~10–20 cycles/min. Auto-PEEP is usually present — and desired. Only for patients who can trigger enough spontaneous breaths

PBW/IBW math

Heuer: males = 50 + 2.3 (height in inches − 60); females = 45.5 + 2.3 (height in inches − 60). Heuer's worked case: a 6-ft (72-in) male → PBW ≈ 78 kg, and 8 mL/kg ≈ 625 mL — and even if that man weighs 120 kg (265 lb) you still set 600–800 mL, definitely not 1200 mL. The deck's version for a 5'10" male: 50 + (2.3 × 10) = 73 kg, so 73 × 8 = 584 mL and 73 × 10 = 730 mL → target ≈ 580–730 mL.

PSV titration numbers: start PS 5–15 cmH2O (Modes deck) or 5–10 cmH2O (Heuer) and titrate to a VT of 8–10 mL/kg IBW (Modes deck) or 10–15 mL/kg (Weaning deck) with a spontaneous rate < 25/min (all three agree on the rate). Then wean down by 3 to 6 cmH2O to about 5–6 cmH2O.
