3Ventilator Alarms — What Triggers Them

Alarms exist to activate the clinician to intervene, minimize false alarms and alarm fatigue, and ensure important conditions are not missed. Heuer's framing: alarms warn of either ventilator malfunction (mostly preprogrammed) or changes in patient status (set by the clinician). The most dangerous action in critical care is silencing an alarm without determining the cause; the sequence is always Alarm → look at the patient → look at the ventilator → determine cause → fix the problem.

AlarmWhere it is set — Heuer vs decksCauses
High inspiratory pressureHeuer: 50 cmH2O, or 10–15 cmH2O above PIP (use the mandatory breath PIP during SIMV). Decks: 10–15 cmH2O ABOVE observed PIP — must not be raised without justificationIncreased resistance or decreased compliance; partial or complete airway obstruction; cough, secretions, mucus plugs; patient–ventilator asynchrony; anxiety, restlessness, pain; alarm accidentally set below the PIP of a pressure-supported breath. Deck list: water in the circuit, kinked ETT, tension pneumothorax, biting the tube, bronchospasm, blood clot, airway edema, obstructed filter or partially closed HME
Low inspiratory pressureHeuer: 8 cmH2O, or 5–10 cmH2O BELOW PIP (use the pressure-supported breath PIP during SIMV). Decks: 10–15 cmH2O BELOW observed PIP — a real conflict, know bothDisconnection, airway/circuit leaks, alarm set above the PS-breath PIP, and (Heuer) improved compliance and resistance
Low exhaled VTHeuer: 100 mL or 10–15% below the set VT (or spontaneous VT during SIMV/CPAP). Decks: 100 mL below the expired VTDisconnection, airway/circuit leaks, pulmonary leak (bronchopleural fistula), shallow spontaneous breathing, coughing, asynchrony. On the loop: loops do not return to zero volume; expiratory volume < inspiratory volume
Low minute ventilationHeuer: 4–5 L/min, or 10–15% below the SIMV/AC minimum. Decks: 4–5 L/min (BiPAP) or 10–20% below baselineSame causes as low exhaled VT, plus sedation/anesthesia and low metabolic rate
High minute ventilationHeuer: 10–15% above baseline VEIncreased metabolic rate (fever), tachypnea, anxiety/restlessness/pain, patient waking from sedation
ApneaHeuer: 20-second delay. Decks: 15–20 secondsSedation/anesthesia, low metabolic rate, set rate < 3/min; decks add circuit disconnection, prolonged neuromuscular block, respiratory center dysfunction, muscle fatigue
High frequencyDecks: 10/min over the observed total rate — must not be raised without clear justificationGenuine need for more minute ventilation (rapid shallow breathing); insufficient flow or pressure support; excessive sensitivity → autotriggering
High PEEP / CPAPabove set PEEPAuto-PEEP, air trapping, insufficient inspiratory flow (long I-time), insufficient expiratory time, and (Heuer) an obstructed/sticking expiratory or PEEP valve
Low PEEP / CPAPHeuer: 3–5 cmH2O below set PEEP. Decks: below set PEEPDisconnection, airway/circuit leaks, malfunctioning PEEP valve
High / Low FiO2 (O2 analyzer)Heuer: preprogrammed, ±5–6% of set FiO2. Decks: 5–10% above/below the analyzed FiO2Gas source failure (air or O2), analyzer needs calibration/replacement, sudden change in delivered FiO2 (e.g., the 100% suction button)
Temperature (Heuer only)preprogrammed ±2°C of set temp, not to exceed 41°CDry water chamber, tubing condensation, defective wiring/probe/chamber, heater malfunction, room temperature change, high VE with high flow
Loss of power / gas supply (Heuer)preprogrammedPower: cord disconnection, backup battery failure, tripped breaker, institutional power failure. Gas: lines not connected to the 50-psi outlets, lines connected to a low-pressure outlet, high-pressure line failure

Heuer's two-alarm pattern-recognition shortcut (Table 6-23):

  • Low volume + LOW pressure = a LEAK. Check and tighten circuit connections.
  • Low volume + HIGH pressure = an OBSTRUCTION (inspiratory or expiratory). Find and correct it, drain condensate, replace the HME, suction the patient. Expiratory obstruction is the more dangerous of the two — it causes rebreathing, asphyxia, or barotrauma — and the most dangerous version is a blocked exhalation port on a single-limb circuit (patient grabbing the circuit, or bedding over the port).
  • Expiratory/PEEP valve open or leaking = low-volume + low-PEEP alarms; obstructed/sticking = high-pressure + high-PEEP alarms. Fix: single-limb → check the expiratory valve line or replace the circuit; dual-limb with an internal valve → replace the ventilator.
The PIP-versus-Pplat reasoning is the single highest-yield alarm skillBecause PIP = Resistance + Compliance and Plateau = Compliance only, the difference PIP − Pplat = Resistance (PTA, transairway pressure). Heuer states the same thing as PIP − Pplat ≈ Raw and Pplat − PEEP ≈ Crs:
  • High PIP + NORMAL Pplat = a RESISTANCE problem (PTA widens, Cdyn falls while Crs is unchanged, so Crs − Cdyn widens). Heuer's causes: bronchospasm, excessive secretions, peribronchial/airway edema and swelling, partial artificial airway occlusion (mucus plug, kinking, biting). Decks add water in the tubing, obstructed filter, partially closed HME. Plateau does not change because flow has stopped during the pause, so resistance is not included.
  • High PIP + HIGH Pplat, both rising in proportion (PIP − Pplat unchanged) = a COMPLIANCE problem. Heuer's causes: atelectasis, pulmonary edema/CHF, ARDS, tension pneumothorax, pleural effusion, overdistention/hyperinflation, ET tube slippage into a mainstem bronchus, bronchial obstruction by a mucus plug, ascites/abdominal distention. The Weaning deck splits these into sudden (tension pneumothorax, pleural effusion, ETT into a mainstem bronchus) versus gradual (pneumonia, ARDS, atelectasis) — sudden = search for the problem, gradual = treat the disease.

Teacher's phrasing: "When PIP and Plateau move together, think lungs. When only PIP moves, think airways."

SourceHeuer Ch 11 — Setting and Adjusting Ventilator Alarms, Table 11-9 (PDF p551-555) · Heuer Ch 6 — Table 6-23 Troubleshooting Ventilator Circuit Problems (PDF p329-331) · Heuer Ch 4 — Pulmonary Compliance and Airway Resistance During Mechanical Ventilation (PDF p229-231) · RCP 105 Wk 1 Lctr 5 (Initiation of Mech Vent, Chang Ch 8), slides 26-28 · RCP 105 Mgmt of Mech Vent (Chang Ch 12), slides 28-32 · RCP 105 Wk 1 Lctr 3 (Principles), slides 12-13, 65 · RCP 105 Wk 6 (Waveform Analysis), slides 8-10, 66 · RCP 202 Lssn 1b (BiPAP Basics), slide 4 · RCP 105 Wk 3 (Weaning), slides 28-29

4Ventilator Circuits — Hospital and Home Care

Heuer and the decks agree exactly: there are two general circuit families — the dual-limb ("wye") circuit and the single-limb circuit — and single-limb circuits come in two flavors, one with a true expiratory "mushroom" valve and one with a leakage-type exhaust port. That makes three circuits total, matched to three settings:

CircuitWhere it is usedMechanics
Dual-limb "wye"Most critical care ventilators; also continuous-flow CPAP circuitsInspiratory limb delivers fresh gas, a standard 15-mm patient connector/swivel adapter, and an expiratory limb returning gas to the ventilator's expiratory or PEEP/CPAP valve — which is what allows accurate exhaled-volume measurement. May also carry heated-wire plug connectors, a pressure-sensing line, and temperature-probe ports. Mechanical deadspace = everything between the wye and the patient's airway — every HME, MDI adapter, or end-tidal CO2 adapter you add there increases deadspace
Single-limb WITH an expiratory valveTransport and home care ventilatorsA separate pneumatic line runs from the ventilator to the valve; pressurized, it blocks outflow during inspiration; at the start of expiration it depressurizes and lets gas out. Because it holds a set pressure through expiration, this valve can also provide CPAP/PEEP. Mechanical deadspace = between the built-in expiratory valve and the airway
Single-limb WITHOUT a valve (leak port)Noninvasive positive-pressure ventilatorsThe continuous flow the NPPV device supplies forces expired gas out the exhaust port; combined with normal interface leak this prevents rebreathing and minimizes mechanical deadspace. PEEP/CPAP is created by continuous regulation of pressure and system flow/leak through the ventilator's demand valve — which is exactly why EPAP must never fall to near zero, or CO2 is not flushed. These are the vented masks: one corrugated tube, vent required for exhalation

Circuit compliance matters in both settings: circuit compliance should be as low as possible, because high circuit compliance holds more compressible volume and reduces the VT actually delivered. Deck numbers: at a PIP of 40 cmH2O, a circuit with compliance 5 mL/cmH2O holds 200 mL of the set VT, while a 3 mL/cmH2O circuit holds only 120 mL. Hence Corrected VT = Expired VT − circuit compressible volume, and a 50 mL loss is huge when VT is 150 mL but trivial when VT is 700 mL (neonates, peds, stiff ARDS lungs).

Heuer's related rule for the pre-use check: never use a "cheater hose" — use the actual circuit so it can be leak-tested, measured, and compensated for compressible volume.

Humidification

All patients ventilated through a tracheal airway need either an active heated humidifier or a passive HME, and Heuer sets one hard number: the gas reaching the airway must carry at least 30 mg/L of water vapor.

  • Heated humidifier. Heuer: 100% relative humidity delivered at 34–41°C. Decks: as close to 100% RH at body temperature, target 37°C, with the temperature probe inside the circuit on the inspiratory limb as close to the patient as possible (at the wye). (Use Heuer's 34–41°C range on a national exam; use 37°C if your instructor asks for "body temperature.") Troubleshooting: airway temperature too high or too low → reset to 34–41°C, check for probe not in the circuit, abrupt flow change, low/empty reservoir, a fan or A/C vent blowing on the probe, or unit failure.
  • HME (Heat and Moisture Exchanger). A passive, temporary device that adds no humidity — it only retains and returns the patient's own exhaled heat and moisture (surface impregnated with CaCl2 or AlCl2), giving 70–90% RH at 30–31°C (decks). Placement: between the wye and the airway interface (Heuer) = between the artificial airway and the circuit (decks). It always adds deadspace and must be positioned for bidirectional flow. Compared with a heated humidifier, an HME with a bacterial-viral filter costs less to maintain and is less likely to colonize bacteria. Do NOT use an HME with: NPPV (one-way flow and large leaks ruin it, and the added deadspace/resistance raises CO2 and WOB), ARDS patients on low VT (< 6 mL/kg), hypercapnic respiratory failure, dehydration, high minute volume, hypothermia, a large exhalation leak, or thick/bloody secretions (which block it and raise circuit resistance).
  • MDI through an HME — the sources differ. Decks: the MDI must be placed BETWEEN the HME and the patient's airway. Heuer: because the HME filters the drug and the aerosol can obstruct the HME, switch to active heated humidification to give aerosolized medication.
  • Condensation is the most common threat to circuit patency. Gas cools between the humidifier and the patient, water condenses and collects in the tubing, obstructing airflow — a listed cause of the high-pressure alarm, of autocycling, and of weaning failure. Manage it with a heated-wire circuit (its connectors plug into the low-voltage outlets on the humidifier), inline water traps at low points, or an HME.

Infection control

Heuer: change circuits only when visibly soiled or malfunctioning; avoid unnecessary disconnections (use an in-line/closed-suction system); prevent condensate from draining into the airway and avoid contamination when disposing of it; use a drainage system that avoids disconnection. His VAP bundle: head of bed elevated 30–45°, daily sedation vacations and SBTs, cuff pressures 20–30 cmH2O (avoid the minimal-leak technique), subglottic-suction tubes, closed suction. The decks agree — do not change circuits routinely; the maximum safe interval is unknown; weekly has been shown optimal; change when visibly soiled.

SourceHeuer Ch 6 — Dual-Limb Circuits, Single-Limb Circuits, Table 6-22 (PDF p323-327), Humidifiers and Table 6-15 (PDF p310-313), Circuit Testing (PDF p328) · Heuer Ch 9 — HMEs contraindicated in NPPV; VAP prevention key points (PDF p461, p468) · RCP 105 Wk 1 Lctr 3 (Principles of Mech Vent, Chang/Hess), slide 63 · RCP 105 Mgmt of Mech Vent (Chang Ch 12), slides 36-40 · RCP 105 Wk 1 Lctr 5 (Initiation of Mech Vent), slide 14 · RCP 105 Wk 1 Lctr 1 (NIV 2023), slide 13 · RCP 105 Wk 3 (Weaning), slide 28

5What Hyperventilation Causes

Hyperventilation means increased minute ventilation → decreased PaCO2 → increased pH, i.e. acute respiratory alkalosis (the deck's classic ABG is pH 7.52, PaCO2 30 mmHg, HCO3− 24 mEq/L).

The cerebral effect

CO2 is a potent cerebral vasodilator, so falling PaCO2 causes cerebral VASOCONSTRICTION. The deck's mechanism: CO2 freely crosses the blood-brain barrier, combines with water to form carbonic acid, and dissociates into H+ and HCO3−; the lower CSF H+ constricts cerebral arterioles. The chain: ↑VE → ↓PaCO2 → ↓cerebral vessel diameter → ↓cerebral blood flow → ↓ICP. Heuer lists hyperventilation/hypocapnia as one of only four causes of a decreased ICP (with head elevation, decreased CSF volume, and severe arterial hypotension), and confirms the mirror image — avoid hypercapnia, which causes cerebral vasodilation and raises ICP. Heuer's ICP numbers: normal 10–15 mmHg supine; > 20 mmHg for more than 5–10 minutes is abnormally high; > 25 mmHg for a prolonged period predicts poor outcomes; and once ICP exceeds 15–20 mmHg, cerebral blood flow can fall to a critical level, producing secondary ischemia.

Numbers for TBI — a conflict worth memorizingThe RCP 105 Critical Issues deck targets a normal PaCO2 of 35–45 mmHg, allows temporary hyperventilation to about 30–35 mmHg for critically elevated ICP or impending herniation, quotes Chang's older allowance of as low as 26 torr in the first 24 hours while insisting you should not drive PaCO2 below 26 mmHg (no additional ICP benefit), and warns to avoid a prolonged PaCO2 < 30 mmHg unless neurosurgery directs it. Heuer is tighter: aim for a PaCO2 of 35–40 torr, PIP ≤ 30 cmH2O, SpO2 > 95%, and good patient–ventilator synchrony. Heuer also says avoid high levels of PEEP (they can lower MAP, raise ICP, and lower cerebral perfusion pressure) and avoid prophylactic/routine hyperventilation — it lowers ICP but can cause cerebral ischemia — reserving it for exactly three situations: (1) to blunt ICP spikes before procedures such as suctioning, (2) confirmed cerebral herniation, and (3) salvage therapy when high ICP does not respond to standard treatment. In neonatal PPHN management Heuer likewise lists hyperventilation under "Do Not Recommend" because it adversely affects cerebral circulation and can cause barotrauma.

The hemoglobin effect (decks only)

Respiratory alkalosis lasting more than 24 hours shifts the oxyhemoglobin dissociation curve to the LEFT. Left = "Love" — hemoglobin holds onto oxygen and will not release it, so despite good saturations the tissues, especially brain tissue, do not get oxygen efficiently. Result: cerebral tissue hypoxia and neurologic injury — dizziness, confusion, altered mental status, decreased cerebral perfusion, worsening neurologic injury. Prolonged hypocapnia also causes hypophosphatemia (phosphate shifts into cells), producing muscle weakness, diaphragm weakness, cardiac dysfunction, and neurologic dysfunction. The oxyhemoglobin dissociation curve shift is not discussed anywhere in the Heuer text — a search of the full extraction for "dissociation curve," "affinity," "left shift," and "P50" returns nothing on this topic, so this point rests on the RCP 105 decks alone.

On the ventilator

Heuer's cause list for hyperventilation on A/C is anxiety, fear, pain, brain injury, fever, or hypoxemia driving a high trigger rate; his fix is switch to SIMV mode, add deadspace, or (Table 11-5) decrease the set rate (keeping it ≥ 8/min), add mechanical deadspace, or decrease VT if it is above 8 mL/kg. His worked NBRC item: a patient on VC SIMV, rate 14, VT giving 6–7 mL/kg, PaCO2 low with respiratory alkalosis, PaO2 115 on FiO2 0.40 → decrease the rate, because the VT is already appropriate and the problem is ventilation, not oxygenation. During weaning, hyperventilation is a sign of weaning failure, and the grave mistake is cutting support to "normalize" the PaCO2 — if the ↓PaCO2 is a response to hypoxia, pain, anxiety, metabolic acidosis, or improper settings, reducing support will worsen the hyperventilation, fatigue the muscles, and cause ventilatory failure. Find and fix the cause first.

SourceHeuer Ch 1 — ICP normals and causes of decreased ICP (PDF p52) · Heuer Ch 11 — Table 11-5 Altering pH and PaCO2 (PDF p538-539) · Heuer Ch 20 — Head Trauma/TBI respiratory management and PPHN "Do Not Recommend" (heuer_full.txt, unpaginated) · Heuer Ch 19/App A — NBRC item on VC SIMV hyperventilation (heuer_full.txt) · RCP 105 Wk 1 Lctr 3 (Principles of Mech Vent, Chang/Hess), slide 48 · RCP 105 Wk 7 (Critical Issues in Mech Vent, Chang Ch 15 HIE/TBI), slide 22 · RCP 105 Mgmt of Mech Vent (Chang Ch 12), slides 23-24 · RCP 105 Wk 3 (Weaning), slide 26 · RCP 105 Monitoring in Mech Vent (Chang Ch 9), slide 30

6Weaning Parameters, SBT Protocol, and Extubation Criteria

Two framing principles: "We don't wean the ventilator — we wean the patient," and "No single value determines weaning readiness." The best predictor of weaning success is improvement of the underlying disease process. Heuer states the same and adds a caution the decks lack: VC and MIP/NIF are less frequently obtained and are used primarily to track respiratory muscle strength in neuromuscular patients, not as the deciding weaning test — a carefully monitored SBT provides the most valid information. The Weaning deck groups the textbook criteria into four concepts: Clinical Readiness, Ventilatory Readiness (RSBI, RR, VT, VE), Oxygenation Readiness (P/F, FiO2, PEEP), and Pulmonary Reserve (VC, MIP/NIF, compliance, VD/VT).

Heuer's five formal criteria to even START a weaning assessment: (1) evidence of some reversal of the underlying cause of respiratory failure; (2) adequate oxygenation — P/F ≥ 150–200, PEEP ≤ 5–8 cmH2O, FiO2 ≤ 0.4–0.5; (3) pH ≥ 7.25; (4) hemodynamic stability (no myocardial ischemia, no significant hypotension); (5) capability to initiate an inspiratory effort.

ParameterNormal — HeuerNormal — decksThreshold / critical value
RSBI (f/VT in L)< 50 is the reference range—> 105 predicts weaning failure (Heuer, both Table 1-15 and Table 4-6; elsewhere Heuer also uses RSBI < 100 as the "no distress" screen). Chang deck: < 100 breaths/min/L; the deck's summary line uses < 105. RSBI = Rate ÷ Size. Measure off the vent for 3 min or until stable; VT must be in LITERS. Low = good. Rapid shallow breathing increases deadspace ventilation because anatomic VD is fixed while VT falls. RR and RSBI are the BEST indicators of tolerance of spontaneous breathing (both sources agree)
MIP / NIF−80 to −100 cmH2O (Table 1-15); −80 to −120 cmH2O (Table 4-6)−80 to −100 cmH2O (Weaning deck); > −60 cmH2O (RCP 203 deck)Critical: 0 to −20 or −25 cmH2O (Heuer, i.e. weaker than −20/−25 = cannot sustain spontaneous ventilation). Weaning deck: < −20 to −30 cmH2O in 20 sec; Chang's text at least −30; RCP 203 gives a minimum of −20 on vent patients to be extubated. Effort-dependent; more negative = stronger. Heuer's technique: a manometer on a one-way valve that permits exhalation but not inspiration, so the patient "bucks down" toward residual volume over 15–25 seconds (decks say 20 sec) — maximum effort is ensured, and the patient need not be conscious. Disease targets (RCP 203): > −40 for COPD, > −20 for myasthenia gravis. Studies: MIP averaged −37 in weaned vs −30 in non-weaned
MEP (maximum expiratory pressure)measured with a manometer, no one-way valve, and only on an alert, cooperative patient—> 60 cmH2O is Heuer's threshold for adequate secretion clearance before extubation (paired with peak cough flow > 60 L/min). In neuromuscular disease Heuer flags MEP < 40 cmH2O (or rapidly declining) and peak cough flow < 270 L/min (4.5 L/sec) as danger signs. The decks give no MEP threshold
VC (vital capacity)70 mL/kg PBW (slow VC)50 mL/kg IBW (~4.8 L) = IRV 3.1 + VT 0.5 + ERV 1.2 L; RCP 203 gives 3–5 L typical adultCritical < 10–15 mL/kg PBW (Heuer); decks say > 10 mL/kg to wean, with RCP 203's critical minimum ~1 L (15 mL/kg). Effort-dependent, requires cooperation. Heuer's technique: slow VC — inhale as deeply as possible, then exhale slowly and completely until no volume change for at least 2 seconds, repeated at least 3 times, best result recorded. Heuer's App B ladder: < 300 = acute lung injury, < 200 = severe hypoxemia, < 100 = ARDS; the decks' ladder: < 300 ALI/mild ARDS, < 200 ARDS, < 150 prone. Worked RSBI example — Heuer Ch 3 and the Chang deck use the identical numbers:

RSBI = f / VT(L) Patient breathing spontaneously at 38/min with an average VT of 300 mL: RSBI = 38 / 0.3 RSBI = 127 breaths/min/L -> ABOVE 105 = predicts WEANING FAILURE Heuer's companion problem (same patient, from VE instead of VT): VT = VE / f = 11.4 L/min / 38 breaths/min = 0.30 L = 300 mL

SBT protocol

The SBT is the major diagnostic test and the current standard — daily SBTs provide the quickest route to discontinuing mechanical ventilation (Heuer's exact phrase), and SIMV should be avoided as a stand-alone weaning modality. Heuer's sequence, which the decks compress:

  1. Screen with the five weaning-assessment criteria above.
  2. A brief supervised screening period of 2–5 minutes of monitored spontaneous breathing, watching breathing pattern, vital signs, and comfort (RR < 30–35, stable vitals, patient comfortable).
  3. If tolerated, continue the SBT for at least 30 minutes but no more than 120 minutes. (RCP 203 gives the same 30–120 min; the Chang Weaning deck says "up to 30 minutes" via ventilator or Briggs adaptor/T-tube, and notes patients who fail usually do so within the first 20–30 minutes, so a full 120-minute trial is often unnecessary.)
  4. Four equivalent methods, no one better than another (both sources agree): straight T-tube, CPAP (~5 cmH2O), pressure support (5–8 cmH2O to offset the ET tube), or CPAP + PS; the decks add automatic tube compensation (ATC). Heuer's tiebreakers: CPAP helps trigger in patients with auto-PEEP, and PS overcomes the imposed WOB of the artificial airway.

Pass criteria (Heuer Ch 4 and Ch 11, matching RCP 203 nearly word for word): SpO2 ≥ 85–90% or PaO2 ≥ 50–60 torr; pH ≥ 7.30; rise in PaCO2 ≤ 10 torr; HR < 120–140/min with a change < 20% from baseline; SBP < 180–200 mmHg and > 90 mmHg with a change < 20%; no vasopressors required; RR ≤ 30–35/min with a change < 50% from baseline.

Subjective failure indicators that stop the trial even when the numbers look fine (Heuer): change in mental status (somnolence, coma, agitation, anxiety), onset or worsening of discomfort, diaphoresis, and signs of increased WOB — accessory muscle use and thoracoabdominal paradox. The decks add tachypnea, tachycardia, hypertension, hypotension, hypoxemia, acidosis, arrhythmias. On failure: return the patient to a support level sufficient to maintain oxygenation and ventilation and prevent muscle fatigue, work with the physician to identify and correct the cause, rest for 24 hours, and repeat the SBT every 24 hours. Only 13% of patients who pass an SBT and are extubated need reintubation (deck).

Extubation criteria. Heuer is explicit that the decision to extubate is made SEPARATELY from the weaning assessment. His four requirements:

  1. Adequate oxygenation and ventilation without support — demonstrated by the SBT.
  2. Minimal risk of upper airway obstruction — demonstrated by a positive cuff-leak test.
  3. Adequate airway protection — positive gag reflex, presence of a cough reflex, and the ability to raise the head off the bed.
  4. Effective secretion clearance — a deep cough on suctioning and, if alert, MEP > 60 cmH2O and/or peak cough flow > 60 L/min.
The cuff-leak test — Heuer's version is QUALITATIVE, not quantitative

Heuer's method: remove secretions above the cuff, fully deflate the cuff, and completely occlude the tube at its outlet. If leakage occurs during spontaneous breathing, that is a "positive" test and the airway is likely patent. A quantitative cuff-leak threshold (a leak volume in mL or a percentage of delivered VT) is Not covered in Heuer or the available decks — the only quantitative extubation number either source supplies is Heuer's MEP > 60 cmH2O / peak cough flow > 60 L/min for secretion clearance, and the airway deck contains only an unlabeled algorithm figure (Hess Figure 17-52, "Algorithm to address issues with an artificial airway cuff leak").

Checked in Hess (2026-09-20): Hess describes the test but does NOT print a numeric threshold either. Hess Ch 17: qualitatively, deflate the cuff and assess for a leak through the upper airway; quantitatively, "the leak can be assessed by measuring the volume of the leak or the percentage of the tidal volume that leaks" — the method, but no cut-off. Hess is also notably sceptical: "The value of the leak test is controversial because false positives and false negatives are common," and it warns the test increases the risk of aspiration, because secretions on the superior surface of the cuff have a clear path to the lower airway with the cuff down. Its recommendation is therefore selective: reserve the cuff-leak test for patients at high risk of post-extubation stridor — traumatic intubation, intubation more than 6 days, a large endotracheal tube, female sex, and reintubation after unplanned extubation. And a practical sequencing point: when upper-airway oedema is suspected, prophylactic corticosteroids decrease the risk of post-extubation stridor and reintubation, and the leak test does not need to be repeated after steroid administration. ⚠ So the numeric threshold (commonly quoted as < 110 mL or < 10–15% of delivered VT) is absent from Heuer, the decks AND Hess — if your instructor wants a number, ask which one.

Heuer's extubation procedure (numbers to know): equipment = suction, BVM, bland-aerosol mask or cannula, an SVN with racemic epinephrine available, and an intubation tray. Steps: place the patient upright (semi-Fowler's or higher); suction the tube and pharynx above the cuff; give 100% O2 by bag for 1–2 minutes; fully deflate the cuff, remove the securing device, insert a new catheter, have the patient cough while you apply suction and pull the tube; then cool humidified O2 at a higher FiO2 than before extubation; assess breath sounds, WOB, vital signs; if stridor develops, give cool humidified O2 and recommend aerosolized racemic epinephrine; start bronchial hygiene/directed coughing; NPO except sips of water for 24 hours.

Reintubation and other deck points

Weaning failure also means the need for reintubation within 48 hours of extubation, caused by hypoventilation and hypercapnia, respiratory acidosis, rapid shallow breathing, excessive secretions, and disuse atrophy. After neuromuscular blockade, confirm reversal with a 5-second head lift, 5-second tongue protrusion, hand grip, PaO2 > 80 mmHg, PaCO2 < 45 mmHg, an adequate MIP and VC — but do not discontinue support until the diaphragm can provide adequate ventilation. Prophylactic corticosteroids decrease the risk of post-extubation stridor and reintubation. A patient extubated onto NIV/BiPAP is "weaning in progress," not weaning failure — and Heuer agrees, listing "provide NPPV" as the action to avoid reintubation in patients who develop mild-to-moderate post-extubation distress.

SourceHeuer Ch 1 — Ventilator Liberation Parameters and Table 1-15 Adult Respiratory Monitoring Thresholds (PDF p60-64) · Heuer Ch 3 — Mechanics of Spontaneous Ventilation, RSBI/VC/MIP technique (PDF p140-144) and Spontaneous Breathing Trials (PDF p166-167) · Heuer Ch 4 — Interpreting Bedside Ventilation Measures, Table 4-6 (PDF p208-211) and Evaluating SBTs (PDF p233-234) · Heuer Ch 9 — Perform Extubation and the cuff-leak test (PDF p473-474, key points p468) · Heuer Ch 11 — Liberating a Patient from Mechanical Ventilation (PDF p572-577) · Heuer Ch 13 — Extubation readiness criteria and MEP/peak cough flow (heuer_full.txt) · Heuer App B — P/F and shunt ballpark rules (PDF p1041-1042) · RCP 105 Wk 3 (Weaning/Extubation, Chang Ch 13/16), slides 5-7, 9-20, 22, 24-30, 50 · RCP 203 Lssn 2b (Perform Procedures/Gather Clinical Info), slides 15-19, 44-45 · RCP 203 Lssn 3a (Evaluate Procedure Results), slides 59-60 · RCP 105 Wk 1 Lctr 2 (Airway Mgmt, Hess Ch 17), slides 64, 90-91

7Making Ventilator Changes from an ABG

The organizing rule: CO2 problem = change VENTILATION (rate and/or VT); O2 problem = change OXYGENATION (FiO2 and/or PEEP). "Would increasing FiO2 lower the PaCO2? No — FiO2 affects oxygenation, not ventilation." Heuer's targets: pH 7.35–7.45 in most cases, but you may accept as low as 7.25 or as high as 7.50 depending on the underlying problem, and normalizing the pH may leave the PaCO2 abnormal — a COPD patient with chronic retention may reach a normal pH at a PaCO2 of 55 torr or higher, while an uncorrected DKA patient may need a lower-than-normal PaCO2.

Rate first, volume second (both sources agree)

Heuer: first adjust the set rate, keeping it between 8 and 24/min (up to 35/min in acute lung injury); only if rate changes alone will not normalize the pH do you alter the delivered VT, and then keep VT between 4 and 8 mL/kg PBW with Pplat ≤ 30–35 cmH2O. If pH is still below 7.30 with rate, VT, and Pplat at their high limits, use permissive hypercapnia — let the PaCO2 rise as long as the pH stays above 7.20–7.25. The Chang Management deck says the same: increasing frequency is the most common approach to regulate PaCO2, VT is second choice because the adjustable VT range is narrow and excessive VT causes ventilator-induced lung injury — increase VT only when the frequency is already too high for the patient's breathing pattern and I:E ratio.

Mode-specific changes (Heuer Table 11-5):

ModeTo ↓ PaCO2 / ↑ pHTo ↑ PaCO2 / ↓ pH
VC A/C↑ set rate (keep ≤ 24/min); ↑ set VT (keep Pplat < 30–35)↓ set rate (keep ≥ 8/min); add mechanical deadspace; ↓ VT if > 8 mL/kg
VC SIMV↑ set rate (≤ 24/min); ↑ set VT (Pplat < 30); add/increase pressure support↓ set rate (≥ 6–8/min); ↓ set VT; ↓ pressure support
PC A/C↑ set rate (≤ 24/min); ↑ ΔP (PIP − PEEP)↓ set rate (≥ 6–8/min); ↓ ΔP
PC SIMV↑ set rate (≤ 24/min); ↑ ΔP; add/increase PS↓ set rate (≥ 6–8/min); ↓ ΔP; ↓ PS
APRV↑ release frequency (↓Thigh); ↑ ΔP (Phigh − Plow)↓ release frequency (↑Thigh); ↓ ΔP

Oxygenation

Heuer's target: PaO2 ≥ 65 torr and SaO2 ≥ 92% for most patients, but as low as PaO2 55 torr in COPD or ARDS as long as SaO2 stays ≥ 88%. His decision rule is the "60-60 rule" (a "50-50 rule" works too): if PaO2 > 60 torr on FiO2 < 0.6, the problem is V/Q imbalance → simply raise the FiO2; if PaO2 < 60 torr on FiO2 > 0.6, the problem is shunt → PEEP/CPAP must be added or increased. Restated as his general guideline: if an acceptable PaO2/SaO2 can be held on less than 50% O2, adjust FiO2; if it cannot, adjust the baseline pressure (PEEP, CPAP, Plow). If the P/F ratio is ≤ 200, or 50%+ O2 with PEEP ≥ 12 cmH2O still cannot give acceptable values, recommend the NHLBI ARDS protocol. Weaning order: drop FiO2 to ≤ 0.50 FIRST, then lower PEEP. Also confirm Hb ≥ 10 g/dL and adequate circulation before blaming the lungs.

The decks' oxygenation list: increase FiO2, improve ventilation and reduce mechanical deadspace, improve circulation, maintain a normal Hgb, initiate CPAP, initiate PEEP (the number-one ventilator adjustment for improving oxygenation), inverse ratio ventilation, HFOV.

THE FORMULAS

The decks print only the desired-rate version. Heuer Ch 13 ("Improving Alveolar Ventilation") supplies the desired-VE version, and because PaCO2 is inversely proportional to alveolar ventilation, the same proportion drives all three. Heuer's warning: be careful specifying the desired PaCO2 — the goal is the PaCO2 that normalizes the pH, which in an acute-on-chronic COPD patient may be substantially higher than 40 torr. And: if the VT is already properly set at 6–8 mL/kg PBW with Pplat < 30 cmH2O, rate changes are the preferred way to change VE.

============================================================ DESIRED MINUTE VENTILATION (Heuer, Ch 13) Known VE x Known PaCO2 New VE = ------------------------------ Desired PaCO2 WORKED EXAMPLE Patient: VT 500 mL at rate 12 -> Known VE = 6.0 L/min ABG PaCO2 = 55 mmHg, you want PaCO2 = 40 mmHg 6.0 x 55 330 New VE = ----------- = ------ = 8.25 L/min 40 40 You must raise VE from 6.0 to 8.25 L/min (an increase of 2.25 L/min) ============================================================ DESIRED RATE (Chang Mgmt deck, slide 4) Known f x Known PaCO2 New f = ------------------------------ Desired PaCO2 WORKED EXAMPLE (the deck's own numbers) On rate 14, ABG PaCO2 = 60 mmHg, target PaCO2 = 40 mmHg 14 x 60 840 New f = ----------- = ------- = 21 40 40 New f = 21 -> increase the set rate from 14 to 21/min CHECK IT: 21 is above Heuer's ceiling of 8-24/min but still legal; if it had come out above 24 (or 35 in ALI), you would raise VT instead. ============================================================ DESIRED TIDAL VOLUME (same proportion, rate held constant) Known VT x Known PaCO2 New VT = ------------------------------ Desired PaCO2 WORKED EXAMPLE 70-kg PBW patient, VT 450 mL at rate 20 (rate already near the ceiling), ABG PaCO2 = 58 mmHg, target PaCO2 = 45 mmHg 450 x 58 26,100 New VT = ------------ = --------- = 580 mL 45 45 SAFETY CHECK: 580 mL / 70 kg = 8.3 mL/kg PBW -- just OVER Heuer's 4-8 mL/kg ceiling. Verify Pplat stays <= 30 cmH2O; if it does not, accept permissive hypercapnia instead (pH may sit at 7.20-7.25). ============================================================
Sanity checks from Heuer's App B ballpark rulesan adult VE at normal rates should land between 4 and 10 L/min, and if you compute a VT below 200 mL or above 1000 mL for an adult, recheck your math.

Then apply the four ABG scenarios the decks drill, because the trap is treating the number instead of the patient:

ABGTypical interpretationCorrect action
pH 7.52, PaCO2 30, HCO3− 24Acute respiratory alkalosisOnly reduce f/VT/PS if the cause is over-ventilation from an improper setting (Heuer: decrease the rate, add deadspace, or switch A/C to SIMV). If the ↓PaCO2 is a response to hypoxia, metabolic acidosis, pain, or anxiety, do NOT reduce support — further hyperventilation, muscle fatigue, and ventilatory failure are likely. Fix the hypoxia, treat the acidosis, sedate for anxiety
pH 7.30, PaCO2 50, HCO3− 24Acute respiratory acidosisUsual action is ↑ f, VT, or PS — but the same picture occurs with oversedation or muscle fatigue from a premature weaning attempt, in which case increasing the frequency is NOT the right action; correct the cause and delay weaning
pH 7.47, PaCO2 46, HCO3− 32 (COPD)Reads like "partially compensated metabolic alkalosis"It is really acute respiratory alkalosis on top of the COPD patient's compensated respiratory acidosis, from acute hypoxia or improper settings. Fix the cause and the gas returns to his baseline pH 7.39, PaCO2 55, HCO3− 32
Any primary metabolic problemMetabolic acidosis or alkalosisDo NOT adjust the ventilator to compensate for metabolic acid-base problems. Do not increase support for metabolic acidosis; do not decrease it for metabolic alkalosis. Treat the cause: renal failure, DKA, lactic acidosis; for alkalosis, hypokalemia

Other numbers

Eucapnic ventilation = PaCO2 near 40 mmHg, or near 50 mmHg in a CO2 retainer. Excessive frequency shortens E-time and causes air trapping (real risk at RR 30 in COPD: auto-PEEP, hyperinflation, ↑WOB, hypotension) — Heuer's guard against this is set flow/I-time for an I:E ≤ 1:1, e.g. 1:2 or 1:3. VT is decreased by 100–200 mL in COPD to lengthen expiratory time (deck).

Permissive hypercapnia = deliberate hypoventilation at VT 4–7 mL/kg letting PaCO2 stay > 50 mmHg; the decks manage the resulting acidosis with bicarbonate or tromethamine (THAM), Heuer simply holds pH > 7.20–7.25. Other deck strategies to improve ventilation: increase spontaneous VT with pressure support (better than increasing spontaneous rate, which risks rapid shallow breathing), change modes (PSV, ATC), use a low-compressible-volume circuit, use the largest appropriate ET tube, remove secretions, reduce mechanical deadspace.

SourceHeuer Ch 13 — Improving Alveolar Ventilation, desired-VE formula and the 60-60 rule (heuer_full.txt, unpaginated) · Heuer Ch 11 — Normalizing the pH and Table 11-5, Normalizing Oxygenation (PDF p537-540) · Heuer App B — Table B-1 Ventilatory Parameters and ballpark rules (PDF p1037-1039) · RCP 105 Mgmt of Mech Vent (Chang Ch 12), slides 4-12, 15-19, 21-27 · RCP 105 Wk 1 Lctr 5 (Initiation of Mech Vent, Chang Ch 8), slides 12-13, 17-18 · RCP 105 Wk 5 (Procedures Related to Mech Vent, Chang Ch 14/15), slide 36 · RCP 105 Monitoring in Mech Vent (Chang Ch 9), slide 30

8Static and Dynamic Compliance — Formulas, Normal Ranges, and Reading Pplat

Compliance = ΔV ÷ ΔP — "how much volume you get for a given pressure." The only difference between the two versions is which pressure goes in the denominator: Pplat for static, PIP for dynamic. Heuer frames it as the simplified equation of motion: the driving pressure has two components — the pressure to overcome elastic recoil of lungs and thorax (VT/Crs), and the pressure to overcome airway resistance (flow × Raw) — and an end-inspiratory pause during volume control "partitions" them apart so you can compute both.

STATIC COMPLIANCE (Cstat / Crs / CLT) DYNAMIC COMPLIANCE (Cdyn) VT VT Cstat = ───────────── Cdyn = ───────────── Pplat − PEEP PIP − PEEP Lung + chest wall stiffness ONLY Total impedance: compliance AND resistance (zero flow -> no resistance component) (flow still moving -> PTA included) AIRWAY RESISTANCE Raw = (PIP − Pplat) / Flow in L/sec (convert: L/min ÷ 60 = L/sec)

Heuer's key relationship: because (PIP − PEEP) is always greater than (Pplat − PEEP) during volume control, Cdyn is ALWAYS less than Crs, and the gap (Crs − Cdyn) is entirely due to airway resistance — the bigger the gap, the bigger resistance's contribution to the driving pressure.

Normal ranges — THREE different answers, know all of them:

MeasureRCP 105 Principles / Waveform decksRCP 203 Lssn 3aHeuer
Static compliance (adult)60–100 mL/cmH2O, normal around 80 mL/cmH2O40–80 mL/cmH2O for intubated adults40–80 mL/cmH2O for intubated adult patients (Ch 4) — matching RCP 203 exactly. But Heuer's own App B ballpark rule says expect CLT to range from 100 mL/cmH2O (normal) down to 10 mL/cmH2O (severe reduction, as in ARDS) — an internal inconsistency in the text, so cite the 40–80 intubated range when the question says "intubated"
Danger zone< 25–30 mL/cmH2O = increased WOB, weaning difficultysame< 25–30 mL/cmH2O — all three agree
Weaning minimum> 30 mL/cmH2O> 30 mL/cmH2O(implied by the < 25–30 danger zone)
Airway resistance (Raw)0.6–2.4 cmH2O/L/sec (Weaning deck, Burton — a non-intubated normal, and the deck notes it runs higher in ventilated patients because of tube and circuit resistance)5–12 cmH2O/L/sec for intubated adults5–12 cmH2O/L/sec for intubated adults (Ch 4); 5–15 cmH2O/L/sec for orally intubated patients (App B ballpark), expect higher in airway obstruction

Heuer's two worked problems (App B, Table B-3):

PROBLEM B.14 -- STATIC COMPLIANCE VC ventilation, VT 700 mL, PEEP 8 cmH2O, PIP 42 cmH2O, Pplat 35 cmH2O CLT = VT / (Pplat - PEEP) CLT = 700 / (35 - 8) CLT = 700 / 27 CLT = 26 mL/cmH2O <- well under 40; stiff lungs, weaning risk (note the PIP of 42 is a decoy here) PROBLEM B.15 -- AIRWAY RESISTANCE VC ventilation, VT 400 mL, inspiratory flow 75 L/min, PEEP 6, PIP 45 cmH2O, Pplat 30 cmH2O Convert flow: 75 L/min / 60 = 1.25 L/sec Raw = (PIP - Pplat) / flow Raw = (45 - 30) / 1.25 Raw = 15 / 1.25 Raw = 12 cmH2O/L/sec <- top of the 5-12 normal range for an intubated adult; borderline high
How to read Pplat off a pressure-time curve

Apply an inspiratory pause / end-inspiratory breath hold — Heuer specifies 0.5–2.0 seconds during volume-targeted ventilation, using a square inspiratory flow pattern, and warns that bedside mechanics require complete relaxation of the respiratory muscles plus 2–3 seconds of zero flow for airway and alveolar pressures to equilibrate, which sometimes requires sedation or paralysis. Airflow stops, the resistive pressure component disappears, and the trace drops from PIP to a flat shelf — that shelf is the plateau pressure (Pplat, also PALV).

The vertical distance between PIP and Pplat is PTA (transairway pressure) = Flow × Resistance, and the distance between Pplat and PEEP is the elastic/compliance component. Under pressure control, Pplat equals the set PIP only if flow has completely ceased before end-inspiration.

Two accuracy warnings from the waveform deck: if the patient exhales during the pause you see an erratic RISE and a falsely high Pplat; if the patient inhales during the pause you see an erratic DROP and a falsely low Pplat. Special case: in a TRUE descending ramp flow waveform, flow reaches zero at end-inspiration on its own, so PTA = 0 and PIP = PALV = Pplat — under those conditions you may compute static compliance from PIP.

Worked example from the waveform deck (slide 26):

From the flow/volume-time waveform: VT = Flow x TI VT = 60 L/min (= 1 L/sec) x 0.5 sec VT = 0.5 L = 500 mL From the pressure/time waveform: true descending ramp -> flow = 0 at end-insp therefore PIP = PALV = Pplat = 35 cmH2O Compliance = VT / PALV = 500 / 35 = 14.3 mL/cmH2O INTERPRETATION: 14.3 is far below normal (60-100 per the Chang decks, 40-80 intubated per Heuer/RCP 203) -- a stiff, non-compliant system.

Falling Cstat vs falling Cdyn — the diagnostic payoff (Heuer Ch 4 states this most cleanly):

  • Both fall proportionately, Crs − Cdyn unchanged = a pure COMPLIANCE problem. In VC you see ↑PIP, ↑Pplat, NO change in PIP − Pplat, ↓Cdyn, and a decreased slope of the P-V loop. In PC you see ↓VT and a decreased P-V loop slope. Causes of an acute fall in Crs (Heuer's list): atelectasis, pulmonary edema/CHF, ARDS, tension pneumothorax, pleural effusion, overdistention/hyperinflation, ET tube slippage into a mainstem bronchus, bronchial obstruction by a mucus plug, ascites/abdominal distention.
  • Cdyn falls while Cstat is unchanged, so the Crs − Cdyn gap WIDENS = a pure RESISTANCE problem. In VC you see ↑PIP, ↑(PIP − Pplat), NO change in Pplat, ↓Cdyn, and increased WIDTH of the P-V loop. In PC you see ↓VT — but only if flow continues to the end of the breath (VT is unchanged if flow ceases early) — and a wider P-V loop. Causes of an acute rise in Raw: bronchospasm, excessive secretions, peribronchial/airway edema and swelling, partial artificial airway occlusion (mucus plug, kinking, biting).
  • Improvements produce exactly the opposite changes.

Heuer's Box 4-5 trend problem, which is the classic exam item:

Over 4 hours: Pplat - PEEP stays constant -> Crs UNCHANGED at 20 mL/cmH2O PIP - Pplat rises 20 -> 35 -> Raw INCREASING DIAGNOSIS: pure resistance problem. LOOK FOR: bronchospasm, excessive secretions, airway edema, partial artificial airway occlusion (mucus plug, kinking, biting).

When to measure compliance

Static compliance, airway resistance, and VD/VT are the three measurements that do NOT depend on patient cooperation or effort — unlike VC and MIP — which is exactly why they indicate the pulmonary workload needed to support spontaneous ventilation. Low compliance, high Raw, and a high VD/VT all raise the workload and, past the patient's threshold, hinder weaning. So measure it (1) as a weaning parameter (> 30 mL/cmH2O), (2) whenever the patient suddenly becomes harder to ventilate — always ask "has compliance decreased?" and "has resistance increased?" — and (3) to track response to therapy (a more vertical P-V loop after PEEP or surfactant means compliance improved).

Heuer adds a fourth use the decks lack: serial Crs measurement is the engine of the decremental PEEP study (see item 9).

SourceHeuer Ch 3 — Pulmonary Compliance and Airway Resistance, Plateau Pressure (PDF p161-165) · Heuer Ch 4 — Pulmonary Compliance and Airway Resistance During Mechanical Ventilation and Box 4-5 (PDF p229-232) · Heuer App B — Table B-3 Pulmonary Mechanics, Problems B.14/B.15 (PDF p1042-1043) · RCP 105 Wk 1 Lctr 3 (Principles of Mech Vent, Chang/Hess), slides 7, 12-14 · RCP 105 Wk 6 (Waveform Analysis, Chang Ch 11), slides 8-10, 25-26, 41, 65 · RCP 203 Lssn 3a (Evaluate Procedure Results), slides 52-56 · RCP 203 Lssn 2b, slides 37-39 · RCP 105 Wk 3 (Weaning), slide 17
06

Airways, Intubation, Chest Tubes & Chest X-ray

7 items · Intubation equipment and process · airways and when to use them · tube size and cuff pressure · chest tube drainage · comparing the chest x-ray · treating pleural effusions

1Intubation Equipment and the Intubation Process2Airway Devices Compared3Tube Size, Cuff Pressure, and the Two Inflation Techniques4ETT Sizes by Patient and the Pediatric Formulas5The Chest Tube Drainage System6Comparing the Chest X-ray7Treating Pleural Effusions — Acute and Chronic

1Intubation Equipment and the Intubation Process

Equipment checklist (Heuer Ch 6 + Ch 16). Laryngoscope handle with fresh batteries and blades (Macintosh and Miller, sizes 00 to 4), ETTs (2 to 10 mm ID) with the selected size plus one size larger and one size smaller, a 10-mL syringe to test and inflate the cuff, water-soluble lubricant, a stylet (oral only — never for nasal; the tip must never extend past the ETT tip), Magill forceps (required for the nasal route), suction with a Yankauer tip, a bag-valve-mask with 100% O₂, topical anesthetic (2% tetracaine spray, or nebulized 4% lidocaine for elective cases), colorimetric CO₂ detector or waveform capnography, tape or a commercial holder, a bite block, and a stethoscope. Difficult-airway backup: bougie (tracheal tube introducer), lighted stylet/light wand, intubating LMA (Fastrach), video laryngoscope, fiberoptic bronchoscope, and supraglottic airways.

Macintosh vs Miller

The Macintosh is the curved blade: its tip goes into the vallecula (the pocket between the base of the tongue and the epiglottis), and lifting the handle lifts the tongue, which raises the epiglottis indirectly. The Miller is the straight blade: it is passed under the epiglottis and lifts it directly. The lecture deck also lists the Wisconsin as a third (straight) blade and describes the Miller as "straight with a slightly curved tip."

Heuer says only that you "may choose Macintosh or Miller depending on preference" — the vallecula-vs-epiglottis technique detail comes from the deck.

Step-by-step (Heuer Box 9-3, adult orotracheal)

1. Test the laryngoscope light and the ETT cuff (inflate and watch for deflation, or immerse in sterile water). 2. Lubricate the ETT/stylet. 3. Position in the sniffing position and suction the oropharynx. 4. Topical anesthetic if desired. 5. Hyperoxygenate 1–2 minutes. 6. Insert the blade, expose and lift the epiglottis, visualize the cords. 7. Advance the ETT until the cuff disappears 2–3 cm past the cords.

8. Inflate the cuff to 20–30 cm H₂O. 9. Ventilate with 100% O₂. 10. Auscultate the chest for symmetrical breath sounds and the epigastrium for absent gurgling. 11. Verify with breath sounds, chest movement, and CO₂ detection/capnography. 12. Secure the tube and record the cm mark at the incisors. 13. Confirm position by chest x-ray. Each attempt should last no more than 20 seconds (Heuer Ch 9) — Heuer Ch 16, describing the RT assisting a physician, says no more than 30 seconds — with 1–2 minutes of oxygenation between attempts.

Confirmation of placement

During resuscitation the AHA (per Heuer) wants continuous quantitative waveform capnography; a colorimetric CO₂ detector is the acceptable alternative, confirmed by a purple → yellow/tan color change sustained over six or more breaths (2.0–5.0% exhaled CO₂, about 15–38 torr). Beware the false negative in cardiac arrest (no pulmonary blood flow) and the false positive with a mainstem intubation. Epigastric gurgling = esophageal intubation → deflate, pull the tube, reoxygenate, reintubate.

Decreased breath sounds/chest movement on the left = right mainstem → withdraw slowly until bilateral. Heuer is blunt that there are only two ways to confirm the tube is properly above the carina: chest x-ray or fiberoptic laryngoscopy — breath sounds and CO₂ prove the tube is in the airway, not that its depth is right.

Depth at the teeth/lip and the carina rule. Average oral placement from teeth/incisors to tube tip: 21–23 cm in adult males and 19–21 cm in adult females; for the nasotracheal route add 2 cm. On the film the ETT tip should sit about 4–6 cm above the carina, usually between T2 and T4, with the head neutral — the tube travels up and down as the neck flexes and extends, so always read head position with the film. Too deep → right mainstem; too shallow → accidental extubation.

Recognizing the difficult airway first

Predict difficult BVM ventilation with M-O-A-N-S (Mask seal, Obesity/Obstruction, Aged, No teeth, Stiff lungs) and difficult intubation with L-E-M-O-N (Look externally, Evaluate external anatomy, Mallampati, Obesity/Obstruction, Neck mobility). Mallampati Class I–II = easy, Class III = difficult, Class IV = very difficult (hard palate only). On direct laryngoscopy the Cormack-Lehane Grade III view shows epiglottis only and Grade IV shows no epiglottis — Grade IV carries over a 90% likelihood of difficult intubation.

SourceHeuer Ch 9 — Recognition of a Difficult Airway / Establishing and Managing a Patient's Airway (PDF p429–435, p448–451) · Heuer Ch 6 — Intubation Equipment, Table 6-25 (PDF p332–333) · Heuer Ch 16 — Assisting with Endotracheal Intubation (PDF p731–734) · RCP 105 Wk 1 Lctr 2 — Airway Management (Hess Ch 17), slides 34, 36, 38–39, 42–44, 48–55

2Airway Devices Compared

DeviceWhat it isWhen to useKey caution / contraindication
OPA (oropharyngeal airway)Rigid plastic airway that curves over the tongue and ends at its base, holding the tongue off the posterior pharyngeal wall. Sized by length: 40 mm (00) premature → 100 mm (5) large adult; 80 mm (3) average adult female, 90 mm (4) average adult male. Size by measuring corner of the mouth to the angle of the jaw (or to the earlobe). Insert tip-up (180°) or from the side, then rotate to midline.Comatose/obtunded patients with tongue obstruction; to make BVM ventilation effective; as a bite block in an intubated patient; during seizures.Not for conscious patients — gagging, vomiting, aspiration. Too large occludes the airway (and can cause gastric insufflation); too small pushes the tongue back and obstructs. Remove immediately if the patient gags. Do not tape over the center opening (a suction catheter passes through it).
NPA (nasopharyngeal airway)Soft tube passed along the floor of the nose (inferior meatus) with the tip behind the tongue above the epiglottis. #6 (24 Fr) for the average female, #7 (28 Fr) for the average male; 32–36 Fr for a large adult. Bevel faces the septum (medially).The alternative to an OPA when the gag reflex is intact; seizures/trismus preventing mouth opening; to reduce trauma from repeated nasotracheal suctioning; to guide a bronchoscope.Must be lubricated with water-soluble jelly and passed without force. Avoid with nasal trauma, polyps, or adenoid hypertrophy. Never cut an NPA — the cut edge is sharp. Complications: epistaxis, mucosal trauma, gagging, and sinusitis with prolonged use. If it will not pass, try the other naris, then a smaller size.
LMA (laryngeal mask airway)Supraglottic tube plus an inflatable mask that seals over the laryngeal inlet, with a standard 15-mm connector. Sized by weight: 1 (<5 kg) → 6 (>100 kg); size 4 for a 50–70 kg adult (max 30 mL), size 5 for 70–100 kg (max 40 mL).Rescue airway for emergency ventilation, and the fallback in the "can ventilate, can't intubate" scenario (LMA or intubating LMA, then fiberoptic-assisted intubation).Inflate to 60 cm H₂O — never exceed 60 cm H₂O (malposition, tissue damage). Contraindicated if aspiration risk is high, and in conscious patients with an intact gag reflex or with trauma/obstructive lesions of the mouth or pharynx. It is not a definitive airway — no aspiration protection, not for long-term PPV. Keep PPV pressures below 20–30 cm H₂O; use a bite block, not an OPA (an OPA malpositions it). If a leak occurs, reposition — do not just add air. If regurgitation occurs, leave the LMA in, head-down, disconnect, suction through it, prepare to intubate.
ETT (endotracheal tube)Cuffed tube with pilot balloon, radiopaque line, 15-mm connector, bevel, and Murphy eye. Variants: subglottic/Hi-Lo Evac (suction port above the cuff for CASS) and double-lumen tubes.Establish and protect the airway from aspiration, bypass upper airway obstruction, and provide short-term positive-pressure ventilation.Oral route preferred. Heuer discourages the nasal route because of higher VAP, sinusitis, and otitis media, the need for a smaller/longer tube (more airway resistance), and necrosis of the septum and naris. Cuff to 20–30 cm H₂O. If a suction catheter will not pass, the tube is kinked or displaced. Subglottic port runs at –20 to –25 mm Hg (Ch 10) / –20 to –30 cm H₂O (Ch 6) and must be flushed with air to stay patent.
Tracheostomy tubeTube through a surgical stoma into the trachea, sized by ID in mm (ISO); ISO size 5 and smaller usually cannot hold an inner cannula. Comes with an obturator (prevents "snowplowing" — remove immediately after insertion, keep at the bedside).Long-term positive-pressure ventilation, bypassing upper airway obstruction when oral/nasal intubation is not feasible, and access for frequent secretion clearance; a permanent airway. Advantages over an ETT: lower resistance, less tube movement, more comfort, easier suctioning, easier reinsertion into a mature stoma.OD should be no more than two-thirds to three-fourths of the tracheal ID — too big blocks airflow around a deflated cuff, too small forces unacceptably high cuff pressures to seal. Never pull or rock the 15-mm connector. Confirm position by x-ray or fiberoptic scope. Complications: bleeding, tissue trauma, airway loss, hypoxemia, aspiration; elective tracheotomy is deferred until coagulopathy is corrected.
Tracheostomy buttonShort cannula flanged at both ends, depth set by spacers, with a plug that forces breathing through the upper airway; a standard connector allows PPV but leaks.To maintain a patent stoma after the trach tube is removed, to facilitate weaning off the trach tube, and to keep long-term access for lower-airway suctioning.Provides no ventilatory support and no cuff. Pass a suction catheter through it regularly to confirm patency. Respiratory distress with an uncapped button means the button is protruding too far into the trachea — reposition by changing the number of spacers; confirm placement with fiberoptic bronchoscopy.
Transtracheal catheterHeuer's version of this is the percutaneous transtracheal jet-ventilation catheter: a 13- to 14-gauge angiocath driven through the cricothyroid membrane/trachea and hooked to a 50-psi jet setup (adjustable regulator + jet control valve) for manually triggered 100% O₂ ventilation.The "can't ventilate, can't intubate" rescue when hypoxemia is worsening — invasive access via cricothyrotomy or percutaneous jet ventilation. Heuer names it the surgical airway of choice in children under 12 years, whose small tracheal diameter makes cricothyrotomy hazardous.It is a temporizing oxygenation route, not a definitive airway — no cuff, no aspiration protection, and barotrauma risk from 50-psi source gas. Note the age split: a percutaneous cricothyrotomy kit (Nu-Trake, QuickTrach, Pertrach) generally requires a patient at least 5 years old. The chronic-oxygen "transtracheal oxygen catheter"/SCOOP catheter is not covered in Heuer or the available decks — neither source uses that term.
SourceHeuer Ch 9 — Establishing and Managing a Patient's Airway; Tables 9-5, 9-7, 9-9; Tracheostomy Buttons (PDF p435–437, p444–453, p460) · Heuer Ch 6 — Artificial Airways, Tables 6-26 and 6-27 (PDF p336–341) · Heuer Ch 16 — difficult-airway equipment table and Assisting with Tracheotomy (PDF p731, p743–745) · RCP 105 Wk 1 Lctr 2 — Airway Management (Hess Ch 17), slides 3–14, 30–32, 45, 56–58, 65–68, 79–80, 99–101

3Tube Size, Cuff Pressure, and the Two Inflation Techniques

Why tube size drives cuff pressure

Modern cuffs are high-volume, low-pressure so that a large, floppy cuff seals at low wall pressure. The deck states the tested cause-and-effect directly: the causes of high cuff pressure are a tube that is too small and incorrect positioning of the ETT. Heuer says the same thing from the trach side — a trach tube's OD should be no more than two-thirds to three-fourths of the tracheal ID, because a tube that is too small requires unacceptably high cuff pressures to achieve a seal, while one that is too big blocks airflow around a deflated cuff.

Heuer Ch 3 adds that when you cannot get a leak-free seal at an acceptable pressure — because peak pressures are high or the tracheal tube is too small for the patient's airway — the answer is to keep pressure below 25–30 cm H₂O and recommend exchanging the tube for a larger one (or one with subglottic suction), not to keep adding air.

The Chang deck reinforces using the largest ETT appropriate for the patient, since by Poiseuille's law halving the radius requires a 16-fold increase in driving pressure/work of breathing.

The safe range, in both units

20 to 30 cm H₂O is the maintenance range (Heuer Ch 3, Ch 9, and the deck all agree), with 30 cm H₂O the maximum acceptable intracuff pressure and below 20 cm H₂O inviting microaspiration → VAP. In mm Hg, using 1 mm Hg = 1.36 cm H₂O: 20 cm H₂O ≈ 14.7 mm Hg and 30 cm H₂O ≈ 22 mm Hg.

Note the internal Heuer wrinkle: Ch 3 says pressures over 25–30 cm H₂O obstruct tissue blood flow, and that most hospitals (including the NBRC "hospital") set 25 cm H₂O as the high-pressure limit, while the intubation checklist in Ch 9 and the VAP list both say 20–30 cm H₂O. Take 20–30 cm H₂O as the range and 25 cm H₂O as the conservative NBRC alarm limit.

Tracheal capillary perfusion pressure

Normally 25 to 35 mm Hg (deck, Hess Ch 17). That is the whole reason for the range: keep the cuff under 25 mm Hg so you stay below capillary perfusion pressure and avoid mucosal ischemia, ulceration, and tracheal wall necrosis, and go lower than 25 mm Hg in a hypotensive patient, whose perfusion pressure is already reduced. Rising cuff inflation volume over time suggests tracheal dilation/tracheomalacia, but Heuer does not recommend routinely tracking volume, because emptying the cuff to measure it raises aspiration risk.

MOV vs MLT

Minimal occluding volume (MOV): place the stethoscope diaphragm on the trachea and inflate slowly until no leak is heard at end-inspiration (end-inspiration is when the trachea is at its widest, so a seal there seals throughout). Minimal leak technique (MLT): inflate until the leak stops, then slowly withdraw a small amount of air until a slight leak is heard at end-inspiration. Conflict worth knowing: the deck presents both as equal options, but Heuer explicitly says to avoid the minimal leak technique in ventilated patients as part of VAP prevention — maintain 20–30 cm H₂O instead (Heuer Ch 9 VAP prevention list and top tips).

How to actually measure it

You need a calibrated manometer, a three-way stopcock, and a 10- or 20-mL syringe (or a commercial cuff-pressure bulb). Open all three ports, connect to the pilot valve, and add or remove air while watching the manometer. Because attaching the manometer itself steals volume and lowers the reading, you must always adjust the pressure — never just measure it. Check at least once per shift and any time the tube is changed or moved, air is added or removed, or a leak appears.

Two special cuffs: the Lanz valve/reservoir automatically holds about 30 cm H₂O, and the Bivona Fome-Cuf (Kamen-Wilkinson) foam cuff seals at atmospheric pressure — deflate and clamp the pilot line before insertion, then open it to atmosphere once positioned.

Cuff pressure target = 20 - 30 cm H2O (30 cm H2O = maximum intracuff) 25 cm H2O = typical hospital / NBRC high-pressure limit Convert cm H2O -> mm Hg : mm Hg = cm H2O / 1.36 (1 mm Hg = 1.36 cm H2O) 20 / 1.36 = 14.7 mm Hg 30 / 1.36 = 22.0 mm Hg Tracheal capillary perfusion pressure = 25 - 35 mm Hg -> keep cuff < 25 mm Hg; go lower still if the patient is hypotensive Too small a tube -> more air needed to seal -> higher wall pressure -> ischemia Fix = exchange for a larger tube, NOT more air

4ETT Sizes by Patient and the Pediatric Formulas

Adults. Heuer Table 9-8 sizes adults by category, and the deck sizes them by sex — the two agree closely.

PatientETT ID (Heuer)ETT ID (deck)Insertion length, incisors to tip
Adult female7.0–8.0 mm7–7.5 mm19–21 cm
Adult male8.0–9.0 mm8–8.5 mm21–23 cm
Large adult8.5 mm and up—23 cm and up

Heuer's female range runs a half-size wider at the top (7.0–8.0 vs the deck's 7–7.5) and its male range runs a half-size wider (8.0–9.0 vs 8–8.5) — report both if asked. For the nasotracheal route in adults, add 2 cm to the insertion length. Always have one size larger and one size smaller at the bedside. The governing principle is to use the largest appropriate tube, since resistance is inversely related to diameter and small tubes multiply work of breathing.

Pediatric sizes and depths (Heuer Table 9-8 — the decks do not give these).

The formulas (Heuer, footnote to Table 9-8):

Uncuffed ETT ID (mm) = (age in years / 4) + 4 Cuffed ETT ID (mm) = (age in years / 4) + 3 (cuffed must be slightly smaller) Worked example - a 4-year-old: Uncuffed: (4 / 4) + 4 = 1 + 4 = 5.0 mm ID Cuffed: (4 / 4) + 3 = 1 + 3 = 4.0 mm ID Cross-check vs Table 9-8: a 5-year-old takes 5.0 mm -> consistent Worked example - an 8-year-old: Uncuffed: (8 / 4) + 4 = 2 + 4 = 6.0 mm ID Table 9-8: 8 years = 6.0 mm -> exact match Insertion length from the table = 15-17 cm

Uncuffed tubes are recommended for premature infants and anyone under 3 kg. Otherwise both cuffed and uncuffed tubes are acceptable in infants and children — and if cuffed, cuff pressure must be monitored and held to the manufacturer's spec, usually no more than 20–30 cm H₂O, exactly as in adults. Anatomy the decks stress: in adults the glottis is the narrowest point; in children it is the cricoid ring, and 1 mm of circumferential edema in an infant cuts airway diameter by more than 50%.

Depth

Heuer gives an age-based insertion-length table, not an age-based depth formula. Hess fills this (Hess, MacIntyre & Galvin, Respiratory Care: Principles and Practice, 4th ed. — not your 203 textbook, so treat it as supporting reference rather than the number your instructor grades from). Hess Ch 22 gives a weight-based infant depth rule rather than the 3 × ID version: "To estimate the approximate distance to insert the ETT, measured from the lips, add 6 cm to the infant's weight in kilograms." So a 3 kg neonate ≈ 9 cm at the lip.

Hess also supplies the paediatric size formula — ETT size (mm ID) = (age in years ÷ 4) + 4 for children 1 to 10 years old — with the habit of keeping one half-size larger and one smaller at hand, length-based tapes for children ≤ 35 kg, and the caution that when a cuffed tube is used in a child, cuff pressure should be kept below 20 cm H₂O (lower than the adult 20–30). ⚠ Original note: a pediatric depth equation (e.g., depth = 3 × ETT ID) is not covered in Heuer or the available decks — use the table's cm ranges.

SourceHeuer Ch 9 — Endotracheal Tube, Table 9-8 and footnotes (PDF p448–449) · Heuer Ch 6 — Table 6-26, adult insertion lengths (PDF p337) · RCP 105 Wk 1 Lctr 2 — Airway Management (Hess Ch 17), slides 22, 34, 42 · Chang Ch 12 — Management of Mechanical Ventilation, slide 41

5The Chest Tube Drainage System

Why and where

A chest tube goes in when air or fluid in the pleural space stops the lung expanding — pneumothorax, hemothorax, pleural effusion, empyema (pus), or chylothorax — to restore negative intrapleural pressure. Air rises, fluid falls, so the tube is directed superiorly toward the apex for air and inferiorly/posteriorly to a gravity-dependent spot for fluid. Sites: the deck teaches the "safe triangle" at the 4th–5th intercostal space, midaxillary line for most tubes today (4th–6th ICS midaxillary for blood/fluid, historically 2nd–3rd ICS midclavicular for air), always entering over the top of the rib because the intercostal vein, artery, and nerve run in the groove under each rib.

Heuer Ch 16 says the pneumothorax tube typically goes into the fourth or fifth intercostal space at the anterior axillary line and fluid tubes into more dependent locations — anterior axillary (Heuer) vs midaxillary (deck) is a real wording difference, so give both if pressed. Emergency needle decompression is the 2nd ICS midclavicular line. Sizes: Heuer's chest tube kit stocks 24–36 Fr; the deck gives 36–40 Fr for large adults, 28–32 Fr small adults/teens, 18 Fr children, 12–14 Fr neonates, with 16–20 Fr adequate for air and 28–40 Fr (usually 36 Fr) for blood or thick fluid.

The three chambers.

  • Chamber 1 — collection. Receives blood, pus, chyle, or serous fluid by gravity; this is where you read amount, color, and consistency. Because it is separate from the seal, accumulating fluid no longer deepens the water seal or increases the patient's work of breathing (the fatal flaw of the old one-chamber bottle).
  • Chamber 2 — water seal. The long tube is submerged 2 cm below sterile water, making a one-way valve: air escapes the pleural space but atmosphere cannot get back in. A short vent tube prevents pressure buildup. If the level falls below 2 cm, refill it.
  • Chamber 3 — suction control. The depth of water in this chamber — not the wall vacuum setting — determines the suction delivered to the pleural space. Fill it to 20 cm of water and the patient gets −20 cm H₂O, whether the wall regulator reads 80 or 150 mm Hg. Recommended level: −10 to −20 cm H₂O (deck); Heuer Ch 6 says fill "usually to obtain −20 cm H₂O" and Heuer Ch 16 says 15–20 cm H₂O of suction is applied after insertion. If the level falls below the prescribed depth, refill it.

Tidaling

The water-seal level rises on spontaneous inspiration and falls on expiration (reversed on positive pressure). Tidaling proves the system is still communicating with the pleural space. Failure to fluctuate = obstruction.

Bubbling — the high-yield distinction.

  • Chamber 3 (suction control): gentle continuous bubbling is normal and expected. Vigorous bubbling means the wall vacuum is too high — more noise and evaporation, not more suction.
  • Chamber 2 (water seal): continuous bubbling means an air leak until proven otherwise. Some leak is expected if the patient has a bronchopleural fistula on positive pressure. To localize the leak, briefly pinch the chest tube at the insertion site: if the bubbling stops, the leak is at the insertion site or in the patient; if it continues, the leak is between the patient and the collection system. A patient-side leak (outwardly displaced tube or open wound) → notify the physician immediately and temporize with a sterile petroleum-jelly gauze pad. A system-side leak → tighten all connections, tape any tear, and prepare a new drainage unit.

Troubleshooting checklist.

  • No bubbling in the suction control chamber: check that the regulator is on and set, check the suction tubing for leaks/kinks, and confirm the atmospheric vent is open and unobstructed.
  • No tidaling in the water seal: check the collection tubing for kinks and dependent loops, then "milk" the tubing toward the collection chamber (do this routinely to prevent clotting). If milking fails, assess the patient for pneumothorax and notify the physician immediately.
  • Bubbling stops abruptly: either the pneumothorax resolved (good) or the tube is obstructed, kinked, or clotted (bad) — decide with breath sounds, SpO₂, work of breathing, and CXR.
  • Drainage eyelets become visible: the tube has partially pulled out — notify the physician; never push it back in.
  • Collection chamber full: replace the whole unit.
  • Transport: keep the unit below chest level and never clamp the chest tube — clamping can convert an air leak into a tension pneumothorax. Heuer: "the one-way seal must be maintained."
  • After removal: watch 1–2 days for recurrence; crepitus at the insertion site always suggests air leaking back into the pleural space. The deck's removal criteria: drainage < 100 mL in 24 hours, pneumothorax resolved on CXR, and no air leak on a Valsalva or forceful cough, with a follow-up CXR in 4 hours.
Suction to the pleural space = water depth in Chamber 3 (NOT the wall vacuum) Chamber 3 filled to 20 cm -> -20 cm H2O at the patient Wall vacuum 80 or 150 mm Hg -> still -20 cm H2O Deck range: -10 to -20 cm H2O Heuer Ch 6: fill "usually to -20 cm H2O" Heuer Ch 16: 15-20 cm H2O applied after insertion Water seal (Chamber 2) depth = 2 cm (refill if it drops below 2 cm) Gentle bubbling: Chamber 3 = NORMAL | Chamber 2 = AIR LEAK No tidaling in Chamber 2 = OBSTRUCTION

7Treating Pleural Effusions — Acute and Chronic

Always treat the cause, not just the fluid. A CHF or cirrhosis effusion often melts away once the systemic problem is managed; an infected or malignant effusion needs an entirely different plan. The four questions to ask: should thoracentesis be done? can the underlying cause be treated? are antibiotics indicated? is a chest tube required?

Acute — thoracentesis

A physician-performed insertion of a needle or catheter into the pleural space. Diagnostic thoracentesis determines the cause of the fluid (transudate vs exudate) and obtains cells to assess for malignancy; therapeutic thoracentesis is recommended for any effusion large enough to cause respiratory distress or to interfere with lung expansion. There are no absolute contraindications; relative ones are an uncooperative patient, severe uncorrected bleeding disorder, severe bullous lung disease, plus insufficient pleural fluid and skin infection at the insertion site.

Equipment (sterile kit): an 8-Fr angiocath over a long 7.5-inch 18-gauge needle with a three-way stopcock and self-sealing valve, a 50- to 60-mL syringe, a large-volume drainage bottle/bag (therapeutic) or specimen vials (diagnostic), 1% or 2% lidocaine, an anxiolytic/sedative, sterile drapes and chlorhexidine. Also have chest tube equipment ready. Positioning: sitting up and leaning forward on a bedside table if the patient can be mobilized; if not, affected side down at the edge of the bed, slightly rotated, that arm behind the head, mid/posterior axillary line exposed, head of bed up 30°.

The RT's job: recommend a cough suppressant beforehand (coughing during the procedure can cause lung or pleural trauma), keep the patient still and not coughing, monitor vital signs and SpO₂, give O₂ to keep SpO₂ ≥ 90%, watch for dyspnea, pallor, coughing, label and route specimens, and ensure a post-procedure x-ray or ultrasound to rule out pneumothorax. Call the physician immediately for dyspnea, hypotension, chest pain, or hemoptysis.

Acute — chest tube

If the effusion is large, infected, or not resolving, place a chest tube at the 4th–5th ICS midaxillary line (Heuer Ch 16: fluid goes to the more gravity-dependent locations), using a large-bore tube or pigtail catheter directed inferiorly. Empyema must be drained — antibiotics alone do not penetrate thick pus — and may require thoracotomy. Supportive protocols: oxygen therapy (for hypoxemia from compression atelectasis and to cut work of breathing and myocardial work), lung expansion therapy used cautiously, and mechanical ventilation for acute ventilatory failure.

Chronic / recurrent — pleurodesis

A sclerosant (talc, tetracycline, or bleomycin) is instilled into the pleural space to trigger inflammation (pleuritis) that fuses the visceral and parietal pleura, obliterating the space so fluid or air can no longer accumulate. The cost is painful pleurisy from that deliberate inflammation. Used for recurrent malignant effusion and recurrent pneumothorax.

Chronic — indwelling tunneled pleural catheter

Not covered in Heuer or the available decks. Neither source uses the term; the decks offer the pigtail catheter and pleurodesis as the recurrent-effusion options, and Heuer's chest-tube and thoracentesis chapters stop at conventional tubes. Look it up separately if your instructor lists it.

Transudate vs exudate — the pivot

A transudate comes from systemic pressure changes — increased hydrostatic or decreased oncotic pressure (CHF, cirrhosis/hepatic hydrothorax, nephrotic syndrome) — and is thin, watery, low-protein, few cells, with normal non-inflamed pleura. An exudate comes from local pleural disease — increased capillary permeability from infection (pneumonia, TB), malignancy, or autoimmune disease — and is high-protein with many cells and debris. High protein = local pleural disease. LDH more than three times the upper limit of normal (often > 1,000 U/L) suggests pleural infection. Exudates generally need antibiotics, drainage, or a cancer workup; transudates are treated by fixing the systemic problem.

Heuer names the transudate/exudate distinction as the reason to recommend a diagnostic thoracentesis but does not reproduce Light's criteria — the protein/LDH detail comes from the pleural-disease deck.

SourceHeuer Ch 16 — Assisting with Thoracentesis and Chest Tube Insertion (PDF p744–748) · Heuer Ch 5 — Thoracentesis (PDF p279) · Disorders of the Pleura and of the Chest Wall (Ch 24), slides 5–11, 22–23 · RCP 203 Lesson 3b (Ch 5 Recommend Diagnostic Procedures), slides 35–36 · RCP 105 Wk 7 — Chest Tubes (Chang Ch 14), slide 3

Clinical Assessment, CXR Correlation & ECG

7 items · CXR vs palpation, percussion and breath sounds · crepitus · orthopnea · levels of consciousness · cyanosis · PEA · ECG troubleshooting and dangerous rhythms

1How a CXR Relates to Palpation, Percussion, and Breath Sounds2Crepitus (Subcutaneous Emphysema)3Orthopnea — Definition, Meaning, Quantifying It, and the Workup4Levels of Consciousness and the Glasgow Coma Scale5Cyanosis — Central vs. Peripheral6PEA — Pulseless Electrical Activity, and the H's and T's7ECG — Lead Placement, Artifact Troubleshooting, and Dangerous Rhythms

1How a CXR Relates to Palpation, Percussion, and Breath Sounds

The CXR and the physical exam tell the same story in two languages. On film, radiopacity = WHITE = "more stuff" and radiolucency = BLACK = "more air" (Heuer Table 2-20). Under your hands, more stuff = dull percussion + increased fremitus, and more air = increased resonance + decreased fremitus. The trachea shifts toward volume loss and away from pressure — which is exactly the mediastinal shift you see on the film.

Heuer's percussion vocabulary (Ch 2, p115)

normal resonance = moderately low-pitched, easily heard, over normal air-filled lung. Increased resonance (also called tympanic or hyperresonant) = hollow/loud/low-pitched → hyperinflation (acute asthma, COPD) or pneumothorax. Dull/flat = short, muted, high-pitched → increased tissue density (pneumonia, atelectasis, lung tumors). Decreased resonance → fluid in the pleural space. Diaphragm excursion by percussion is normally 5-7 cm in adults, and is reduced in neuromuscular disease and severe hyperinflation.

Heuer Table 2-18 — Physical Findings in Common Disorders is exactly this question:

ConditionCXRPercussion noteTactile / vocal fremitusBreath sounds
PneumothoraxIncreased radiolucency (blacker); trachea/mediastinum shifted AWAY; hemidiaphragm may look flattened on the affected sideIncreased resonance (hyperresonant)Decreased — lung separated from the chest wall by airAbsent breath sounds; inspection shows unilateral expansion
Consolidation / pneumonia (or tumor)Increased radiopacity (whiter); air bronchogramsDull noteINCREASED vocal fremitus — denser tissue transmits betterBronchial breath sounds in the periphery + late inspiratory crackles; deck adds whispered pectoriloquy
Pleural effusionIncreased radiopacity; blunted costophrenic angle; a large effusion can also flatten the hemidiaphragm; trachea shifted AWAY if largeDull note (Heuer also calls pleural fluid decreased resonance)ABSENT vocal fremitusAbsent breath sounds; inspection shows unilateral expansion + tracheal shift away
AtelectasisIncreased radiopacity; elevated hemidiaphragm on the affected side; mediastinum/trachea shifted TOWARDDull noteABSENT vocal fremitusAbsent breath sounds (bronchial sounds and crackles as alveoli reopen)
Asthma (bonus row)HyperinflationIncreased resonance—Expiratory wheezing; accessory muscle use, reduced expansion
COPD (bonus row)Hyperinflation, flattened diaphragmIncreased resonanceDecreased (hyperinflation)Diffuse decrease in breath sounds + early inspiratory crackles; ↑ AP diameter
Diffuse interstitial fibrosis (bonus row)Increased radiopacitySlight decrease in resonanceOften normal; increased fremitusLate inspiratory crackles; rapid, shallow breathing
