Gap

⚠ Where the deck and Heuer differ on atelectasis fremitus: the older draft/deck framing said fremitus is increased in atelectasis if the bronchus stays patent and decreased if plugged. Heuer's Table 2-18 states flatly "absent vocal fremitus" for atelectasis. Go with Heuer's "absent" on the exam; the patent-bronchus nuance comes from Heuer's separate Vocal Fremitus list (increased with pneumonia, atelectasis; decreased when a lobe or segment is blocked by a mucus plug or foreign body), so both statements exist in the book.

If asked, the safe answer is: fremitus is decreased/absent when anything separates or blocks the lung (air, fluid, plug), increased when solid tissue conducts (pneumonia).

Diaphragm clues on film (Heuer Table 2-21)

blunted costophrenic angle → lower-lobe pneumonia or pleural effusion; flattened diaphragm → hyperinflation (emphysema/asthma), tension pneumothorax, or large effusion; elevated diaphragm → phrenic nerve paralysis, hepatomegaly, or atelectasis; air under the diaphragm → perforated GI tract. Heart failure on CXR: cardiomegaly (CT ratio > 50% on a PA film), prominent upper-lobe vascular markings, Kerley B lines, and pleural effusions. ETT tip should sit 4-6 cm above the carina, roughly T2-T4.

SourceHeuer Ch 2 — Integrating Physical Examination Findings, Table 2-18 (PDF p118-120); Percussion (p115-116); Tables 2-20/2-21 (p122-123) · RCP 203 Lssn 2a, slides 44, 50, 52-53

2Crepitus (Subcutaneous Emphysema)

Crepitus is the crackling, "Rice-Krispies" sensation you feel when you palpate skin that has air trapped in the subcutaneous tissue (sub = below, cutaneous = skin). Heuer: especially in patients receiving positive-pressure ventilation, gas leaks into the tissues around the head, neck, and chest, forming subcutaneous bubbles — that is subcutaneous emphysema, and palpating those bubbles produces crepitus.

Why it mattersHeuer says subcutaneous emphysema itself is harmless, but it often occurs together with a pneumothorax. So if you detect crepitus you must (1) assess the patient for pneumothorax and (2) immediately communicate the finding to the physician. Most clinicians also recommend a chest x-ray whenever crepitus occurs in a mechanically ventilated patient — and note subcutaneous emphysema is itself listed as a cause of increased radiolucency on the CXR (Table 2-20). Heuer's test-taking tip states it plainly: neck/upper-chest crepitus in a patient on positive pressure suggests a pneumothorax.

Other causes: thoracic injury or surgery, tracheostomy, and post-op air leaks (deck). Heuer adds one more clinical setting: crepitus at a chest-tube insertion site after the tube is removed always suggests recurrence of air leakage into the pleural space (Ch 16). Air follows the path of least resistance, so extensive crepitus signals a clinically significant leak — document location and extent and correlate with airway pressures, imaging, and clinical status.

SourceHeuer Ch 2 — Palpation: Skin and Soft Tissues (PDF p114), T4 Test-Taking Tips (p129); Ch 16 — Chest Tubes · RCP 203 Lssn 2a, slide 40 · RCP 103 Clinical Data PP, slide 34

3Orthopnea — Definition, Meaning, Quantifying It, and the Workup

Orthopnea is a patient's sensation of uncomfortable breathing when lying down (recumbent), typically relieved by sitting or standing up. It is gathered during the interview, alongside dyspnea, work of breathing, and exercise tolerance. Heuer: both dyspnea and orthopnea are associated with a variety of cardiac and pulmonary disorders; on inspection, a patient "always elevated with pillows" implies orthopnea and congestive heart failure.

How it is quantifiedBedside shortcut (deck): ask how many pillows the patient sleeps on. Formally, Heuer quantifies the related sensation of breathlessness with the Borg Scale, a 0-10 scale (0 = no sensation of dyspnea, 10 = maximal), always tied to a predefined level of activity (e.g., an exercise-test stage or the end of a 6-minute walk test). Exercise tolerance itself: graded cardiopulmonary exercise test = gold standard; 6MWT = practical alternative; ATS Breathlessness Scale = simplest.

Workup — sorting cardiac from pulmonary:

TestNumbersWhat it tells you
BNPHeuer: reference < 20 pg/mL; < 100 pg/mL rules OUT CHF; > 500 pg/mL rules IN CHF ⚠ Deck: normal < 100, 100-300 = HF may be present, > 300 mild, > 600 moderate, > 900 severeDistinguishes a cardiac cause of SOB (CHF) from a pulmonary one (COPD)
Troponin IHeuer: < 0.4 μg/L; rises 3-6 hr after insult, peaks at 12 hr, persists 7 days ⚠ Deck: normal < 0.1 ng/mLAMI / acute coronary syndrome
CK-MB< 4-6% of total CK; rises 4-6 hr, peaks at 24 hr, normal in 2-3 daysAMI
CXRCT ratio > 50% on a PA film = cardiomegalyHeart failure vs. primary lung disease: cardiomegaly, prominent upper-lobe vascular markings, Kerley B lines, pleural effusions
Echocardiogram—Cardiac structure and function; systolic HF shows decreased LV function
12-lead ECG—Rhythm, ischemia, infarction
SourceHeuer Ch 2 — Breathing Difficulties and Exercise Tolerance (PDF p89), Table 2-7 (p97-98); Ch 1 — Table 1-3 Laboratory Reference Ranges (p43), Heart Size on CXR (Ch 2, p123-124) · RCP 203 Lssn 2a, slides 10-11 · RCP 103 Clinical Data PP, slides 17, 48, 54, 60

4Levels of Consciousness and the Glasgow Coma Scale

Assess level of consciousness (LOC, also called "sensorium") fast by asking time, place, and person. An alert patient is "oriented × 3" (add situation for × 4; a patient who answers only two is charted × 2, e.g., "oriented × 2, unaware of time"). Heuer's common causes of disorientation: neurologic injury, sedation and analgesics (especially opioids), and severe hypoxemia OR hypercapnia. In general, only alert patients can fully cooperate and participate in their care. On inspection, a depressed sensorium implies poor cerebral oxygenation, degenerative brain disorders, or drug overdose.

The graded scale (the deck's ladder; Heuer names the endpoints but teaches AVPU and GCS as its formal tools):

LevelDescriptionClinical point
AlertAwake, responsive, appropriate, oriented × 3Normal — can cooperate with care
LethargicSluggish, slow to respond, but still appropriateTired, ill, or mildly impaired
ObtundedVery drowsy; arousable but reduced cough/gag reflex⚠ Airway-protection concern — aspiration risk ↑
StuporousResponds only to strong or painful stimuliSignificant impairment; close monitoring
ComatoseNo response to any stimulus; flaccidCannot protect the airway — airway and ventilation support usually needed

Deck-only intermediate rungs you may still see charted: somnolent (easily aroused but drifts back to sleep — think sleep apnea or excess O₂ in COPD), confused (disoriented, inappropriate answers), and semi-comatose (minimal response even to pain).

AVPU Scale (Heuer Box 2-1) — quick, comprehensive alternative: Alert and oriented (report as oriented × 1, 2, or 3) · responds to Verbal stimulus (note whether the response is appropriate) · responds to Pain (firmly pinch the skin; look for moaning or withdrawal) · Unresponsive (if no response on one side, try the other; flaccid and silent = unresponsive).

Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS) — Heuer calls it the most objective assessment of consciousness. Score three components — Eye, Verbal, Motor — and sum them.

GCS elementRange
Total score3 (deep coma) to 15 (fully alert)
Mild impairment13-15
Moderate impairment9-12
Severe impairment (coma)≤ 8 ⚠ GCS ≤ 8 = intubate — Heuer's Ch 2 tip states "a GCS score of ≤ 8 indicates coma"; the deck adds the airway-protection rule

Shorthand: components are charted like "E3, V2, M2" = Eyes 3, Verbal 2, Motor 2, total 7 → severe impairment.

Gap⚠ Gap worth knowing: the point-by-point GCS component table (Heuer Table 2-1) — what earns a 4 for eyes, a 5 for verbal, a 6 for motor — is a figure in the source text and is not reproduced in the available Heuer text or in any deck. Memorize the 3/15 range, the 13-15 / 9-12 / ≤ 8 bands, and the E-V-M shorthand; get the individual point values from your ACLS/trauma card.
SourceHeuer Ch 2 — Level of Consciousness, AVPU Box 2-1, Glasgow Coma Scale (PDF p86-88), Table 2-7 (p99), T4 Tips (p128) · RCP 203 Lssn 2a, slides 6-8 · RCP 103 Clinical Data PP, slides 13-14

5Cyanosis — Central vs. Peripheral

Cyanosis is a blue/purple discoloration. The deck supplies the number Heuer does not: cyanosis becomes visible only when the blood contains at least 5 g/dL of reduced (deoxygenated) hemoglobin. Heuer states the same physiology without the figure: "the intensity of cyanosis increases with the amount of Hb in the blood."

Central cyanosisPeripheral cyanosis (acrocyanosis)
What's wrongOXYGENATION problem — low SaO₂ from poor oxygenation of blood by the lungsPERFUSION / blood-flow problem — poor blood flow
Where you see itMucous membranes of the lips and mouth (bluish tint); deck adds the tongueExtremities only — hands, feet, nail beds, fingertips; Heuer Table 2-7 also lists mottled/acrocyanotic skin
When it appearsWith normal Hb, generally first appears when SaO₂ drops below 80% (PaO₂ 45-50 torr)Can occur with a completely normal SaO₂
Paired findingArterial hypoxemiaWith cool extremities and capillary refill ≥ 3 seconds → circulatory failure
Why anemia masks itthe sign depends on an absolute amount of desaturated hemoglobin, not a percentage. Heuer: patients with anemia can be severely hypoxic before cyanosis ever appears, because there simply isn't 5 g/dL of reduced Hb to see. The mirror image: patients with polycythemia can look cyanotic yet still have adequate O₂ content.
Gap⚠ Cyanosis is a late and unreliable sign of hypoxemia; visibility is affected by hemoglobin concentration, skin pigmentation, perfusion, and lighting. "Never rely on cyanosis alone to determine oxygenation" — no cyanosis does not mean adequate oxygenation. Typical NBRC intervention: increase FiO₂ or check ventilation.
SourceHeuer Ch 2 — Cyanosis (PDF p100-101), Table 2-7 (p99-100), T4 Tips (p128) · RCP 203 Lssn 2a, slides 21-22 · RCP 103 Clinical Data PP, slides 21, 23 (the 5 g/dL figure appears only in the deck)

6PEA — Pulseless Electrical Activity, and the H's and T's

PEA = the monitor shows organized electrical activity but the patient has NO pulse — electrical activity without effective circulation. Heuer: PEA is one of four cardiac dysrhythmias producing pulselessness — ventricular fibrillation (VF), ventricular tachycardia (VT), PEA, and asystole. The unifying idea: either no rhythm, or a rhythm that produces no pulse.

Treatment

PEA and asystole are NOT shockable — do not defibrillate and never cardiovert. Management is high-quality CPR + epinephrine 1 mg IV every 3-5 minutes with a flush (vasoconstrictor; improves cerebral perfusion). Epinephrine may be given via ETT if IV or IO access is not established — in that case double the dose and follow with 10 mL of normal saline. The RT's job is to oxygenate, ventilate, and correct acidosis, because acidosis and hypoxia blunt epinephrine's effect.

Capnography is now a standard of care during resuscitation: if PetCO₂ < 10 mm Hg and intra-arterial diastolic pressure < 20 mm Hg, the quality of CPR must be improved; failure to achieve PetCO₂ > 10 mm Hg after 20 minutes of CPR can be used as an indicator to terminate resuscitation. Pulse/breathing checks are done generally after five cycles of CPR.

Gap⚠ The reversible causes — Heuer's "5 Hs" and "5 Ts" (found in Ch 15; absent from every deck):
5 H's5 T's
HypovolemiaTension pneumothorax
HypoxiaTamponade (cardiac)
Hydrogen ion (acidosis)Toxins (drug overdose, anaphylactic reactions)
Hypo-/hyperkalemiaThrombosis, pulmonary (pulmonary emboli)
HypothermiaThrombosis, cardiac (MI or ischemia)

Note the RT owns several of these directly: hypoxia (airway/O₂), hydrogen ion (ventilation), and tension pneumothorax (needle decompression/chest tube).

IO access (deck): if IV access fails, the intraosseous route delivers drugs at IV speed — proximal tibia or humerus, needle out within 3-4 hours. Sodium bicarbonate 0.5-1 mEq/kg is a last resort only, to keep pH > 7.15; it raises CO₂, so you must ventilate. Survival drops 7-10% per minute without CPR, 3-4% per minute with CPR.

SourceHeuer Ch 15 — Adult Resuscitation Protocols, Monitoring and Assessment, "5 Hs and 5 Ts" (PDF p685-690), Table 15-2 ACLS Medications (p688), T4 Tips (p720) · Chptr 21 Vasopressors/Inotropes/Antiarrhythmics deck, slides 46-47, 54 (the H's and T's appear only in Heuer)

7ECG — Lead Placement, Artifact Troubleshooting, and Dangerous Rhythms

What it's for. The 12-lead ECG assesses rhythm disturbances, determines the heart's electrical axis, and identifies the site and extent of myocardial damage.

Electrodes vs. leads⚠ An electrode is the sticker on the patient; a lead is an electrical VIEW. Think electrodes = cameras, leads = pictures. You place 10 electrodes to produce 12 leads — Heuer: "there are only 10 actual leads to place, not 12." The right leg (RL) "doesn't count" because it is just a ground. The difference is made up by the 3 augmented limb leads (aVR, aVL, aVF), which the machine mathematically derives from the RA, LA, and LL electrodes. Deck detail: Lead I = RA(−) → LA(+), Lead II = RA(−) → LL(+), Lead III = LA(−) → LL(+).

Limb electrodes — apply to muscular areas of the arms and legs: RL / green = right leg, LL / red = left leg, RA / white = right arm, LA / black = left arm. Mnemonics: "smoke over fire" (black over red), "clouds over grass" (white over green), "white on right."

Chest leadPlacement (Heuer Figure 3-1 and T4 tip)
V14th intercostal space, RIGHT sternal border
V24th intercostal space, LEFT sternal border
V3Between V2 and V4
V45th intercostal space, midclavicular line
V55th intercostal space, anterior axillary line
V65th intercostal space, midaxillary line

Procedure (Heuer Box 3-1): turn on and run self-test/calibration → position the patient supine or semi-Fowler's → remove all jewelry and metal, have the patient relax → apply limb electrodes → place chest leads → ensure comfort and privacy → run the tracing, aiming for a stable isoelectric baseline with no noise or electrical interference.

Artifact troubleshooting. Most units auto-detect problems and won't record until the signal is good. The two most common problems are absent or "noisy" signals.

  • Failure to obtain a signal → usually a loose, missing, or defective lead, or the patient's hair preventing skin contact.
  • A noisy signal → poor electrical connection, motion artifact, or improper filtering of extraneous electrical activity (deck adds oily/sweaty skin, shivering/muscle tremor, and 60-Hz interference from nearby equipment).
GapHeuer's fix list, in order: verify the snaps and connectors are clean and corrosion-free → verify electrodes are connected properly to the patient → verify the electrode gel is not dry (replace suspect electrodes) → check the main cable for continuity (replace if damaged) → confirm the patient is motionless, supporting the limbs if needed → verify the filter settings. ⚠ Practical RT rule: if a tracing suddenly looks bizarre, LOOK AT THE PATIENT before calling it an arrhythmia.
Reading damage and electrolytes

Ischemia/NSTEMI = ST-segment depression (horizontal or downsloping ≥ 0.5 mm) + T-wave flattening or inversion ≥ 1 mm. Acute STEMI = ST elevation ≥ 2 mm (men) or ≥ 1.5 mm (women) in two contiguous precordial leads, pathologic Q waves, prominent R waves in V1-V2. Tall T waves = hyperkalemia (also acute MI); small/flat/inverted T waves = ischemia; prominent U waves = hypokalemia or hypomagnesemia.

QRS > 0.12 sec = PVCs, bundle branch block, VT, hyperkalemia. PR > 0.20 sec = first-degree or Mobitz I AV block. Rate shortcut — the "rule of 300": at 25 mm/sec, each small box = 0.04 sec, each large box = 0.20 sec; divide 300 by the number of large boxes in the R-R interval. ⚠ The 12-lead ECG alone cannot diagnose an MI — you need presentation plus cardiac biomarkers, and STEMI (thrombolytics/angioplasty) is treated very differently from NSTEMI (antiplatelets/anticoagulants).

Dangerous rhythms and their treatment:

RhythmShockable?Treatment (Heuer)
Ventricular fibrillation (VF)✅ YES — unsynchronized defibrillationCPR + defibrillation ASAP. Biphasic: 120-200 J initial (or per manufacturer), same dose for subsequent shocks (higher may be considered). Monophasic: 360 J. Then epinephrine 1 mg IV q3-5 min. Antiarrhythmics: amiodarone 300 mg rapid infusion, then 150 mg in 3-5 min and every 10 min, up to 2.2 g/day; or lidocaine 1.0-1.5 mg/kg, then 0.5-0.75 mg/kg q5-10 min, up to 3 mg/kg
Pulseless VT (and polymorphic/irregular VT)✅ YES — unsynchronized defibrillation ⚠ NEVER cardiovert theseSame as VF: CPR + defibrillation, epinephrine 1 mg q3-5 min, then amiodarone or lidocaine
Asystole❌ NOCPR + epinephrine 1 mg IV q3-5 min; fix oxygenation/ventilation; work the 5 Hs and 5 Ts
PEA❌ NOCPR + epinephrine 1 mg IV q3-5 min; RT corrects hypoxia and acidosis; work the 5 Hs and 5 Ts (see item 9)
SVT / atrial fibrillationSynchronized cardioversionA-fib: 120-200 J biphasic, increase stepwise. Also antiarrhythmics, radiofrequency ablation, pacemaker; treat the underlying cause (hypoxemia, electrolyte imbalance). ⚠ Immediate cardioversion if the ventricular rate exceeds 150/min despite drugs. Deck adds adenosine for SVT (12-second half-life, give central/proximal with the arm raised and flush immediately; side effects bronchospasm, dyspnea, cough)
Atrial flutterSynchronized cardioversion50-100 J biphasic, increase stepwise. "Sawtooth" pattern between QRS complexes, no PR interval
Monomorphic VT WITH a pulse (stable)Synchronized cardioversion100 J biphasic, increase stepwise
Symptomatic bradycardia (rate < 60)❌Atropine 0.5 mg IV q3-5 min, up to a maximum of 3 mg; positive chronotropes (atropine, epinephrine, dopamine); warm blanket and fluids if hypothermic; evaluate ECG and cardiac enzymes; CPR if needed; pacing
PVCs during a respiratory procedure❌⚠ Stop what you are doing, give supplemental O₂ to stabilize the patient, and notify the physician immediately
Pediatric defibrillationinitial 2 J/kg, subsequent at least 4 J/kg (higher may be considered); monophasic 2 J/kg. ⚠ Cardioversion is NEVER used for VF, pulseless VT, or polymorphic VT — those need unsynchronized defibrillation. Cardioversion is also not used for multifocal atrial or junctional tachycardia.
SourceHeuer Ch 3 — 12-Lead ECG, Box 3-1, Figure 3-1, troubleshooting (PDF p134-136), T4 Tips (p193); Ch 4 — Interpreting a 12-Lead ECG, Tables 4-1 and 4-2 (p201-204); Ch 15 — ACLS, Table 15-2 drugs and Table 15-3 defibrillation doses (p688-689); Ch 16 — Table 16-6 Synchronized Cardioversion Energy Levels (p749); Ch 1 — ECG Rhythms (p69-72) · RCP 203 Lssn 2b, slides 5-7 · RCP 103 Clinical Data PP, slides 55, 60 · Chptr 21 deck, slides 44-47, 50-54 (defibrillation and cardioversion joules appear only in Heuer)

PFTs, Lung Volumes, Shunt & Oxygenation Calculations

1Lung Volumes and Capacities — Know All of Them2PFTs — When and How, the Bronchodilator-Response Threshold, and Spirometry Hazards3PFT Pattern Comparison — Obstructive vs Restrictive vs Mixed vs Normal vs Poor Effort4Bronchoprovocation / Methacholine Challenge + the Three Lung-Volume Methods5Single-Breath N2 Washout — What It Measures and the Four Phases6Shunt Calculation — Estimate, Classic Equation, and the Normal-Value Conflict7NIF/MIP and MEP — Respiratory Muscle Strength8Acute and Chronic Respiratory and Metabolic ABG Patterns9Inspiratory Flow Demand — How to Calculate It

1Lung Volumes and Capacities — Know All of Them

One rule runs through this whole table: every "capacity" is two or more "volumes" added together. The four primary volumes (VT, IRV, ERV, RV) never overlap; the four capacities (IC, FRC, VC, TLC) are sums of them. RV cannot be measured by spirometry — you can never exhale it — so FRC must be measured indirectly (item 4), and everything else falls out of it.

Heuer's own algebra set (App B, Table B-4) is worth memorizing cold:

VC = TLC − RV VC = IRV + VT + ERV TLC = FRC + IC TLC = IRV + VT + ERV + RV RV = FRC − ERV RV = TLC − VC FRC = RV + ERV FRC = TLC − IC HEUER WORKED EXAMPLE (App B, Problem B.16) Given: FRC 3800 mL, VT 400 mL, ERV 1300 mL. Find RV. RV = FRC − ERV RV = 3800 − 1300 RV = 2500 mL = 2.5 L

Normal values below: static volumes from RCP 203 Lssn 3a slide 77 (RV 1.2 L, FRC 2.4 L, TLC 6.0 L — Heuer's Table 4-22 is a figure), the VC breakdown from RCP 105 Wk 3 Weaning slides 15–16, and the percent-predicted cutoffs from Heuer Ch 4 (p242) and Ch 1 (p48).

Abbrev.Full nameNormal value (adult)Composed of
VTTidal Volume0.5 L (500 mL); Heuer monitoring range 5–7 mL/kg PBW, critical < 4–5 mL/kg PBW or < 300 mLPrimary volume (not divisible)
IRVInspiratory Reserve Volume3.1 LPrimary volume
ERVExpiratory Reserve Volume1.2 LPrimary volume
RVResidual Volume1.2 LPrimary volume; RV = FRC − ERV = TLC − VC
ICInspiratory Capacity3.6 L (= TLC 6.0 − FRC 2.4)IRV + VT
FRCFunctional Residual Capacity2.4 LERV + RV
VC / SVC(Slow) Vital Capacity4.8 L; Heuer reference 70 mL/kg PBW, critical < 10–15 mL/kg PBW; weaning threshold > 10 mL/kg; intubate a neuromuscular patient at VC < 1.0 L or < 15 mL/kgIRV + VT + ERV
TLCTotal Lung Capacity6.0 LFRC + IC (= all four volumes)
FVCForced Vital Capacitynormal ≥ 80% of predicted; abnormal ≤ 80% predictedSame components as VC, blasted out forcefully
FEV1Forced Expiratory Volume in 1 secondnormal ≥ 80% of predicted; Heuer's ballpark FEV1% > 75% and FEV3% > 95%; normals blow out 80–90% of the FVC in about 3 secondsPortion of FVC exhaled in the first 1 sec
FEV1/FVC (FEV1%)FEV1-to-FVC ratioA normal FEV1% is 70% or more for all patients; < 70% confirms obstruction (GOLD: post-bronchodilator < 0.70); asthma adults < 0.75–0.80, children < 0.90FEV1 ÷ FVC × 100
FEF25–75%Forced Expiratory Flow, mid-portion≤ 70% of predicted = small airway obstructionAverage flow over the middle half of the FVC
FEF200–1200Forced Expiratory Flow, early portion≤ 70% of predicted = large airway obstructionFlow between 200 and 1200 mL of the FVC
DLCODiffusing capacity (not a volume)Heuer: 25–30 mL/min/mm Hg; deck: 25 ± 5; < 80% predicted abnormal; 65–80% mild, 50–65% moderate, < 50% severeCO transfer across the A-C membrane

Percent-predicted severity bands Heuer says the NBRC uses: normal 80–120%, mild 70–79%, moderate 50–69%, severe < 49% of predicted.

2PFTs — When and How, the Bronchodilator-Response Threshold, and Spirometry Hazards

When you recommend which test (Heuer Table 5-3):

TestRecommend it to…
PEFRMonitor airway tone in asthma over time (diary); assess response to bronchodilator (FEV spirometry is preferred). Heuer's tip: use PEFR to monitor obstruction, not to diagnose it.
Screening/bedside spirometryScreen for lung dysfunction, assess response to treatment, assess surgical risk, monitor disease progression
Pre-/post-bronchodilator spirometryConfirm need for bronchodilator, individualize the dose, decide if a drug/dose/frequency change is needed
Laboratory spirometryQuantify severity and prognosis; follow up non-definitive bedside results; occupational/environmental exposure; drug pulmonary toxicity; disability claims
MVVIntegrated function of airways + lungs + chest cage + muscles; preop evaluation; breathing reserve for exercise testing
FRC and TLCGrade hyperinflation; quantify gas trapped in cysts/bullae by comparing to body-box TGV; assess restriction
DLCOFollow ILD/fibrosis, emphysema, CF; separate chronic bronchitis vs emphysema vs asthma; amiodarone/bleomycin toxicity
Bronchial provocationAirway hyperreactivity, occupational asthma, risk of developing asthma
How the maneuver is performed (Heuer Box 3-6 + ATS Table 6-35)

Enter age, sex, height, ethnicity (required to compute predicted values). Remove gum/dentures, loosen tight clothing, use nose clips, patient sits or stands (be consistent and record which). Demonstrate first. Inhale fully to TLC, then blast out as fast and as long as possible — at least 6 seconds (severe COPD may take up to 15 seconds). ATS specifics: back-extrapolated volume must be < 5% of the FVC or 150 mL, the trace must be smooth with a distinct start, minimum 6 seconds with a 1-second plateau, three acceptable maneuvers (max eight attempts), and the two largest FVC and FEV1 values must be within 200 mL or 5% of each other.

If three acceptable maneuvers cannot be obtained, do not report the results. Report the two largest FVC and FEV1 values even if from different attempts; the best test is the maneuver with the biggest sum of FVC + FEV1. If the test is a pre/post study, wait for peak drug effect — 15–30 minutes for spirometry, 10–30 minutes for peak flow.

PERCENT PREDICTED = (actual ÷ predicted) × 100 HEUER WORKED EXAMPLE (App B, Problem B.19) Measured FVC 3.25 L, predicted normal FVC 3.82 L % predicted = 3.25 ÷ 3.82 × 100 = 85% of normal PERCENT CHANGE = [(post − pre) ÷ pre] × 100 HEUER WORKED EXAMPLE #1 (App B, Problem B.18) Pre-bronchodilator FEV1 = 2.4 L Post-bronchodilator FEV1 = 2.7 L (2.7 − 2.4) ÷ 2.4 = 0.3 ÷ 2.4 = 0.125 0.125 × 100 = 12.5% → MEETS the 12–15% bar (borderline positive) HEUER WORKED EXAMPLE #2 (Ch 4, PDF p245 — same numbers as the deck) Pre FEV1 = 3.0 L Post FEV1 = 3.3 L (3.3 − 3.0) ÷ 3.0 = 0.3 ÷ 3.0 = 0.10 0.10 × 100 = 10% → NOT a significant response

THE BRONCHODILATOR-RESPONSE THRESHOLD — "AND" vs "OR" IS A REAL CONFLICT. Report both:

  • Heuer, Ch 4 (PDF p245) — the exact wording is "or": "If the patient's FEV1 improves by at least 12–15% (or by 200 mL or more in adults during PFTs), then the obstruction is classified as reversible, as in asthma. Lesser improvement indicates that the obstruction is not reversible, as in most forms of COPD."
  • Heuer, App B (PDF p1045) ballpark rule — percent only: "for FEV1, and the PEFR the improvement must be at least 12–15% to be considered a significant response to bronchodilator therapy."
  • Heuer, Ch 5 Table 5-3 (PDF p267) — for PEFR: "A post-bronchodilator improvement of 12% or more is considered significant."
  • Heuer, Ch 1 (PDF p48): "An increase in flows of 12–15% or more after bronchodilator therapy suggests significant reversibility."
  • RCP 203 Lssn 3a slide 76 — the deck changed the conjunction to "and": "If the patient's FEV1 improves by at least 12–15% (and by 200 mL or more in adults), then the obstruction is classified as reversible."
  • Asthma deck slide 23: positive = ↑ FEV1 ≥ 12% AND ≥ 200 mL in adults; ≥ 12% of predicted in children; also accepts PEFR day-to-day variability > 10% (adults) / 13% (children).
Bottom line for the examthe numbers 12–15% and 200 mL are not in dispute — only whether both must be met. Heuer says "or"; the RCP 203 and asthma decks say "and." If a question offers only one, pick 12% and 200 mL for a formal PFT lab report and 12–15% for a bedside/PEFR screen.

Hazards, contraindications, and cautions

Heuer does not print a single consolidated "hazards of spirometry" box, but it scatters the tested hazards through Ch 3 and Ch 6 — collect them here:

  • Effort/consciousness limits: the SVC "can be obtained only with alert and cooperative patients"; MEP "can only be measured on an alert and cooperative patient"; MVV is "extremely effort dependent, so suboptimal effort could be interpreted as impairment."
  • MIP/NIF causes anxiety — "this process can cause anxiety in alert patients, you should provide a careful and reassuring explanation before performing the procedure."
  • Helium dilution: "Hypercapnia or hypoxemia may occur if CO2 is not removed or O2 not added." Leaks from poor mouth seal, perforated eardrums, or tracheostomies invalidate it.
  • N2 washout: "the ventilatory drive may be depressed in some patients who breathe 100% O2." Wait a minimum of 15 minutes before repeating.
  • Body plethysmography: "claustrophobic patients may not tolerate" the sealed box.
  • DLCO: "patients incapable of a breath-hold may not be able to perform the test."
  • Incentive spirometry: "If a patient performing incentive spirometry develops mild lightheadedness, dizziness, and tingling fingers, the likely cause is hyperventilation" — slow them down.
  • Exercise testing (the one place Heuer gives a formal contraindication list): general contraindications are acute MI, uncontrolled heart failure, unstable angina, significant cardiac dysrhythmias, acute pulmonary disorders, and severe hypertension; it requires direct physician supervision, a fully stocked crash cart with defibrillator, and ACLS-certified staff. Stop the test for wide swings in blood pressure, severe angina, dyspnea, serious arrhythmia, or dizziness/confusion/cyanosis.
  • 6MWT: not within one month of MI or unstable angina; relative contraindications resting HR > 120/min, SBP > 180 mm Hg, DBP > 100 mm Hg. Terminate O2 titration with exercise if SaO2 drops below 85%.
  • The classic AARC list (syncope, pneumothorax, bronchospasm, raised ICP, recent eye/chest/abdominal surgery) is not printed in Heuer or the decks — it lives in the AARC CPG only.
SourceHeuer Ch 4 — Interpreting Screening Spirometry / % Change After Bronchodilator (PDF p242–245) · Heuer App B — Table B-4, Percent Change & Percent Predicted (PDF p1044–1045) · Heuer Ch 5 — Table 5-3 Recommending PFTs (PDF p267–269) · Heuer Ch 3 — Box 3-6 Screening Spirometry Procedure (PDF p188–189), Table 3-7 (PDF p190–192), exercise testing (PDF p179), 6MWT (PDF p182) · Heuer Ch 6 — Table 6-35 ATS Guidelines (PDF p354), Table 6-36 (PDF p356) · Heuer Ch 1 (PDF p48) · RCP 203 Lssn 3a, slides 74–76 · RCP 203 Lssn 2b, slides 19–20, 65, 73–75 · Asthma deck, slide 23

3PFT Pattern Comparison — Obstructive vs Restrictive vs Mixed vs Normal vs Poor Effort

Work Heuer's algorithm (Figure 4-8) in strict order: FEV1% first → then VC → then TLC → then DLCO. Heuer defines the cutoffs on the figure itself: "Less than normal is < 70% for the FEV1% and < 80% predicted for the VC, TLC, and DLCO."

  • FEV1% < 70% → obstructive or mixed. Then DLCO splits it: low DLCO = emphysema; normal DLCO = asthma or chronic bronchitis (separate those two with a pre/post-bronchodilator study or a methacholine challenge).
  • FEV1% ≥ 70% → normal or restrictive. It is restrictive if the TLC is low. Then DLCO splits it: normal or high DLCO = chest wall or neuromuscular problem; low DLCO = interstitial lung disease such as pulmonary fibrosis.
PatternFVCFEV1FEV1/FVCTLCRVExamples
Normal≥ 80% predicted≥ 80% predicted≥ 70% (80–90% of FVC out in ~3 sec)≥ 80% predicted (≈ 6.0 L)≈ 1.2 L—
ObstructiveNormal or slightly ↓↓↓ (falls more than FVC)↓ < 70% (GOLD post-BD < 0.70)↑ (hyperinflation)↑ (air trapping; TGV > FRC)COPD, emphysema, chronic bronchitis, asthma, bronchiectasis, CF
Restrictive↓ < 80% predicted↓Normal or ↑ (preserved — FEV1 and FVC fall together)↓ < 80% predicted↓Pulmonary fibrosis/ILD, kyphoscoliosis, neuromuscular disease, obesity, large pleural effusion
Mixed / Combined↓↓↓ < 70%↓VariableAdvanced/overlap disease — deck rule: "all three ↓ = Combined"
Poor effortFalsely ↓ and NOT reproducibleFalsely ↓Often near-normal or uninterpretable——Sedated, confused, uncoached, or malingering patient

Graphic tells (Heuer Figures 4-5 to 4-7): an obstructive flow-volume loop shows a concave downward expiratory slope = dynamic airway collapse with reduced flows; a restrictive loop shows high flows with rapid emptying of a markedly reduced volume.

Recognizing poor effort — Heuer says it in one sentence (PDF p239): "because the test is so dependent on patient effort and starting lung volume, you should always consider the possibility of either poor effort or poor technique whenever the PEFR is significantly below normal." Practical tells:

  • Non-reproducibility. ATS: you need three acceptable maneuvers, and the two largest FVC and FEV1 must agree within 200 mL or 5%. PEFR must be repeated until three values vary by less than 10% — record the highest.
  • Jerky motion or a slow start — Heuer flags these as causing "flow or start-of-test errors"; back-extrapolated volume > 5% of FVC or 150 mL is the objective version.
  • Exhalation shorter than 6 seconds, or no 1-second plateau.
  • Effort-dependent tests that can all be faked low: PEFR, FVC, FEV1, MVV, SVC, MIP/NIF, MEP. The weaning deck's warning: VC and MIP are effort-dependent — poor effort makes a patient look weaker than they are. The neuro deck adds: "if a patient is heavily sedated, confused, or unable to follow commands, don't assume they have weak respiratory muscles — they may simply be unable to perform the test correctly."
  • Software tell (Heuer Ch 6): falsely high or low percent-normal computations usually mean incorrect patient data entry (age, height, sex, ethnicity) — re-enter and recompute before blaming the patient.
SourceHeuer Ch 4 — Figure 4-8 Interpretation of PF Laboratory Test Results (PDF p248), Interpreting Screening Spirometry & Table 4-21 (PDF p242–243), PEFR poor-effort caution (PDF p239) · Heuer Ch 6 — Table 6-35 ATS Guidelines (PDF p354) · Heuer Ch 3 — PEFR technique (PDF p140) · Heuer Ch 1 — Table 1-6 Restrictive vs Obstructive (PDF p49) · RCP 203 Lssn 3a, slides 74–75, 79 · RCP 203 Lssn 2b, slides 18, 20, 73, 76 · RCP 105 Wk 3 Weaning, slide 16 · Neuro-Respiratory Disorders deck, slides 15, 39 · COPD deck, slides 13, 16, 19

4Bronchoprovocation / Methacholine Challenge + the Three Lung-Volume Methods

Bronchoprovocation is used when spirometry is nondiagnostic but asthma is still suspected — the spirometry looks normal or fails to show clear obstruction or reversibility. Heuer lists four reasons to recommend it: assess the presence/severity of airway hyperreactivity, evaluate occupational asthma, determine the relative risk of developing asthma, and assess response to therapeutic interventions. Methacholine is a cholinergic (parasympathomimetic) agent given deliberately to provoke the airways — essentially an induced, controlled attack; the patient inhales it and spirometry is repeated.

POSITIVE METHACHOLINE CHALLENGE ≥ 20% decrease in FEV1 = airway hyperresponsiveness, consistent with asthma (Asthma deck, slide 23) WORKED EXAMPLE Pre-challenge FEV1 = 3.0 L 20% of 3.0 = 3.0 × 0.20 = 0.6 L Threshold = 3.0 − 0.6 = 2.4 L An FEV1 falling to 2.4 L or below is a POSITIVE test. HEUER'S PARALLEL NUMBER — EXERCISE CHALLENGE (Ch 4, PDF p223) "If the test is being done to detect exercise-induced bronchospasm, look for at least a 20% drop in FEV1 post-exercise."

Conflict/gap to know

Heuer never prints a numeric % fall for the methacholine challenge — it names the test and its indications only. The 20% figure comes from the Asthma deck (slide 23), and Heuer independently uses the same 20% FEV1 drop for the exercise challenge, so the number is safe to carry over.

The three FRC-measurement methods (Heuer Table 3-7 and Table 6-36):

MethodHow it worksWhat it actually measuresPreferred / limitations
Helium (He) dilution (closed circuit)Starting at FRC, patient rebreathes 5–10% He with soda-lime CO2 absorption and O2 added at ~0.25 L/min; test ends at equilibration (% He constant for 2 min)Actual FRC — only lung communicating with the airwaysEasy (tidal breathing only). Underestimates FRC in air trapping; severe air trapping requires a long time; leaks (poor seal, perforated eardrum, trach) ruin it; hypercapnia/hypoxemia if CO2 not removed or O2 not added
Nitrogen (N2) washout (open circuit)Starting at FRC, patient breathes 100% O2 for 7 minutes or until % N2 falls below 1.0%; expired N2 and volume measured continuouslyActual FRC — again only communicating lungOpen circuit, no CO2 absorber needed; distribution of ventilation can be read off the graph. Ventilatory drive may be depressed by 100% O2; assumes initial alveolar N2 of 80% after ≥ 15 min on room air; wait ≥ 15 min before repeating
Body plethysmography (body box)Patient pants against a closed shutter in a sealed box; Boyle's law (P1V1 = P2V2) converts box pressure change to thoracic volumeTotal Thoracic Gas Volume (TGV) — all intrathoracic gas, communicating or notPreferred whenever air trapping is suspected (bullous emphysema, severe COPD); quick and accurate; usually paired with airway resistance/conductance. Claustrophobic patients may not tolerate; needs specialized equipment; needs 3–5 acceptable panting maneuvers averaged at ~1 cycle/sec

The single tested sentence (Heuer Ch 3, PDF p189): "Normally, the FRC and TGV are equal. A TGV that exceeds FRC indicates the presence of 'trapped' gas that is not in communication with the airways, as seen in bullous emphysema and air-trapping." Both dilution methods are repeatable to ±500 mL in adults, and both require starting at FRC with no leaks. Once FRC is known: RV = FRC − ERV and TLC = FRC + IC.

SourceHeuer Ch 3 — Static Lung Volumes and Table 3-7 (PDF p187–192) · Heuer Ch 6 — Table 6-36 Indirect Spirometry Equipment (PDF p356) · Heuer Ch 5 — Table 5-3, Bronchial Provocation (PDF p268) · Heuer Ch 4 — exercise-induced bronchospasm 20% (PDF p223) · Asthma deck, slides 22–24 · RCP 203 Lssn 2b, slide 75 · RCP 203 Lssn 3b, slides 16–17 · Pharmacology Chptr 5 (CNS & PNS), slide 18

5Single-Breath N2 Washout — What It Measures and the Four Phases

What it measures

Heuer treats "N2 washout" as the multi-breath, open-circuit FRC method described in item 4, and adds one line about the graph: "Distribution of ventilation can be determined by the graph produced by this test." Heuer also lists closing volumes and distribution of ventilation among the tests a full PF lab performs, but does not work them up. The single-breath technique Heuer does detail is the single-breath DLCO: exhale to RV, inhale to TLC a mix of 21% O2, 10% He, 0.3% CO, 10-second breath-hold, discard the first (anatomic dead space) portion, then collect 0.5–1.0 L of expired gas for % He and % CO; repeat after ≥ 4 minutes until results agree within 5% or 3 mL/min/mm Hg. Helium gives the alveolar volume (VA, an estimate of TLC); CO uptake gives the DLCO.

Hess fills this (Hess, MacIntyre & Galvin, Respiratory Care: Principles and Practice, 4th ed. — not your 203 textbook, so treat it as supporting reference rather than the number your instructor grades from). Hess Figure 8-27 names all four: Phase I is dead space gas · Phase II is mixed gas (dead space and alveolar) · Phase III is alveolar gas · Phase IV is closing volume. The test itself is a maximal inspiration of 100% oxygen followed by a slow exhalation to RV, with a nitrogen analyser plotting expired nitrogen concentration against expired volume.

What each part tells you: both the slope of phase III and the percent change in nitrogen between 750 mL and 1250 mL of exhaled gas (ΔN₂ 750–1250) are indices of NON-UNIFORM ventilation. A flat phase III means relatively uniform gas distribution; as distribution becomes more heterogeneous, the slope of phase III steepens. The onset of phase IV marks the onset of airway closure in the dependent regions, and the volume exhaled during phase IV is the closing volume. With the onset of obstructive lung disease, both the closing volume and the slope of phase III increase.

Gap

⚠ Original note, now resolved from Hess but still true of your own sources: the four phases are NOT covered in Heuer or the available decks. I searched Heuer's full text for "single-breath," "nitrogen washout," "Phase I/III/IV," "alveolar plateau," "closing volume," and "closing capacity" — the only "Phase I–IV / alveolar plateau" material in the book is the capnogram (Figure 4-2), not the N2 curve, and the book index carries "nitrogen washout method, 80, 81t" with no phase discussion.

The decks are equally silent; the only adjacent statement is from RCP 203 Lssn 3a slide 31 notes: "closing volume (the volume at which small airways begin to collapse during expiration) increases with age," which traps gas in the dependent lung and lowers PaO2.

For the exam, get the phases from Egan's or Ruppel's: **Phase I = pure anatomic dead space (100% O2, no N2). Phase II = mixed dead space + alveolar gas (N2 rising steeply). Phase III = the alveolar plateau (slope reported as ΔN2/L). Phase IV = the abrupt upswing marking the onset of closing volume as dependent small airways close.*

What an abnormal (steep) Phase III slope means — the one thing to carry forward: a steep Phase III means uneven/nonuniform distribution of ventilation — poorly ventilated lung units empty last and are N2-rich. This is exactly the property Heuer credits to the N2 washout graph. A normal Phase III is nearly flat (< ~1.5–2% N2/L); it steepens in COPD, asthma, bronchiectasis, and CF, and with aging. An early Phase IV (large closing volume) means small airways are collapsing at a higher-than-normal lung volume — the aging mechanism the deck describes, and the reason dependent lung traps gas and PaO2 drifts down.

Do not confuse this with the capnogram, which Heuer does phase out (Figure 4-2): A–B = pure dead space, no CO2; B–C = dead space mixing with alveolar gas, CO2 rising; C–D = alveolar plateau (PetCO2 is read at the END of the plateau); D–E = inhalation of fresh gas, CO2 falls to zero. Heuer notes a clear alveolar plateau may never appear in COPD, CHF, auto-PEEP, V/Q mismatch, and pulmonary emboli.

SourceHeuer Ch 3 — N2 Washout in Table 3-7 and single-breath DLCO (PDF p189–192) · Heuer Ch 6 — Table 6-36 N2 Washout, "distribution of ventilation" (PDF p356), PF lab closing volumes (PDF p353) · Heuer Ch 4 — Figure 4-2 capnogram phases (PDF p209) · RCP 203 Lssn 2b, slides 75–76 · RCP 203 Lssn 3a, slide 31 (notes on closing volume and aging)

6Shunt Calculation — Estimate, Classic Equation, and the Normal-Value Conflict

A shunt is perfusion without ventilation — "wasted blood." Blood flows past alveoli that get no air and leaves the lung essentially unchanged, which is why shunt produces refractory hypoxemia that does not respond to more FiO2.

HEUER'S SHUNT METHOD IS THE ESTIMATE, NOT THE CLASSIC EQUATION. Heuer's Box 4-2 and App B Table B-2 both give only the P(A-a)O2 shortcut; the classic CcO2-based physiologic shunt equation does not appear anywhere in Heuer (I grepped the whole book for "shunt equation," "Qs/Qt," "CcO2," and "end-capillary"). Heuer says only that "when obtained at the same time, arterial and mixed venous samples can be used to compute cardiac output and physiologic shunt." So learn both — Heuer's estimate for the NBRC, the classic equation from the deck for the RCP 203/105 exam.

HEUER'S ESTIMATED-SHUNT SHORTCUT (Box 4-2, PDF p219; App B Table B-2, PDF p1041) Box title: "Estimating the Percent Shunt [Assuming PaO2 > 100 torr and C(a-v̄)O2 = 5 mL/dL]" RULE OF THUMB: breathing 100% O2, every 100 torr of P(A-a)O2 ≈ a 5% shunt. Step 1 — PAO2 = FiO2 × (PB − 47) − (1.25 × PaCO2) Step 2 — P(A-a)O2 = PAO2 − PaO2 Step 3 — % shunt = [P(A-a)O2 ÷ 100] × 5 HEUER WORKED EXAMPLE (Box 4-2) Patient on 100% O2 has a P(A-a)O2 of 300 torr. 300 ÷ 100 = 3 Approximate % shunt = 3 × 5 = 15% HEUER WORKED EXAMPLE, FULL CHAIN (App B, Problems B.9 + B.10) Given: FiO2 1.0, PaO2 250 torr, PaCO2 60 torr, PB 760 (sea level) PAO2 = 1.0 × (760 − 47) − (1.25 × 60) = 713 − 75 = 638 mm Hg (torr) P(A-a)O2 = 638 − 250 = 388 mm Hg (torr) % shunt = (388 ÷ 100) × 5 = 19.4% → Heuer prints 0.189 = 19% ("~20% physiologic shunt")
CLASSIC PHYSIOLOGIC SHUNT EQUATION — "gold standard" (RCP 105 Wk 3 Weaning, slide 14; NOT in Heuer) Qs CcO2 − CaO2 ---- = -------------- Qt CcO2 − Cv̄O2 WORKED EXAMPLE (deck slide 14) Given: PaO2 60, SaO2 90%, Pv̄O2 40, Sv̄O2 75%, Hb 15 g/dL, FiO2 1.0, PaCO2 40, PB 760, PH2O 47, RQ 0.8 Step 1 — Alveolar PO2 PAO2 = FiO2 × (PB − PH2O) − (PaCO2 ÷ RQ) PAO2 = 1.0 × (760 − 47) − (40 ÷ 0.8) = 713 − 50 = 663 mm Hg [same as Heuer's form: 1.25 × 40 = 50] Step 2 — Ideal end-capillary content (assume 100% saturated) CcO2 = (15 × 1.34 × 1.00) + (663 × 0.003) = 20.1 + 1.99 = 22.1 mL/dL Step 3 — Arterial content CaO2 = (15 × 1.34 × 0.90) + (60 × 0.003) = 18.1 + 0.18 = 18.3 mL/dL Step 4 — Mixed venous content Cv̄O2 = (15 × 1.34 × 0.75) + (40 × 0.003) = 15.1 + 0.12 = 15.2 mL/dL Step 5 — Shunt fraction Qs/Qt = (22.1 − 18.3) ÷ (22.1 − 15.2) = 3.8 ÷ 6.9 = 0.55 = 55% ANSWER: 55% of cardiac output is shunted — severely abnormal, consistent with ARDS or large areas of atelectasis.

NORMAL SHUNT FRACTION — THREE-WAY CONFLICT. Report all of them:

ValueSource
Normal shunt fraction is typically < 5%RCP 203 Lssn 3a slide 35 (notes); RCP 105 Wk 1 Lctr 3 slide 23 ("less than 5% of blood misses gas exchange")
A calculated physiologic shunt of 10% or less is considered normalRCP 105 Wk 3 Weaning slide 14
Heuer gives NO explicit "normal shunt %." It implies normal by giving P(A-a)O2 on 100% O2 = 25–65 torr as normal, which at 5% per 100 torr works out to ≈ 1–3% shunt; and it flags > 300 torr on 100% O2 as "severe shunting." Heuer's Box 4-2 also assumes C(a-v̄)O2 = 5 mL/dL and PaO2 > 100 torr for the estimate to holdHeuer Ch 4, Table 4-10 and Box 4-2 (PDF p218–219)

Grading (RCP 105 Wk 3 Weaning slide 14): 10–20% mild, 20–30% significant, > 30% critical/severe; Qs/Qt must be < 20% before a weaning attempt (with P/F ≥ 150). Lssn 3a slide 35 notes agree that shunt above 20–30% indicates significant oxygenation problems.

Heuer's other shunt-severity yardsticks (Table 4-10, PDF p218):

P/F ratio (PaO2 ÷ FiO2): Normal > 350–380 200–300 V/Q mismatch / mild ARDS 100–200 some shunting / moderate ARDS < 100 refractory hypoxemia, severe shunting / ARDS P(A-a)O2: abnormal if > 5–10 torr on room air > 25–65 torr on 100% O2 > 300 torr on 100% O2 = severe shunting a/A ratio (PaO2 ÷ PAO2): Normal > 0.75 0.35–0.75 hypoxemia, likely V/Q mismatch < 0.35 hypoxemia due to shunting Oxygenation Index OI = (FiO2 × mean airway pressure × 100) ÷ PaO2 Normal < 5–8; > 8 ≈ P/F < 200; > 20 severe (consider HFOV); > 40 high mortality / ECMO indication Heuer worked example (App B, B.13): OI = (1.0 × 20 × 100) ÷ 50 = 2000 ÷ 50 = 40

Deck-only A-a reference set worth keeping (RCP 105 Wk 3 Weaning slide 13): 5–15 mm Hg on room air normal; 25–65 mm Hg on 100% O2 normal; 66–300 mm Hg = V/Q mismatch → FiO2 up to 60%; > 300 mm Hg = shunting → ventilate with PEEP; weaning criterion P(A-a)O2 < 350 mm Hg on FiO2 1.0; age-adjusted room-air normal = (age ÷ 4) + 4.

SourceHeuer Ch 4 — Box 4-2 Estimating the Percent Shunt (PDF p219) and Table 4-10 (PDF p218) · Heuer App B — Table B-2, Alveolar PO2 / A-a Gradient / Percent Shunt Estimate / P/F / OI, Problems B.8–B.13 (PDF p1040–1042) · RCP 203 Lssn 3a, slides 34–35 · RCP 105 Wk 3 Weaning, slides 13–14 · RCP 105 Wk 1 Lctr 3, slides 22–24

7NIF/MIP and MEP — Respiratory Muscle Strength

MIP (Maximal Inspiratory Pressure), also called NIF (Negative Inspiratory Force), measures INSPIRATORY muscle strength — especially the diaphragm. MEP (Maximal Expiratory Pressure) measures EXPIRATORY muscle strength — the cough. Deck framing: MIP asks "how strong are the breathing muscles?" while VC asks "how much air can the lungs move?" — successful weaning requires both. Heuer's indications for the whole bedside set (VT, f, V̇E, VC, MIP, MEP): assess the progress of diseases affecting respiratory muscle strength (e.g., neuromuscular disorders) and evaluate a patient's potential need for mechanical ventilation.

How it is performed (Heuer Ch 3, PDF p143–144)"You measure MIP (aka negative inspiratory force [NIF]) using a manometer attached to a one-way valve configured to allow exhalation but not inspiration. With this setup, the patient 'bucks down' toward residual volume (RV) on each successive breath, at which point a maximum effort is ensured." The deck adds that the reading is taken over 20 seconds of occlusion, that the same one-way valve is simply flipped around to measure MEP, and that in some ventilated patients a waiting period off assisted ventilation is needed to induce the mild hypoxia and hypercapnia that drive maximal effort. Because the maneuver can cause anxiety in alert patients, give a careful and reassuring explanation first. The more negative the number, the stronger the muscles.

CONFLICT — does the patient have to be awake?

  • Heuer, Ch 3 (PDF p143): for MIP, the one-way-valve setup ensures maximum effort so "the patient need not be conscious." For MEP, Heuer says the opposite: "unlike MIP measurements, MEP does not require a one-way valve and can only be measured on an alert and cooperative patient."
  • RCP 203 Lssn 2b slide 19: "You can only measure MIP and MEP on an alert and cooperative patient."
  • Resolution for the exam: MEP definitely requires an alert, cooperative patient (both sources agree). For MIP, Heuer says consciousness is not required because of the one-way valve; the deck says it is. Heuer is the primary source — go with MIP can be obtained in an unconscious patient; MEP cannot unless the question is drawn straight off the slide.

VALUES — three sources, three sets of numbers. Know all of them.

ValueSource
Normal MIP/NIF: 80–100 cm H2O (negative), i.e. −80 to −100Heuer Ch 1, Table 1-15 (PDF p64–65)
Critical threshold: less negative than −20 to −25 cm H2O = "indicating possible inability to maintain adequate spontaneous ventilation"Heuer Ch 1, Table 1-15 (PDF p65)
T4 tip: "MIP/NIF values less negative than −20 to −25 cm H2O indicate that muscle strength likely is insufficient to support spontaneous ventilation."Heuer Ch 4, T4 Top Test-Taking Tips
Intubate a Guillain-Barré or myasthenia gravis patient if MIP/NIF < −25 cm H2O or MEP < 40 cm H2O (or rapidly declining), together with VC < 1.0 L or < 15 mL/kgHeuer Ch 20 (GB/MG case management)
MEP > 60 cm H2O (and/or peak cough flow > 60 L/min) = secretions can be cleared → extubation criterionHeuer Ch 9 and Ch 13 (extubation readiness)
MEP < 40 cm H2O and peak cough flow < 270 L/min (4.5 L/sec) = failing cough in neuromuscular diseaseHeuer Ch 20
Normal NIF: > −60 cm H2O; need a minimum of −20 on vent patients to be successfully extubated; > −40 for COPD patients; > −20 for myasthenia gravisRCP 203 Lssn 2b, slide 19 (+ notes)
Normal MIP/NIF: −80 to −100 cm H2ORCP 105 Wk 3 Weaning, slide 15; Neuro-Respiratory Disorders deck
Normal range −60 to −100 cm H2O; weaker than −30 = severe inspiratory muscle weakness (e.g. an MIP of −15 confirms inadequate ability to sustain ventilation)Neuro-Respiratory Disorders deck
Weaning readiness: −20 to −30 cm H2O generated in 20 secRCP 105 Wk 3 Weaning, slide 16
Weaning likely successful if MIP is at least −30 cm H2O; across 11 studies MIP averaged −37 cm H2O in weaned vs −30 cm H2O in non-weaned patientsRCP 105 Wk 3 Weaning, slide 15

Note the normal-value conflict

Heuer says normal MIP is −80 to −100 (agreeing with the weaning deck), while Lssn 2b slide 19 says "normal NIF is > −60." Both appear on exams — −80 to −100 is the textbook normal; anything weaker than −60 is abnormal; weaker than −20 to −25 means the patient cannot sustain spontaneous ventilation. The wording trap the decks call out: some books write "≥ −20 to −30" because they mean the magnitude — "the patient should generate at least 20–30 cm H2O of negative pressure" — which as a signed measurement is −20, −30, −40, −50 …

Companion weaning numbers (Heuer Table 1-15) to quote alongside MIP:

Parameter Reference range Critical threshold Tidal volume (VT) 5–7 mL/kg PBW < 4–5 mL/kg PBW or < 300 mL Respiratory rate (f) 12–20/min < 6/min or > 30–35/min Minute ventilation (V̇E) 5–10 L/min < 4 L/min or > 10 L/min RSBI (f ÷ VT in L) < 50 > 105 VD/VT 0.25–0.35 > 0.6–0.7 Vital capacity (VC) 70 mL/kg PBW < 10–15 mL/kg PBW MIP / NIF 80–100 cm H2O (neg.) < 20–25 cm H2O (negative) PBW male (kg) = 50 + 2.3 × (height in inches − 60) PBW female (kg) = 45.5 + 2.3 × (height in inches − 60) HEUER WORKED EXAMPLE — RSBI (Ch 3, PDF p141–142) f = 38/min, V̇E = 11.4 L/min VT = 11.4 ÷ 38 = 0.300 L (300 mL) RSBI = 38 ÷ 0.300 = 127 → > 105, predicts WEANING FAILURE

8Acute and Chronic Respiratory and Metabolic ABG Patterns

Start from the baseline: pH 7.40 / PaCO2 40 mm Hg / HCO3− 24 mEq/L. Heuer's normals (Table 1-4): pH 7.35–7.45, PaCO2 35–45 torr, PaO2 80–100 torr, HCO3− 22–26 mEq/L, BE 0 ± 2, SaO2 95–98%.

Heuer's four-step method: (1) categorize the pH; (2) determine respiratory involvement (PaCO2); (3) determine metabolic involvement (HCO3−); (4) assess for compensation.

Primary disturbance from pH + PaCO2 alone (Heuer Ch 1, Figure 1-1):

  • Normal pH + normal PaCO2 → normal
  • pH < 7.35 with PaCO2 > 45 → respiratory acidosis; pH < 7.35 with PaCO2 ≤ 45 → metabolic acidosis
  • pH > 7.45 with PaCO2 < 35 → respiratory alkalosis; pH > 7.45 with PaCO2 ≥ 35 → metabolic alkalosis

HEUER'S COMPENSATION RULES — this is a BASE-EXCESS method, not the "for every 10 mm Hg" method:

1. COMPENSATION IS OCCURRING if the PaCO2 and BE are BOTH abnormally high or BOTH abnormally low. Then use the pH to name the primary problem: pH < 7.40 → the primary problem is the one causing ACIDOSIS pH > 7.40 → the primary problem is the one causing ALKALOSIS 2. FULL vs PARTIAL: pH back inside 7.35–7.45 → compensation is FULL pH still outside 7.35–7.45 → compensation is PARTIAL 3. COMBINED DISTURBANCE if the PaCO2 and BE DIVERGE in opposite directions: high PaCO2 + low BE = combined respiratory AND metabolic ACIDOSIS low PaCO2 + high BE = combined respiratory AND metabolic ALKALOSIS 4. SPEED: Renal/metabolic compensation for a respiratory problem is SLOW (hours to DAYS) ← this is why "acute" gases have a near-normal HCO3− Respiratory compensation for a metabolic problem is FAST (MINUTES) "a failure of the lungs to quickly compensate for a primary renal/metabolic acid-base disturbance indicates impaired pulmonary function" 5. BE confirms metabolic involvement: BE > +2 = base gain (metabolic alkalosis) BE < −2 = base deficit (metabolic acidosis)

The deck's numeric rules of thumb (RCP 103 ABG deck) — use these when a question gives you a PaCO2 change and asks for the expected pH: for every 10 mm Hg rise in PaCO2, pH falls ~0.06 and HCO3− rises ~1 mEq/L; for every 20 mm Hg rise, pH falls ~0.12 and HCO3− rises ~2 mEq/L. For metabolic problems: for every 1 mEq/L fall in HCO3−, PaCO2 should fall ~1.2 mm Hg (begins within the first hour, complete by 12–24 hours); for every 1 mEq/L rise in HCO3−, PaCO2 rises ~0.7 mm Hg.

WORKED COMPENSATION CHECK Baseline: pH 7.40, PaCO2 40, HCO3− 24 The patient's PaCO2 rises acutely to 60 mm Hg. ΔPaCO2 = 60 − 40 = 20 mm Hg 20 ÷ 10 = 2 increments pH : 2 × 0.06 = 0.12 → 7.40 − 0.12 = 7.28 expected pH HCO3− : 2 × 1 = 2 → 24 + 2 = 26 mEq/L expected If the measured pH and HCO3− are BOTH LOWER than predicted, suspect an added metabolic (lactic) acidosis layered on the respiratory acidosis. Heuer's version of the same catch: PaCO2 high + BE low = COMBINED acidosis.
DisorderpHPaCO2HCO3− / BEExample gas values

Build it from Heuer's own rules: a low PaCO2 with a low BE, both abnormal in the same direction = compensation occurring, and pH > 7.40 = the primary problem is the alkalosis. Typical constructed set: pH 7.44 / PaCO2 28 / HCO3− 19 / BE −5 — chronic hyperventilation of high altitude, pregnancy, chronic liver disease, or CNS disease. Nearest printed confirmation: RCP 105 Mgmt of Mech Vent deck, slide notes — "in compensated respiratory alkalosis the pH is on the alkalotic side of normal range (7.41 to 7.45)."

Hypoxemia severity on room air (Heuer Ch 1): mild PaO2 60–79 torr, moderate 40–59 torr, severe < 40 torr — but a PaO2 < 60 on FiO2 ≥ 0.60 is severe regardless.

9Inspiratory Flow Demand — How to Calculate It

The normal adult inspiratory flow is 40–60 L/min (RCP 105 Wk 1 Lctr 3, slide 2). That is the number to compare every calculated answer against. Heuer's practical restatement: to guarantee a stable FiO2, a high-flow O2 system must deliver a total flow of at least 40 L/min, "because the higher the FiO2, the higher the input flow (flowmeter setting) required to meet the patient's inspiratory demand."

HEUER'S FORMULA — set the flow from minute volume and %I-time (Ch 13, Box 13-1):

STEP 1 V̇E = f × VT STEP 2 %I-time (duty cycle) = I part of I:E ratio ---------------------- I part + E part STEP 3 Inspiratory flow (L/min) = V̇E ÷ %I-time (use the DECIMAL for %I-time) HEUER WORKED EXAMPLE (Box 13-1) An apneic patient on volume-control A/C at f = 15/min with VT = 600 mL. The physician orders an I:E ratio of 1:4. What flow do you set? Step 1 — minute volume V̇E = 15 breaths/min × 600 mL = 9,000 mL/min = 9 L/min Step 2 — convert I:E to %I-time 1:4 → 1 ÷ (1 + 4) = 1 ÷ 5 = 0.20 (20% I-time) Step 3 — inspiratory flow Flow = 9 L/min ÷ 0.20 = 45 L/min ANSWER: set 45 L/min — squarely inside the normal 40–60 L/min range. ✔ QUICK %I-TIME CONVERSIONS (from the same formula) I:E 1:1 → 1 ÷ 2 = 0.50 (50%) I:E 1:2 → 1 ÷ 3 = 0.33 (33%) I:E 1:3 → 1 ÷ 4 = 0.25 (25%) I:E 1:4 → 1 ÷ 5 = 0.20 (20%) I:E 1:5 → 1 ÷ 6 = 0.17 (17%) CROSS-CHECK USING TIME Ti (sec) = VT (L) ÷ flow (L/sec) [flow L/min ÷ 60 = L/sec] Same example: 45 L/min ÷ 60 = 0.75 L/sec Ti = 0.6 L ÷ 0.75 L/sec = 0.8 sec Total cycle time = 60 ÷ 15 = 4.0 sec Te = 4.0 − 0.8 = 3.2 sec → I:E = 0.8 : 3.2 = 1:4 ✔
Why it matters clinicallyInsufficient inspiratory flow produces a long inspiratory time, which shortens expiratory time and causes air trapping / auto-PEEP. Heuer: patients with severe expiratory airflow obstruction breathe spontaneously at I:E ratios of 1:4 or 1:5, and if you do not match that on the ventilator, auto-PEEP will occur; the fix is to decrease the I:E ratio by increasing E-time and decreasing I-time — that is, by RAISING the inspiratory flow. Normal I:E is 1:2 to 1:4. Conversely, an excessive flow setting shows up on waveforms as a pressure peak achieved too early (PC) or an upward spike on inspiratory flow (VC) — reduce the flow.

Flow and resistance (RCP 105 Wk 1 Lctr 3, slides 2–4; Heuer Ch 4 T4 tips):

Raw = ΔP ÷ V̇ where ΔP = PIP − Pplat and V̇ = inspiratory flow (L/sec) Crs (static compliance) = VT ÷ (Pplat − PEEP) Cdyn = VT ÷ (PIP − PEEP) Cdyn is ALWAYS less than Crs; the gap is Raw Normal Raw (deck) = 0.6–2.4 cm H2O/L/sec (≈ 1–3) Heuer (Ch 1) = values above 5–9 cm H2O/L/sec are associated with obstruction Deck thresholds = Raw > 5 → airway obstruction Raw > 10 → severe bronchospasm, mucus plugging, kinked/obstructed ETT Heuer adds: an ETT itself adds at least 4–6 cm H2O/L/sec to total resistance. Ventilator clue: High PIP + NORMAL Pplat → RESISTANCE problem (bronchospasm, secretions, ETT obstruction) High PIP + HIGH Pplat → COMPLIANCE problem (ARDS, pulmonary edema, pneumothorax, atelectasis)

Flow demand outside the ventilator

Heuer's oxygen-device numbers are the other half of this topic: low-flow devices deliver less than the patient's inspiratory flow, so the FiO2 is only an estimate that varies with rate, depth, and inspiratory flow; high-flow devices deliver flows exceeding the patient's inspiratory flow, so the FiO2 stays precise. Titrate a high-flow device by changing FiO2, never by lowering the flow — dropping the flow leaves the FiO2 unchanged but may fail to meet inspiratory demand (and on a reservoir system can cause CO2 rebreathing).

If a patient's demand outruns the device, recommend a high-flow nasal cannula at 20–40 L/min, add a second air-entrainment nebulizer in tandem, or bleed in supplemental O2. Note also that a DPI requires the patient to generate > 60 L/min of inspiratory flow.

SourceHeuer Ch 13 — Box 13-1 Determination of Inspiratory Flow Setting During Volume-Control Ventilation, %I-time and I:E computations · Heuer Ch 6 — Air-Entrainment Systems, total flow ≥ 40 L/min (PDF p330–331), Table 6-5 · Heuer Ch 11 — troubleshooting inspiratory flow demand, HFNC 20–40 L/min · Heuer Ch 3/Ch 4 — Raw, Crs, Cdyn T4 tips (PDF p194–195) · Heuer Ch 1 — Raw > 5–9 cm H2O/L/sec (PDF p66) · RCP 105 Wk 1 Lctr 3, slides 2–4 · RCP 105 Wk 1 Lctr 5, slide 19 · RCP 105 Wk 6 Ventilator Waveform Analysis, slides on excessive flow · RCP 105 Management of Mechanical Ventilation, slide 32 · RCP 203 Lssn 3a, slides 57–59

Capnography, Hemodynamic Monitoring & Transcutaneous Monitoring

6 items · Capnography and the capnogram · PaCO2 and PETCO2 · hemodynamic measurements · pulmonary artery catheters · transcutaneous monitoring

1Explain Capnography; how to interpret a Capnogram2Capnography: how to measure; difference between waveforms, sudden increases or decreases3PaCO2 and PETCO2 relationships4Explain Hemodynamic Measurements; CVP and PA Catheters — normal ranges5Pulmonary Artery Catheters – hemodynamic parameters6Explain Transcutaneous Monitoring – electrode placement, troubleshooting

1Explain Capnography; how to interpret a Capnogram

Capnography is the measurement of the partial pressure of CO2 in a gas sample, displayed graphically across the whole respiratory cycle; when the sample is taken at the very end of exhalation it is the end-tidal PCO2 (PETCO2 / EtCO2). Know the three words apart: capnometry = the CO2 number, capnography = number + waveform, capnogram = the actual waveform. The normal capnogram has four phases: A to B (Phase I) = exhalation of pure anatomic deadspace gas, PCO2 near 0 mm Hg; B to C (Phase II) = rapid upstroke as deadspace gas mixes with alveolar gas; C to D (Phase III) = the alveolar plateau, pure alveolar gas, and PETCO2 is measured at the very end of this plateau; D to E (Phase IV / inspiratory downstroke) = inhalation of fresh gas, CO2 falls back to zero.

A good tracing must have a zero inspiratory baseline (PETCO2 = 0 torr) — if the baseline does not return to zero, think CO2 rebreathing or a circuit/equipment problem. Abnormal shapes the decks name: the bronchospasm/obstructive tracing (shown as the dotted line on Chang's Figure 9-13 — a sloping, blunted upstroke and plateau rather than a square wave); kinked or obstructed ET tube, where PETCO2 falls in proportion to the obstruction and reads zero with complete obstruction; sudden loss of waveform and numbers = circuit disconnection; and a sudden proportional drop with cardiac arrest from failed lung perfusion.

The terms "shark fin" and "curare cleft" are not used in the available slide decks — the obstructive pattern is described only as the bronchospasm tracing; look up "curare cleft" (spontaneous effort against a paralytic wearing off) in Chang Fig. 9-13 / Persing Chapter 4 waveform figures.

SourceChang Ch.9 – Monitoring in Mechanical Ventilation, slides 44–46, 48; RCP 203 Lesson 3a (Chp 4), slides 20–22; RCP 203 Lesson 2b (Chp 3), slide 10; RCP 203 Lesson 3b (Chp 5), slide 23

2Capnography: how to measure; difference between waveforms, sudden increases or decreases

Exhaled CO2 is measured by infrared absorption using one of two sensor styles. A mainstream sensor sits directly in the ventilator circuit on an ET-tube adaptor: its advantage is fast response time between sampling and display, and water condensation does not affect it; its disadvantages are excess weight on the ET tube, added circuit deadspace, inaccuracy from secretions on the cell windows, and more frequent handling (it must be manually disconnected to suction).

A sidestream (aspirating) sensor keeps the analyzer inside the monitor and draws a sample through small-bore tubing: its advantages are ease of handling and that it can be attached to a cannula or mask (not just an ET tube); its disadvantages are secretions/water occluding the sample line, equipment contamination, and a lag time a few tenths of a second longer than mainstream (clinically negligible).

Setup: calibrate per manufacturer, complete the power-on self-test, confirm a zero inspiratory baseline, compare the reading against a current PaCO2, and set alarms (example only, per protocol: PETCO2 high 50–55 torr, low 25–30 torr; RR 7–35/min for adults; apnea alarm 20–30 sec).

Change in ETCO2Possible causes (per the decks)
Sudden drop to zeroVentilator/circuit disconnection (immediate disappearance of values and waveform); complete ET tube obstruction/kink; esophageal intubation (no waveform — waveform capnography is the gold standard for confirming tracheal placement)
Sudden decrease (not to zero)Cardiac arrest / falling cardiac output — values drop suddenly and proportionally because lung perfusion is inadequate; partial ET tube obstruction or kink; increased alveolar deadspace (e.g., pulmonary embolism) — this is not an improvement in ventilation, so do not lower the ventilator rate
Gradual increaseHypoventilation trend (rising PaCO2 — confirm with ABG); increased CO2 production / rising metabolic rate; a baseline that rises off zero indicates rebreathing in the circuit
Gradual decreaseHyperventilation trend (falling PaCO2 — confirm with ABG); rising physiologic deadspace / worsening V/Q; falling cardiac output or lung perfusion (during CPR, PETCO2 < 10 torr means poor or absent pulmonary blood flow; a rise in PETCO2 signals effective compressions or ROSC)
SourceChang Ch.9 – Monitoring in Mechanical Ventilation, slides 44, 46, 48; RCP 203 Lesson 2b (Chp 3), slide 10; RCP 203 Lesson 3b (Chp 5), slides 22–24; RCP 105 Airway Management (Hess Chp 17), slide 49

3PaCO2 and PETCO2 relationships

PETCO2 correlates well with PaCO2 and is normally slightly lower than it, because exhaled gas is diluted by deadspace gas. The RCP 203 deck gives normal values of PaCO2 ≈ 35–45 mm Hg and PETCO2 ≈ 30–43 mm Hg, with PETCO2 typically running 1–5 torr less than PaCO2. The two decks give different numbers for the gradient and you should know both: Chang states the normal P(a-et)CO2 gradient is about 2 mm Hg in normal individuals, with a gradient of 5 mm Hg considered acceptable in critically ill patients; the RCP 203 (Persing) decks state 1–5 torr.

The gradient is widened primarily by alveolar deadspace ventilation (classically pulmonary embolism), and also by old age, presence of pulmonary disease, and changes in mechanical tidal volume and ventilator modality. Clinically this matters because PETCO2 is a static measure: a fall in PETCO2 caused by deadspace does not mean ventilation improved, so the ventilator frequency must not be reduced on that basis. Once a good PaCO2-to-PETCO2 correlation is established for a given patient, the number of routine ABGs can be reduced.

SourceChang Ch.9 – Monitoring in Mechanical Ventilation, slides 44, 47, 48; RCP 203 Lesson 2b (Chp 3), slide 10; RCP 203 Lesson 3a (Chp 4), slides 20–21

4Explain Hemodynamic Measurements; CVP and PA Catheters — normal ranges

Invasive hemodynamic monitoring works because liquid is noncompressible and pressure applied at one point in a fluid-filled closed system is transmitted equally to every other point at the same level; a transducer converts that pressure signal into an electronic signal that is amplified and displayed. Values are reported in mm Hg in the U.S. (kPa under SI units). Three catheter types are used: an arterial catheter (systemic BP, radial is first choice), a central venous catheter (SVC or right atrium), and a pulmonary artery / Swan-Ganz catheter (PAP, PCWP, CO, SvO2).

Always level the transducer to the phlebostatic axis (4th intercostal space, midaxillary line) and read CVP and PAWP at end-expiration; do not remove the patient from PEEP/CPAP to measure. If PEEP ≤ 10 cm H2O just read at end-expiration; if PEEP > 10 cm H2O, use Corrected pressure = Measured pressure − [0.5 × (PEEP/1.36)].

ParameterNormal range (units)Deck source
CVP (vena cava)0–6 mm Hg (mean)Chang Ch.10 sl. 14
CVP (RCP 103 deck — conflicting)2–6 mm HgRCP 103 Clinical Data sl. 12

SvO2 (mixed venous sat)75% (range 68–77%)Chang Ch.10 sl. 32

What raises and lowers each

CVP/RAP is the right-heart filling pressure (RV preload): it rises with positive pressure ventilation, right ventricular failure (cor pulmonale from chronic pulmonary hypertension, right-sided MI), and fluid overload; it falls with hypovolemia. Because venous return = MAP − CVP, a rising CVP narrows that gradient, so venous return falls, ventricular output falls, and cardiac output falls. PAP rises with mechanical ventilation and PEEP, increased PVR, left ventricular dysfunction or failure, and mitral valve disease, and falls with hypovolemia (ventilator effects are variable and depend on PIP, PEEP, compliance, and hemodynamic status).

PCWP estimates left atrial pressure and LV preload (LVEDP): PCWP ≥ 18 mm Hg with a near-normal PAP means left ventricular dysfunction/left heart failure, and cardiogenic pulmonary edema has a PCWP > 18 mm Hg while non-cardiogenic edema (ARDS, increased capillary permeability, acute pulmonary hypertension) has a normal or low PCWP (< 18 mm Hg); mitral stenosis or regurgitation also elevate PCWP.

CO/SV fall with low contractility, decreased preload (hypovolemia, shock), and increased afterload (downstream obstruction, hypervolemia). SVR is elevated in systemic hypertension; PVR is elevated in pulmonary hypertension and left heart obstruction. SvO2 falls (50–70%) with decreased O2 delivery (low cardiac output, anemia, hypoxic hypoxia) or increased O2 consumption (fever, seizures, increased WOB, stress, pain); 30–50% = onset of lactic acidosis, 25–30% = severe lactic acidosis, < 25% = cellular death.

SvO2 rises (uncommon) with an improperly wedged PA catheter, reduced O2 consumption (hypothermia, sedation/analgesia, low metabolic rate), and sepsis or cyanide poisoning where tissues cannot use oxygen. Remember the surrogates: CVP = RV preload, PCWP = LV preload, PVR = RV afterload, SVR = LV afterload.

SourceChang Ch.10 – Hemodynamic Monitoring, slides 3–5, 8–10, 12, 14–15, 19, 21, 23, 25–33; RCP 203 Lesson 2b (Chp 3), slide 33; RCP 103 – Clinical Data, slides 9, 12; Pulmonary Edema and PVD, slide 66

5Pulmonary Artery Catheters – hemodynamic parameters

The pulmonary artery (Swan-Ganz) catheter is a flow-directed, balloon-tipped catheter, typically 110 cm long with three lumens, marked in 10-cm segments so the tip depth can be estimated. It carries a distal PA port at the tip, a proximal injectate port about 30 cm back (sits in the right atrium), a balloon near the tip with a 1.5 mL maximum inflation volume, and a thermistor at the tip; a fiberoptic element allows continuous SvO2.

Path: inserted into the subclavian or internal jugular vein → superior vena cava → right atrium (balloon is then inflated, and blood flow carries it like wind in a sail) → right ventricle → pulmonary artery → wedged in a smaller branch of the pulmonary artery, at which point the balloon is deflated and the catheter secured. PAP is read with the balloon deflated; the wedge (PCWP) is obtained by momentarily re-inflating the balloon, reading at end-expiration, then deflating the balloon immediately.

PCWP estimates left atrial pressure and, indirectly, left ventricular end-diastolic pressure (LVEDP) — i.e., LV preload. The PCWP waveform components are the a wave (left atrial contraction), x descent (fall in LA pressure after contraction), c wave (mitral valve closure, if present), v wave (LV contraction with passive atrial filling), and y descent (fall in pressure after the mitral valve opens) — the analogous CVP waveform shows the same letters for the right side (a = atrial systole after the P wave, c = tricuspid closure, v = ventricular systole after the T wave, y = tricuspid opening).

Cardiac output is measured by thermodilution: 10 mL of iced or room-temperature D5W is injected into the proximal port and the temperature change at the thermistor is used to compute flow. Hess fills this (Hess, MacIntyre & Galvin, Respiratory Care: Principles and Practice, 4th ed. — not your 203 textbook, so treat it as supporting reference rather than the number your instructor grades from).

Hess Ch 3 describes the tracings and the pressures. Right ventricle (Figure 3-10): the waveform's components are labelled rapid filling (RF), slow filling (SF), atrial contraction (a), end-diastole (ed), and systole (Sys); normal RV systolic pressure is 15 to 30 mm Hg and RV end-diastolic pressure is 0 to 8 mm Hg. A raised RV systolic pressure occurs in pulmonary hypertension and pulmonic valve stenosis, and in acute pulmonary embolism — though Hess notes RV systolic pressures rarely exceed 40 to 50 mm Hg in the acute setting, a useful discriminator from chronic disease. A raised RV end-diastolic pressure is seen in cardiomyopathy, RV ischaemia or infarction, and cardiac constriction or tamponade.

Pulmonary artery (Figure 3-11)

"The main components of the PA tracing are the systolic and diastolic pressures and the dicrotic notch, which represents closure of the pulmonic valve." Normal PA systolic 15 to 30 mm Hg; normal PA diastolic 4 to 12 mm Hg. Elevated PA pressures occur with volume overload or with raised pulmonary vascular resistance — Hess's Table 3-4 lists left heart failure of any cause, primary lung disease, mitral valve disease, pulmonary embolism, hypoxaemia with pulmonary vasoconstriction, idiopathic pulmonary arterial hypertension, and left-to-right shunts. The occlusion (wedge) tracing is obtained by inflating the balloon at the catheter tip to obstruct flow.

Gap⚠ Original note: the chamber-by-chamber waveform appearance is not described in the available slide decks beyond the CVP and PCWP waveforms above — look up the chamber-by-chamber insertion tracing in Chang Figure 10-7. Complication lists specific to the PA catheter are also not covered in the decks; the decks list complications only for the arterial catheter — bleeding, blood clot, and infection, plus measurement problems from air bubbles and loose connections (damped signal), improper transducer leveling (false high/low readings), inadequate pressure on the flush bag (blood backs up in the tubing), and clotting at the catheter tip or the tip lodging against the vessel wall. Look up PA catheter complications (balloon rupture, pulmonary infarction, arrhythmias, knotting) in Chang Chapter 10 Table 10-8.
SourceChang Ch.10 – Hemodynamic Monitoring, slides 11, 16–19, 21–23, 25; RCP 203 Lesson 2b (Chp 3), slides 33–35

6Explain Transcutaneous Monitoring – electrode placement, troubleshooting

Transcutaneous monitoring gives continuous, noninvasive estimates of arterial PO2 and PCO2 through a heated sensor on the skin. The PtcO2 electrode is a combined platinum and silver electrode under an oxygen-permeable, hydrophobic (water-repelling) membrane with a reservoir of phosphate buffer and potassium chloride; the membrane lets O2 and CO2 through but blocks moisture and electrolytes. Heat is essential — a heating element arterializes the capillary bed: 44°C is the usual setting when monitoring both PtcO2 and PtcCO2, as low as 37°C is adequate if only PtcCO2 is monitored, and Chang states 44°C for PtcCO2 with 40–42°C in neonates, maximum 45°C.

Placement: choose a site with good superficial circulation — the side of the chest, the abdomen, or the inner thigh — clean with alcohol, apply the adhesive fixation ring, apply contact gel to the sensor face avoiding air bubbles, seat the sensor flat with the edges sealed, and allow 5–20 minutes to stabilize. Burn risk drives site rotation: the decks conflict slightly — Chang says change the site every 4 hours to avoid erythema and burns; the RCP 203 procedure deck says every 2–6 hours depending on sensor temperature, infant size, and manufacturer recommendation.

Population: it suits neonates and infants best — in neonates PtcO2 closely approximates PaO2, keep PtcO2 in the 50–80 torr trend range (Chang: optimal 50–70 mm Hg), set the high PtcO2 infant alarm around 80 torr, and infant PtcO2 is the best way to screen for hyperoxemia and help prevent retinopathy of prematurity (ROP). In adults PtcO2 reads low because of thicker skin, so pulse oximetry is preferred; PtcCO2, however, is reliable in hemodynamically stable adults and is a good choice when capnography is difficult, e.g., during NPPV.

What makes it inaccurate: poor perfusion / shock / low cardiac output / dehydration (a disproportionate fall in PtcO2; PtcCO2 reads falsely high from tissue CO2 accumulation), skin edema, hypothermia, a PaO2 or operating range above 80–100 mm Hg, an underheated sensor, placement on a bony surface, too much pressure or too much contact gel, poor skin integrity or adhesive allergy, and the 5–20 minute setup time that rules it out in emergencies.

PtcCO2 normally reads higher than PaCO2 because heating the tissue increases local CO2 production. If the PaO2 is substantially higher than the PtcO2, suspect poor peripheral circulation; after setup or a site change, correlate against a simultaneous arterial or capillary sample and record the monitor value at the moment the sample is drawn. Two extras: dual-sensor PtcO2 screens newborns for critical congenital heart disease (CCHD) — suspect it if the pre-ductal (right upper chest) PtcO2 is ≥ 15 torr higher than the post-ductal (lower abdomen/thigh) value — and wound-tissue PtcO2 > 30–40 torr indicates perfusion adequate for healing.

SourceChang Ch.9 – Monitoring in Mechanical Ventilation, slides 49–51; RCP 203 Lesson 2b (Chp 3), slides 11–12; RCP 203 Lesson 3a (Chp 4), slides 18–19; RCP 203 Lesson 3b (Chp 5), slide 21; RCP 203 Lesson 1 (Chp 1), slide 44
