Neonatal Scoring, Pediatric Airway Emergencies & Disease Comparison

5 items · APGAR · L/S ratio and mature surfactant · croup vs epiglottitis · bacterial PNA, emphysema, PE and pulmonary fibrosis · hypoxia in the infant

1How to Obtain an APGAR Score2L/S Ratio and Mature Surfactant Production3Croup vs. Epiglottitis4Lab Values and Presentation: Bacterial PNA vs. Emphysema vs. PE vs. Pulmonary Fibrosis5How Hypoxia Appears in an Infant

1How to Obtain an APGAR Score

The Apgar score is taken on every newborn at 1 minute and again at 5 minutes after birth. You score five clinical signs — Appearance, Pulse, Grimace, Activity, Respirations — each from 0 to 2, then add them up for a minimum of 0 and a maximum of 10. It was created in 1952 by Dr. Virginia Apgar, an anesthesiologist, to standardize newborn assessment.

The single most important rule on the slide is the red statement: needed interventions must never be delayed to obtain the Apgar score — Apgar does not decide whether resuscitation begins. Resuscitation decisions are made from the baby's condition immediately after birth; you do not wait for the 1-minute score.

Component (mnemonic)What is being assessedScore range
A — AppearanceColor0 / 1 / 2
P — PulseHeart rate0 / 1 / 2
G — GrimaceReflex irritability0 / 1 / 2
A — ActivityMuscle tone0 / 1 / 2
R — RespirationsRespiratory effort0 / 1 / 2
TotalSum of all five0–10
TimingTotal scoreInterpretation
1 minute and 5 minutes after birth7–10Normal
"4–6Moderately depressed — typically needs more intensive support
"0–3Severely depressed — usually undergoes resuscitation; requires immediate assessment/support

Hess fills this (Hess, MacIntyre & Galvin, Respiratory Care: Principles and Practice, 4th ed. — not your 203 textbook, so treat it as supporting reference rather than the number your instructor grades from). Hess Table 22-1 prints every row:

Parameter012
Heart rateNone< 100 beats/min> 100 beats/min
Respiratory effortNoneWeak, irregularStrong cry
ColorPale blueBody pink, extremities blueCompletely pink
Reflex (irritability to suction)No responseGrimaceCry, cough, or sneeze
Muscle toneLimpSome flexionWell flexed

Hess adds the timing and the cautions: the score was introduced in 1952 by Virginia Apgar and is assigned at 1, 5, and 10 minutes of life. "Therapeutic interventions should not be delayed to assign the 1-minute score" — the 1-minute score simply gives an immediate evaluation to guide intervention. When the score is less than 7 at 5 minutes, an additional score is usually assigned at 10 minutes. And the grim one: if the Apgar score remains 0 after 10 minutes of resuscitation, the infant is unlikely to survive. Finally, a limitation worth knowing: the Apgar score is not as useful for babies born prematurely, because three of the five criteria — muscle tone, respiratory effort, and reflex irritability — depend on neurological maturity.

Original note: the deck names the five components and the 0–2 scale but does not print the individual 0/1/2 descriptor criteria (e.g., exact heart-rate cutoffs) for each row — look those up in the textbook table on p.40 if the exam asks for the specific descriptors.

SourceRCP 203 Lssn 2a (Chp 2 – Perform Clinical Assessment), slide 28

2L/S Ratio and Mature Surfactant Production

Your instructor excluded this, but Heuer covers it — know both facts. The RCP 203 lecture notes say directly: "Do not study the biochemical-analysis details in that table — L/S ratio, PG, L/A ratio, etc." So it is unlikely to be tested in this course. Heuer, however, states the numbers plainly, and they are standard NBRC content:

  • Lecithin/sphingomyelin (L/S) ratio > 2:1 indicates fetal lung maturity. Lecithin (a surfactant phospholipid) rises sharply near term while sphingomyelin stays roughly flat, so the ratio rising past 2 is the maturity signal.
  • Lecithin/albumin (L/A) ratio ≥ 40.0 is the parallel maturity threshold Heuer gives.
  • Phosphatidylglycerol (PG) present is a confirmatory maturity marker, particularly useful in diabetic mothers where the L/S ratio can mislead.

An immature ratio predicts infant respiratory distress syndrome (IRDS) from surfactant deficiency — the reason for antenatal steroids and postnatal surfactant replacement.

SourceHeuer Ch 1 — Maternal and Perinatal/Neonatal History (PDF p53-55) · RCP 203 Lssn 2a notes (explicitly de-emphasised)

What the decks do teach about surfactant: lecithin is named in the pharmacology chapter as another name for dipalmitoylphosphatidylcholine (DPPC), the surfactant component predominantly responsible for reducing alveolar surface tension. Surfactant is 85–90% lipid by weight, mostly phosphatidylcholine (75–80%), plus proteins SP-A, SP-B, SP-C, SP-D. It is synthesized in Type II alveolar cells and stored in vesicles called lamellar bodies, with lung inflation the major stimulus for secretion.

Because 90–95% of surfactant is taken back up by the Type II cell, reprocessed, and re-secreted, one or two doses of exogenous surfactant are usually enough. The basic problem in RDS is lack of surfactant from lung immaturity → high surface tension → higher ventilating pressures needed; prophylaxis is indicated in premature infants < 29 weeks gestational age at high risk, or weighing < 1250 grams at birth.

ConceptWhat the decks say
Lecithin= DPPC (dipalmitoylphosphatidylcholine); the component predominantly responsible for lowering alveolar surface tension
SphingomyelinMembrane phospholipid that stays ~constant through gestation — the stable denominator that makes the rising L/S ratio meaningful.
L/S ratio for maturityExplicitly excluded ("do not study… L/S ratio, PG, L/A ratio")
Phosphatidylglycerol (PG)Named only in that same exclusion note — no role given
Where made / storedType II alveolar cells; stored in lamellar bodies; secretion stimulated by lung inflation
CompositionLipids 85–90% by weight; phosphatidylcholine 75–80%; proteins SP-A, SP-B, SP-C, SP-D
Recycling90–95% reabsorbed and re-secreted → 1–2 doses of exogenous surfactant usually suffice
Prematurity thresholdsProphylaxis for RDS at < 29 weeks gestation, or birth weight < 1250 g
SourceChptr 10 – Surfactant Agents, slides 11–13, 18 (and slide 16 for the < 1250 g prophylaxis criterion); RCP 203 Lssn 2a, slide 29 (exclusion note)

3Croup vs. Epiglottitis

Both are important causes of upper-airway obstruction in children, and both are dangerous because of pediatric airway anatomy: in children the narrowest point is the cricoid ring, so 1 mm of circumferential edema can cut the airway diameter by more than 50% — the same 1 mm in an adult is merely "noticeable." That is exactly why croup, epiglottitis, post-extubation edema, and airway trauma make pediatric patients deteriorate rapidly.

The two board-level radiographic pearls are croup → steeple sign → AP view (subglottic narrowing/edema below the vocal cords) and epiglottitis → thumb sign → lateral neck view (the swollen epiglottis). Racemic epinephrine (2.25% solution, 0.25–0.5 mL = 5.63–11.25 mg, QID) is the deck's listed treatment for reducing airway swelling in both croup and epiglottitis, as well as post-extubation stridor. The single most important safety rule the deck states is that airway management takes priority over obtaining an x-ray if the child is unstable or has significant airway compromise.

FeatureCroup (LTB — laryngotracheobronchitis)Epiglottitis
OrganismViral — human parainfluenza viruses (HPIV) listed as a cause of croup (infection of larynx, trachea, bronchi)Bacterial — Haemophilus influenzae, which causes invasive disease including epiglottitis ("swelling in the throat")
Incubation / onset speedHPIV symptoms appear 2 to 7 days after infectionNot stated in the decks (classically abrupt)
AgeNot stated in the decks; HPIV affects infants and young childrenDeck notes epiglottitis has become much less common in children following Hib vaccination
Hallmark signHarsh, barking cough; stridor with upper-airway obstructionNot stated in the decks — drooling and the tripod position do not appear in any deck
Location of swellingSubglottic — narrowing of the airway below the vocal cords from edema/inflammationSupraglottic — swollen epiglottis
X-ray sign / viewSteeple sign on the AP viewThumb sign on the lateral neck view
Treatment namedRacemic epinephrine (strong α-adrenergic) to reduce airway swelling — 2.25%, 0.25–0.5 mL SVN, QIDRacemic epinephrine listed for the same purpose; airway management takes priority over the x-ray if unstable
Do-not-examine-the-throat ruleNot covered in the available slide decks. The decks do give the closest available principle — with suspected epiglottitis, secure the airway before obtaining imaging if the child is unstable — but the classic "never depress the tongue or inspect the pharynx in suspected epiglottitis, because it can trigger complete laryngospasm/obstruction; examine only in the OR with intubation capability" rule is not written in any deck. Look it up in a pediatric emergency reference.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 2a, slides 60–61; Chptr 6 – Adrenergics, slides 18, 21–22; RCP 105 Wk 1 Lctr 2 – Airway Management, slide 22

4Lab Values and Presentation: Bacterial PNA vs. Emphysema vs. PE vs. Pulmonary Fibrosis

Group these by what the lung is doing. Bacterial pneumonia and pulmonary fibrosis both produce a restrictive picture with dull percussion, bronchial breath sounds, crackles, and whispered pectoriloquy — but pneumonia is acute, febrile, productive, and shows consolidation with air bronchograms, while fibrosis is chronic, nonproductive, and shows a bilateral reticulonodular pattern with honeycombing and ↑ tactile/vocal fremitus. Emphysema is the only obstructive one: decreased flows (FEV₁, FEV₁/FVC, FEF₂₅₋₇₅), hyperresonant percussion, a quiet chest, and compensated respiratory acidosis.

PE is the odd one out — the CXR is often normal and its job is to rule out pneumonia/pneumothorax; the diagnosis rests on D-dimer and CTPA. Three of the four (PNA, PE, fibrosis) share the same ABG trajectory: acute alveolar hyperventilation with hypoxemia early → acute ventilatory failure with hypoxemia when severe.

ConditionPresentationCXRABG patternLabsKey distinguishing feature
Bacterial PneumoniaDiaphoretic, cyanotic, tachypnea; crackles, bronchial breath sounds, whispered pectoriloquy; dull percussion; cough starts dry → productive yellow/green, rust-colored sputum or hemoptysis; fever > 101°F (viral < 101°F), ↑ HR, ↑ RR, ↑ BP↑ density from consolidation/atelectasis; air bronchograms (classic); possible abscess with air-fluid level or pleural effusion/empyemaEarly: respiratory alkalosis with hypoxemia (hyperventilation). Severe: respiratory acidosis with hypoxemia (acute ventilatory failure)CBC: ↑ WBC (bacterial) vs. ↓/normal WBC (viral); sputum culture & sensitivity to identify organismAir bronchograms + ↑ WBC + fever > 101°F; hypoxemia is from capillary shunting and is refractory to O₂ therapy
Emphysema (Type A COPD, "Pink Puffer")Thin, cachectic, barrel chest, pursed-lip breathing, heavy accessory muscle use, tachypnea; minimal cough, little/no sputum; pink skin tone; hyperresonant percussion; decreased breath sounds — quiet chestHyperlucency, hyperinflation, ↑ A-P diameter, flattened/depressed diaphragms, blebs/bullaeCompensated (partial or full) respiratory acidosis with hypoxemia; during exacerbation, acute-on-chronic respiratory acidosisHct/Hgb normal in mild–moderate, may ↑ in late stages; ↓ Cl⁻ and ↑ Na⁺ only in late stages; sputum usually normal (minimal); α₁-antitrypsin < 15–20% of normal is highly suggestive of AATD emphysema↓ DLCO (alveolar-capillary membrane destroyed) + obstructive PFT: ↓ FEV₁, ↓ FEV₁/FVC, ↓ FEF₂₅₋₇₅; pulmonary HTN/cor pulmonale uncommon (vs. common in chronic bronchitis)
Pulmonary Embolism (PE)Sudden dyspnea, pleuritic chest pain, cough ± hemoptysis, wheezing; tachycardia, arrhythmias, right heart strain, ↑ S₂, weak pulse, hypotension; anxiety, diaphoresis, syncope, cyanosis; leg swelling (DVT clue)Often normal — its role is to rule out pneumonia/pneumothorax; may show infiltrates or an elevated hemidiaphragm (nonspecific)Mild–moderate: acute alveolar hyperventilation with hypoxemia (↓ PaCO₂, ↑ pH). Severe: acute ventilatory failure with hypoxemia (↑ PaCO₂, ↓ pH). Severe hypoxia → lactic acidosis drops pH lower than the PaCO₂ predictsD-dimer: normal rules out PE; elevated > 500 ng/mL only suggests clot and is not specific. CBC + clotting profile (not diagnostic); Factor V Leiden genetic screen in recurrent/high-riskDead-space ventilation is the hallmark physiology; CT pulmonary angiography (CTPA) = gold standard (V/Q scan if CT contraindicated); venous ultrasound for DVT. Pulmonary Fibrosis / ILDTachypnea, ↑ HR, ↑ BP; cyanosis, digital clubbing; cor pulmonale signs — distended neck veins, pitting edema, enlarged tender liver; nonproductive cough; ↑ tactile & vocal fremitus, dull percussion, bronchial breath sounds, fine crackles, pleural friction rub, whispered pectoriloquyBilateral reticulonodular pattern, irregularly shaped opacities, granulomas, cavity formation, honeycombing, pleural thickening, pleural effusionMild–moderate: acute alveolar hyperventilation with hypoxemia (respiratory alkalosis). Severe: chronic ventilatory failure with hypoxemia — ↑ PaCO₂ with significantly ↑ HCO₃⁻ and a normal pH (fully compensated). SourceInfectious Pulmonary Disease deck, slides 26–27; COPD deck, slides 12–15; Pulmonary Edema and PVD deck, slides 43–45, 48, 55; ILD deck, slides 39–42, 44–46, 49–50

5How Hypoxia Appears in an Infant

Infants show hypoxia through visible work of breathing before they show it through numbers, and the classic cluster is tachypnea, retractions, nasal flaring, wheezing/crackles, hypoxemia, and — uniquely in infants — apnea. Retractions occur when the chest wall moves inward during inspiration and are more noticeable in children because of increased chest wall compliance. Nasal flaring reflects increased inspiratory effort and is described as a classic sign of respiratory distress, particularly in pediatric patients.

Cyanosis is a major indicator of hypoxemia — check lips, mucosa, tongue (central) and nail beds/fingertips (peripheral) — but remember it only becomes visible when the blood holds at least 5 g/dL of reduced hemoglobin, so a severely anemic infant can be badly hypoxemic without looking cyanotic. In a high-risk infant, the combination of retractions, tachypnea, cyanosis, hypotension, and asymmetrical chest motion should also make you think pneumothorax and consider transillumination at the bedside.

SignWhat it means in an infant
TachypneaEarliest compensation — hypoxemia stimulates peripheral chemoreceptors; stiff/low-compliance lungs force rapid, shallow breathing
RetractionsChest wall pulled inward on inspiration; signals significant airway obstruction; more pronounced in children due to increased chest wall compliance
Nasal flaringIncreased inspiratory effort — a classic pediatric distress sign
"See-saw" breathingThoracic-abdominal dyssynchrony/paradox — chest moves in on inspiration while abdomen moves out; a sign of abnormally high WOB and of damage to the structures of breathing
CyanosisVisible only at ≥ 5 g/dL reduced Hgb; central (lips, tongue) = dangerous hypoxemia, peripheral (hands, feet) often poor perfusion
ApneaListed specifically as a possible progression in infants (e.g., RSV/bronchiolitis) — adults do not typically do this
Accessory muscle use / gruntingAccessory muscle use = increased WOB. Expiratory grunting is scored on the Silverman-Anderson Index (Row 5): 0 = none · 1 = audible by stethoscope only · 2 = audible to the naked ear. Mechanically it is exhalation against a partially closed glottis, generating auto-PEEP to hold alveoli open.
Why infants decompensate differently than adults

it is anatomy. In adults the narrowest point of the upper airway is the glottis (vocal cords) and the trachea below is cylindrical — roughly uniform. In children the narrowest point is the cricoid ring, and the airway is funnel-shaped, getting narrower as you go down. Because the child starts with a much smaller airway, a little swelling causes a huge loss of airflow: 1 mm of circumferential edema in an adult is noticeable but often tolerated, while 1 mm in an infant reduces airway diameter by more than 50% and causes significant distress.

That is why pediatric patients deteriorate rapidly with croup, epiglottitis, post-extubation edema, or airway trauma — and why an infant can look compensated one minute and be in failure the next.

SourceRCP 105 Wk 1 Lctr 2 – Airway Management, slide 22; RCP 103 Clinical Data deck, slides 21, 26; RCP 203 Lssn 2a, slides 10–11, 31; Chptr 1 – Intro to Respiratory Care Pharmacology, slide 5

Special Precaution Words — They're Trying to Trick You

Adequate · Aggressive pain control does NOT mean too much meds · At least · Blunted · Changes to treatment plan and "actual orders" are NOT the same · Eliminate does NOT mean Filter (lungs vs kidneys) · Except · First · Laryngotracheal (injury) · Mechanical Ventilation = vent OR BiPAP, not vents only · Most commonly used · Most likely · Next · Nonpulmonary · Not · Observation · Obliterated · Precedence (importance) · Recommend at this time (first step) · Routinely · Severe · Significance · Warrants
